- **AgentTrace** - Validation, analysis, baseline/candidate comparison, escaped HTML timelines, JUnit XML reports, Markdown job summaries and SARIF output for AI agent execution traces. The timing inspector keeps four quantities apart that one total would blur: step-duration sum, recorded span, busy interval union and peak recorded spans — nested steps can add up to more than the run covers, and a parent-path duration sum is not elapsed time or a causal critical path. Missing timestamps stay unknown; recorded gaps do not prove idle time, overlap does not prove wasted work, and peak spans do not count workers. The inspector cannot establish complete instrumentation or synchronized clocks. A second inspector tests a hypothetical fixed-interval retention policy instead of the recorded run: given supplied intervals, drop penalties and an overlap cap, it keeps the subset with the least dropped penalty — at cap 1, two touching spans worth 6 each beat one enclosing span worth 9. Integer min-cost flow supplies the assignment with a certificate of matching primal and dual bounds, and the inspector shows every edge, residual reduced cost and capacity-frontier choice. Scope is at most 12 intervals, cap 0..4, with bounded integer endpoints and penalties; 250 seeded inputs were checked at all five capacities against full-subset/unit-cell references, 120 larger controls check feasibility and certificates, and independent browser enumeration agrees with native reports on 24 controls. It deletes no trace records and infers no real workers. 64 Python tests retain all 57 prior tests, including the original 33, with seven Node/actual-Chrome tests. [Timing inspector](https://midya8.github.io/agenttrace/) · [Policy inspector](https://midya8.github.io/agenttrace/capacity.html)
  - Case study: [agenttrace.md](agenttrace.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/agenttrace](https://github.com/midya8/agenttrace)
  - [Live graph/timing inspector](https://midya8.github.io/agenttrace/): Full-record graph/timing inspector: every endpoint, interval segment, parent path/relation, normalized record and p95 term. Independent Python/JavaScript recomputation and current JSON export. 57 Python tests retain all original 33 tests; 24 synthetic controls, 11,110 interval checks and an isolated installed-wheel reproduction. Parent links are not causal dependencies; recorded coverage/overlap is not CPU, idle, waste or utilization. No model/API call.
  - [Live fixed-interval policy inspector](https://midya8.github.io/agenttrace/capacity.html): the separate 0.2 retention policy — with one lane, two touching spans worth 6 each beat one enclosing span worth 9; the inspector shows the retained subset, hypothetical lanes, all five capacity choices and the complete min-cost-flow certificate. At most 12 intervals, cap 0..4, integer endpoints and penalties.

- **PromptGuard** - Local prompt linting with full-stage baseline/gate evidence, independent browser recomputation and complete JSON export; redaction and incomplete-scan fixes. 100 Python tests, including all 87 earlier tests, 24 complete quota reports and 4,374 occurrence-rank controls. No security-effectiveness claim.

- **CTCLab** - Offline CTC target marginalization with log-space forward/backward, full state/symbol posteriors and logit gradients, plus every exhaustive path and collapsed transcript. A frame-level best path is not the same as a highest-mass transcript: the editable independent JavaScript inspector shows why a greedy best path can lose to summed transcript mass — two uniform blank/A frames assign 0.25 to empty output and 0.75 to A. Dependency-free Python computes blank-expanded-state log-space forward/backward; an independent exhaustive oracle collapses all C^T paths and aggregates transcript masses, and independent scalar JavaScript recomputes the editable specification with complete state, gradient, path and transcript records, posterior lattice geometry and JSON export. 25 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 include 90 seeded 90-digit Decimal controls, 40 independently enumerated state-sequence controls and 300 central finite-difference gradient coordinates; twenty retained reports match 5,764 numeric and 650 other leaves in independent JavaScript, and actual Chrome checks 176 state rows, 142 gradient rows, 449 path rows and 143 transcript rows, every complete record, 176 posterior circles and 205 transition lines, invalid/stale input, export and 320/390/1280 containment, with isolated wheel reproduction outside the checkout. The controls show adjacent repeated labels need a separating blank, empty input supports only an empty target, changing blank index must preserve the corresponding computation, ordinary probabilities can underflow while log likelihood remains finite, and impossible targets retain undefined loss and gradients rather than silently zeroing them. Established CTC on tiny synthetic logits, not a trained recognizer, speech/OCR benchmark or novel algorithm; at most six frames, four classes and 1,024 paths, so this deliberately exponential audit is not a production decoder or speed benchmark. Target-conditioned latent alignments are not observed timestamps or causal explanations, and ranking uses disclosed 1e-8-nat score buckets followed by lexicographic indices, with raw masses and the raw greedy gap still available.

- **DPPLab** - Exact-rational finite DPP subset laws with every determinant, inclusion-kernel witness, Schur include/exclude conditioning and fixed-total-size contrast, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with an independent BigInt JavaScript recomputation — it keeps the repulsion visible instead of letting a sampled subset hide which items repel each other: native Fraction elimination and Gauss-Jordan inversion compute every subset weight, normalizer, inclusion kernel and Schur residual, while the independent JavaScript uses permutation determinants and adjugate inverses, and the two agree on 24 complete reports / 4,050 exact leaves. The control makes the point numerically: L = [[4,4,0],[4,4,0],[0,0,1]] gives normalizer 18, items 0 and 1 each have inclusion probability 4/9 but their joint inclusion is zero, versus 16/81 under independent equal marginals, while item 2 is independent with probability 1/2; mean size is 25/18 and variance 113/324 — supplied mathematical repulsion, not semantic diversity measured on a corpus. Duplicate-item controls separate zero co-occurrence from equal-marginal independence, and include/exclude conditions are kept separate from optional fixed total size k, with a highlighted warning against treating residual kernels as fixed-k kernels: identity L with k=1 gives zero probability to the pair even though its event-only inclusion determinant is 1/4, and impossible events return null laws rather than a silently repaired answer. 24 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 include 120 determinant references, 50 inverse identities, 140 full Gram-kernel oracles, 100 conditioning oracles, 40 fixed-k scale controls and 20 sign-conjugation controls, and actual Chrome verifies 252 subset rows and full records, 786 matrix cells, 84 pair rows, 504 bar fraction/geometry witnesses, all selected laws, modes and matrix records, invalid/stale states and JSON export, with isolated installed-wheel reproduction outside the checkout and 320/390/1280 viewport containment. One to five real-symmetric PSD likelihood-kernel items, at most 32 subsets, every principal minor checked exactly and singular valid kernels kept; input rational strings carry length and reduced numerator/denominator bounds, and the native strict JSON parser rejects duplicate keys while browser JSON.parse keeps the last — a parser difference stated explicitly rather than hidden behind numerical agreement. Established bounded L-ensembles, not learned relevance, a scalable sampler or a downstream quality result.

- **SubmodLab** - Complete finite submodular selection with every subset value, optimum tie and greedy gain enumerated exactly, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with an independent BigInt JavaScript recomputation — it keeps the guarantee separate from the optimum instead of letting a worst-case factor stand in for the answer: on the default six-row coverage control with k=2, updated greedy reaches 5 while the exact optimum is 6, an actual ratio of 5/6 against a finite-k worst-case factor of 3/4, and the proof-derived prefix certificate uses the k largest remaining gains because substituting the remaining budget would certify 5 below the true optimum. A duplicate-representative matrix makes singleton top-k score 5 where updated greedy scores 9, the exact optimum; a monotone synergy table gives greedy 3 against an optimum of 10 and fails submodularity, while a submodular but nonmonotone table ends exactly-k greedy at 0 against an at-most-k optimum of 2 — both withhold theorem and bound fields. The deterministic rounding inspector exposes the exact multilinear extension, every excluded-item gradient and both endpoints of every deterministic cardinality swap: a two-item half-probability control has expected count 1 yet exceeds budget 1 with probability 1/4. 33 Python tests on 3.10–3.13 include 120 facility controls, 100 arbitrary-table comparisons, 40 positive-scale controls, 30 facility/table equivalence controls and 60 prefix-bound controls; 24 complete reports match 16,426 exact native/BigInt leaves and 21 rounding reports match 10,682, with actual Chrome verifying 360 subset rows/bars and 2,056 full records, and isolated installed-wheel reproduction verifies all reports, the console CLI and four module origins outside the checkout. At most six items, 64 subsets and eight facility rows; exact rational strings, no learned embeddings, cost/knapsack or matroid constraints, lazy or stochastic greedy, runtime benchmark, downstream quality metric or new theorem.
  - Case study: [submodlab.md](submodlab.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/submodlab](https://github.com/midya8/submodlab)

  - [Method and limits](https://github.com/midya8/submodlab/blob/main/METHOD.md): Nemhauser, Wolsey and Fisher; Krause and Golovin's survey, Submodlib objective documentation and the Isabelle Archive of Formal Proofs; implementation not formally verified.

- **CutLab** - Exact binary submodular energy graph cuts with every flow/cut witness, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with an independent BigInt JavaScript recomputation — it keeps the global optimum certified instead of letting a local search stop at the first dip: signed rational unary and asymmetric submodular pair tables reduce to a directed s-t network, every pair entry reconstructed, every terminal shift retained, every assignment satisfying original energy = offset + cut capacity, and non-submodular pairs refused rather than clipped, with exact original-energy enumeration kept visible as an independent tiny reference. The inspector shows every deterministic shortest augmenting path, residual bottleneck and complete edge-flow state — a six-augmentation fixture actually needs a reverse variable arc to cancel earlier flow — and every cut exposes capacity minus flow = outgoing residual slack, so matching feasible flow and cut prove optimality. The residual SCC graph recovers the whole forward-closed family of minimum cuts and forced/free labels: free does not mean independently selectable, a tied agreement has two free variables but only two optimal masks, and the zero six-variable graph has all 64 assignments optimal. The controls make the failure modes concrete: strict best single-flip search starts at mask 0 with energy 2, both one-flip neighbors cost 3, so it stops while mask 3 has energy 0, certified by flow 1 plus offset -1; unary-only chooses energy 5 in a separate coupling control while two exact tied minima cost 3, a gap of 2 labeled as an incomplete baseline, not a solver benchmark. 30 Python tests on 3.10-3.13, including all 21 original fixed-cost tests, and six Node 22+/actual-Chrome tests: the fixed-cost checks include 250 independent dense-energy cases, 160 every-cut slack audits, full augmentation replay, conservation, capacity, independent reachability/SCC, optimum-family and lattice checks, 100 scaling/offset controls and 80 relabelings; 24 complete native and independent BigInt reports agree on 24,624 exact scalar leaves, and actual Chrome checks the rendered cut, flow, reduction, residual and local-search records, SVG labels and colors, with summaries, all optimum-family fields, invalid/stale clearing and export checked and 320/390/1280 containment. An installed wheel reproduces all 24 fixed-cost and 18 path reports, comparing console/module CLI output for both workflows, with five module origins checked outside the checkout. At most six binary variables and finite rational costs; established binary submodular inference and maxflow/mincut, not a new algorithm, trained model, real-image segmentation, predictive validation, speed/memory benchmark, multilabel or higher-order method, or machine-checked formal proof; exhaustive family verification is exponential.

- **FactorLab** - Exact rational finite factor elimination with every joined product, two-branch reduction, fill edge and small-order census, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with an independent BigInt JavaScript recomputation — it keeps the question separate from the answer instead of letting a mixed sum/max schedule report a bound as a mass: on the retained factor on scope [0,1] with little-endian values [6,5,0,5], summing the hidden variable first gives x0 masses [6,10] and the optimum x0=1 at 10/16=5/8, while maximizing first computes max(6,5)+max(0,5)=11, an upper bound no assignment reaches; the joint mode x0=0,x1=0 with weight 6 projects onto the wrong marginal decision. A separate five-variable star gives an identical all-sum partition for hub-first and leaves-first schedules, but their dense joined-table counts are 62 and 18 with induced widths 4 and 1 — an exact allocation comparison, not a wall-clock or memory claim. Nonnegative rational binary factors use explicit little-endian scope indexing, with evidence restriction, scalar factors and injected units for variables absent from all factors; every joined product carries source-factor indices and values, every two-branch reduction keeps all max ties and complete generated factors, and the primal graph, per-step fill edges and induced width are computed without numerical sparsity shortcuts. The structural census covers up to 120 orders with replay in the browser, and independent exhaustive joint weights, partition, marginal-MAP masses and all optimal ties are checked against it; the editable independent BigInt inspector supports full current JSON export with stale/error clearing. 24 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 include 160 dense scalar references, 100 full current-factor-product references, all orders on 20 random four-variable graphs for all-sum/all-max/mixed queries, 40 rescaling controls and an independent adjacency-set census; 24 complete native/BigInt reports match 23,114 exact leaves, and actual Chrome checks 1,939 complete selected records — 325 joint, 406 joined products, 203 reductions, 75 steps, 69 sliced factors and 861 census records — plus 21 fill edges, 75 bars, all tables/summaries, SVG geometry/colors, order replay, export, invalid/stale clearing and 320/390/1280 containment; the Node tests run on Node 22+ against an installed google-chrome, or CHROME_BIN. Installed-wheel reproduction verifies all reports, both CLI routes and four module origins outside the checkout. At most five binary variables, ten factors and 32 full assignments; exhaustive reference/census work is exponential, negative factors are rejected, sum-before-max is sufficient not necessary, invalid schedules return bounds not assignments or posterior probabilities, zero evidence mass leaves probabilities null, and an evidence-restricted partition is not P(e) without the full unconditioned normalizer. Established variable elimination and mixed-query theory, not a new algorithm, learned predictor, belief propagation, variational inference or formal verification.

- **UnifyLab** - Finite first-order unification with complete equation rewrites, composed substitutions and independently checked certificates, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with independent JavaScript engines — it keeps the binding separate from the solution instead of letting a substitution that type-checks stand in for a unifier: a normalized simultaneous substitution is applied to every original equation, every equation rewrite, composed earlier binding, exact constructor clash and zero-based occurs-check cycle path is retained, alias reversal is not automatically a cycle, and a resource limit never means non-unifiability. Independent Python and JavaScript engines reproduce 167 complete reports and 270 rewrites; a separate native certificate checker reconstructs local rules without calling the solver, and a third lazy-environment oracle tests controls, 600 seeded systems and all 64 term-pool pairs. Seventeen controls retain 38 rewrites, including actual state/result expansion limits. Twelve native tests on Python 3.10-3.13, three Node/Chrome tests and seventeen isolated installed reports through two CLIs in solve/verify modes, with every retained browser step, equation, binding, witness, export and invalid edit checked at 320/390/1280. The subset stage tests every indexed subset, keeps duplicate equation identities, and distinguishes an inclusion-minimal conflict from a smallest conflict: confirmed deletion repairs list indices to REMOVE with failing single restorations, the default control has independent duplicate/cycle conflicts and repairs of different sizes, and a resource limit stays unresolved with no inferred status. Eight synthetic controls cover 371 subsets, including all 256 masks at the eight-equation bound; independent Python/JavaScript engines match 108 full audits and 1,032 subset summaries, bringing the suite to nineteen native tests on Python 3.10-3.13 and six Node/Chrome tests, with a third lazy-binding oracle checking all proper subsets and independent minimal hitting-set enumeration, and installed wheels reproducing seventeen base reports and eight subset audits through both entry points. The generalization stage is finite ground-term anti-unification: keep common constructors and give the same ordered disagreement pair the same variable, with fresh/reuse records, two substitution maps and exact reconstructions — and the independent native checker rejects coherent overgeneralization even when both inputs reconstruct. Python and independently written JavaScript agree on 209 reports; nine synthetic controls expose 43 node records, bringing the suite to 27 native tests and nine Node/Chrome feature tests -- fifteen Node tests in all with the six navigation-readiness checks -- with isolated wheel checks reproducing nine generalizations through both CLI entry points in all five modes, six installed module origins, at 320/390/1280. Established finite-tree unification, anti-unification and constraint-diagnosis concepts, not new theory or a new scalable MUS/MCS algorithm; at most six variables, eight equations, 96 input nodes, 4,096-node state/result budgets and 256 rewrites, with at most 256 independent bounded solves in the subset stage and ground finite trees only in generalization — no rational trees, higher-order/AC unification, resolution prover, linear-time or benchmark-win claim, and most-generality follows the established rewrite/solved-form theory.

- **STRIPSLab** - Bounded classical AI planning with the delete effects kept visible, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with independent JavaScript engines — it keeps the real plan separate from the relaxed plan instead of letting a delete-relaxed heuristic stand in for an exact shortest plan: zero to six Boolean facts, positive initial/goal/precondition sets and at most eight positional unit-cost actions, where the real successor deletes then adds and the relaxed successor only adds, and facts absent from a state are false, not unknown. The export retains every one of the 2^m states including unreachable ones, with every blocked/applicable action, missing preconditions, actual added/removed facts, real/relaxed successors, exact remaining goal distances, all shortest suffix choices, decimal-string sequence counts and one representative replay per state; initial-state reachability is reported separately and initial optimal-plan edges retain every shortest tie, not one favorable path. The failures stay visible: a shared effect achieves two goals in one real action while h-add counts it twice; a consumable resource permits either goal but not both, so the relaxed graph finds a plan where the real graph has none; a restoration action makes the real optimum three steps against two relaxed steps; independent goals expose h-max's lower estimate while independent action orders retain six shortest sequences; and empty goals, zero facts, duplicate action identities, no-ops, cyclic prerequisites, reachable traps and a six-fact chain test the boundaries. Synchronous h-max and h-add fact-cost propagation retains every round, the final unchanged round and all tied best fact achievers; exact h-plus comes from the full relaxed state graph, not either propagation estimate, and local achiever ties are not claimed to be complete relaxed plans. The search stage adds a complete A*/best-first inspector exposing every selected frontier, strict cost improvement, applicable action decision and returned plan: a controlled task returns cost 3 with h-add versus exact cost 2 under the disclosed tie and goal-pop policy, while zero, h-max and exact h-plus return cost 2, with admissibility/consistency diagnostics, unsolvable exhaustion, strict CLI certificates, an independently implemented browser engine and a separate synthetic reopening test. The availability stage disables every static action subset and keeps every exact counterfactual distance, shortest-plan count, original action ID, representative and optimal edge, separating an action used by one representative from one required by every optimal plan or every valid plan, and retaining all inclusion-minimal sufficient sets and all solvability/optimality cuts with independently checked minimal-hitting-set duals: the original duplicate-shortcut control leaves a longer detour when both shortcuts are removed, so optimality fails before solvability does. Eighteen controls retain 466 scenarios, 21,731 state rows and 408 optimal edges; Python BFS and independent JavaScript distance sweeps agree on 118 complete reports covering 2,515 scenarios, and independent native set-transition and all-proper-subset oracles cover another 80 seeded tasks. The original base release had eight Python tests and three Node/Chrome tests; the current suite has seventeen Python tests and nine Node/Chrome tests across all three modes, with an isolated installed wheel reproducing 14 base, 15 search and 18 availability reports, checking five installed module origins and running both CLI entry points in all three modes, and a fresh public checkout passing native, browser and installed-package checks; the 47 isolated installed reports preserve all earlier modes, and Chrome checks every retained UI record and every retained scenario at 320/390/1280. Established STRIPS and heuristic-planning theory with original bounded implementations and synthetic tasks, seven inspected sources including Bonet/Geffner and Haslum/Geffner attributed in METHOD.md with source coverage and implementation differences; no PDDL parsing or grounding, negative preconditions, conditional effects, non-unit costs, concurrency, probabilistic outcomes, partial observability, safety guarantee, scalable-planner or benchmark-win claim, and full-state precomputation is explicit — a bounded audit, not a planner speedup or new algorithm claim.

- **ConceptLab** - Bounded formal concept analysis with every concept, cover, meet/join, attribute closure and object derivation enumerated by independent Python and JavaScript engines — it keeps the implication separate from the evidence instead of letting a rule that reads true stand in for a checked one: every implication query retains its supporting and counterexample rows, and an empty premise extent means vacuous validity and null confidence, not evidence. Canonical implications, pseudo-intent decisions, NextClosure pivots, forward rule traces and rule-removal countermodels are all inspectable. Fourteen original controls include duplicate rows, zero support, empty universes, strict joins, nonminimal pseudo-intents and multi-rule closure; the retained suite covers 194 attribute subsets, 390 object subsets, 69 concepts, 30 canonical rules, 635 ordered lattice operations and 5,762 implication queries, and independent set-based checks cover all 689 contexts with zero to three objects/attributes plus 100 seeded larger contexts per engine. The incomplete-context stage enumerates every compatible binary completion of a three-valued table — unknown is not false — and inspects certain, contingent and impossible implications, all object counterexamples, exact defined row-confidence bounds and endpoint ties, with zero-support completions kept separate; goals are intersected in one shared completion and every inclusion-minimal impossible goal set is retained with deletion witnesses. Original controls expose two traps: both extreme fill-ins can pass while an intermediate world refutes an implication, and separately possible goals can be jointly impossible; overlapping-premise tautologies, empty universes, duplicate objects and all 64 worlds of six unknowns remain explicit, and twelve controls cover 86 completions, 438 implication queries, 4,634 query/completion witness rows and 63 goal subsets. Independent Python and JavaScript engines, 17 native tests on four Python versions, six Node/Chrome tests and 26 installed-wheel reports preserve the complete-context workbench, with every retained UI record, export and invalid-input clearing checked at 320/390/1280. The attribute-exploration stage asks the strongest observed implication for a premise against an explicitly supplied complete expert table — an implication can fit every observed row and still fail in the supplied domain — retaining every expert-table counterexample, revealing the lowest-ID refutation and retrying the same premise; accepted implications change the next lectic pivot, and every seed, earlier-coordinate disagreement and derivation round remains inspectable. A separate set-based Python checker reconstructs theory closure by intersecting all satisfying models, checks each pivot against exhaustive lectic successors and checks the canonical basis through an independent recursive pseudo-intent characterization, without calling either generator; the final audit compares accepted-knowledge, full-expert and final-observed closures for every attribute subset, with support IDs and all missing-attribute counterexamples. Fresh feature/main reproduction passes 24 native tests on Python 3.10/3.11/3.12/3.13 and all eleven Node/Chrome tests, with all 6,654 small expert-family/initial-observation combinations checked and Python and JavaScript agreeing across 6,966 reports and 64,658 events; the installed 0.3.0 wheel runs both entry points outside the checkout for fourteen complete-context, twelve partial-context and twelve exploration controls, with forged-export rejection. Optional concepts 0.9.2 agrees on 175,003 support/closure checks (88 empty-axis requests excluded from that package comparison and tested internally), and actual deployed Chrome checks 36 exploration reports at 1280/390/320, 273 events, 204 pivots, 294 premise obligations, 702 displayed derivation rounds and twelve downloaded exports accepted by the independent Python CLI, with both earlier inspectors passing their full behavior checks; all fifty native/fresh feature/main files and fourteen deployed assets plus root match exactly. Exact scope for this stage is five attributes and eight supplied objects, with twelve original synthetic controls, and the expert is an explicitly supplied complete table, not a human or language model — no minimum-question claim, live human expert, background theory, partial/noisy expert, statistical generalization or production scalability claim. Finite exact teaching/audit tool: at most six attributes and eight objects in the complete-context workbench, and four attributes, four objects and six unknown cells in the completion stage; completion counts are not probabilities and confidence bounds describe supplied rows, not unseen data. Existing FCA theory and software are attributed; no new algorithm, learned prediction, fuzzy concept analysis, noisy-data inference, causality, imputation, graded possibility model, incomplete-context basis or scalable mining claim.

- **HallLab** - Exact all-different propagation for tiny constraint-based AI assignment, where distinctness is a global promise, not a pairwise one: every retained variable/value is backed by a complete injective assignment, and every pruned edge is explained by a forced-residual Hall obstruction. Two variables reserving {v0, v1} leave seven edges under pairwise disequalities but five under global support, forcing x2 to v2; a second control has three variables sharing two values, where every binary constraint has support but no full assignment exists. The comparison is against full synchronous pairwise arc consistency, not a weakened one-pass baseline. The original 12 native tests, retained in the current 36-test suite, cover 8,777 exhaustive domain graphs and 120 seeded larger inputs, with 682 full Python/JavaScript report comparisons and actual Chrome checking every control edge, subset and propagation round at 320/390/1280; the original synthetic controls include hidden Hall bottlenecks, noninterval domains and a full 48-edge scope; CI spans Python 3.10-3.13 and Node 22, and an installed wheel replays the controls outside the checkout. The overlapping-scope stage repeats full local propagation to a synchronous fixed point and compares it with exact common-assignment support: a two-color triangle keeps every local value but has no joint assignment, and a satisfiable triangle keeps local values that never occur in a common solution — local support is not a shared solution. Eight new native tests and three new Node/Chrome tests cover 621 overlap reports and nine browser controls at 320/390/1280, over at most six variables, eight values and six scopes (262144 full tuples). The conflict stage enumerates all inclusion-minimal conflicting scope sets and all inclusion-minimal scope-removal repairs, with complete single-deletion witnesses: the default diamond has a size-one and four size-two minimal removals, so minimal is not minimum. Twenty-eight native tests and nine Node/actual-Chrome tests cover 621 cross-engine reports and 4413 active subsets; domains stay fixed, and an empty-domain background has no scope-removal repair. Classic matching and Hall theory throughout — at most six variables and eight values under one all-different relation, not a complete constraint solver, a scalable propagator or a new algorithm; a full match for one constraint does not ensure a shared solution for several. v0.4 adds an alternating-path inspector: one variable and two values produce three singleton alternating SCCs, yet both domain edges have support — the inspector follows a free value through an even alternating path, flips its edges and shows the resulting full assignment, with dummy-node SCCs exposing the same characterization without hiding where the spare value starts. Thirty-six Python tests and 12 Node/Chrome tests cover 5,058 tiny domain graphs, 120 seeded bound-wide cases, 821 complete cross-language reports and 136 primitive forgeries, with pinned Choco 4.10.18 AC_REGIN propagation agreeing over 821 cases; the deployed inspector replays all 12 controls at 1280/390/320, every edge certificate, reach layer, SCC and flip step. [Live alternating inspector](https://midya8.github.io/halllab/alternating.html) · [Exact contract and eight-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/ALTERNATING.md) · [Pull request](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/pull/4).

  - [Overlapping-scope inspector](https://midya8.github.io/halllab/overlaps.html): Full local propagation to a synchronous fixed point compared with exact common-assignment support across overlapping scopes. [Exact overlap contract and sources](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/OVERLAPS.md).

[Exact contract and seven-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/halllab/blob/main/CONFLICTS.md).

- **STNLab** - Tiny temporal-network inspector where every implied time difference carries its evidence, built from scratch in dependency-free Python with an independent JavaScript solver — it keeps the bound separate from the schedule that attains it instead of letting a tight path stand in for a reachable deadline: an edge [u,v,w] means t[v] - t[u] <= w, a tight path gives a bound, and the bound is only evidence once a complete schedule meets it, with every time point assigned, while an unbounded pair is settled by an anchored ray, not by the absence of a path; a negative cycle shows exactly why no schedule exists, even away from the anchor, and finite bounds are individually attainable, not necessarily jointly selectable. Eleven editable controls include duplicate edges, zero cycles, free components and a chain with bound 100, and removing one edge returns an alternate implication path or a complete separating schedule. Native Floyd-Warshall/Bellman-Ford generation, independent permutation/edge-product verification and recursive browser search agree; nine native tests cover exhaustive tiny graphs, seeded networks and strict CLI rejection, and real Chrome checks all proof panels at three widths. Six points and twelve constraints, real-valued times, no resource capacity, uncertain durations or execution guarantee; the anchor t0=0 only removes a common translation, negative times are allowed, and inconsistent networks produce no finite-bound or redundancy report. The insertion stage takes one proposed temporal constraint and returns an exact consistency threshold plus every changed implied bound, with native old-matrix one-edge closure checked against independent full recomputation: inspect the old return path, a complete schedule at the feasibility boundary, a candidate-first negative cycle, or a still-unbounded anchored ray, with all finite post-insertion bounds carrying attaining schedules and 41 integer weights as separate alternatives, not accumulated changes. Ten new synthetic controls cover exact-boundary feasibility, a missing return path, a pre-inconsistent base, duplicate/self-loop cases, a six-point threshold outside the input window and a 12-edge base plus candidate, with 17 Python tests, six Node/real-Chrome tests and installed-wheel checks covering both families. The projection stage hides time points without losing the bounds their paths imply: it restricts the closed network to retained original IDs and lifts jointly feasible retained assignments into full original schedules, exposing retained assignments that naive edge deletion wrongly permits; each finite projected bound has an original path and a full attaining schedule, unbounded pairs have anchored rays, and hidden negative cycles remain impossible, audited by independent Fourier-Motzkin elimination, path enumeration and pinned-cycle checks, with native Python and separate browser recomputation agreeing across 12 projection controls, installed CLI reproduction and real Chrome proof-panel tests at 320, 390 and 1280 pixels. The conflicts stage enumerates every inclusion-minimal temporal conflict and every inclusion-minimal correction set, replaying negative cycles, conflict-deletion schedules, repaired schedules and restoration contradictions, with an independent native verifier checking complete hitting-set duality and a separate JavaScript engine powering the inspector; minimal is explicitly distinct from minimum-cardinality. Validation covers all four families: 31 native tests on Python 3.10-3.13, 12 Node/Chrome tests, 316 cross-engine reports, exhaustive tiny networks and installed-wheel reproduction, with Chrome visiting every control proof at 320/390/1280; controls include duplicate edges, disconnected conflicts, self-loops, empty repairs and the 12-edge boundary. Classical temporal-network theory over real-valued time, not a new optimization algorithm, calendar executor, uncertainty model or scalable solver.

- **HornLab** - Bounded positive propositional Horn inference with synchronous derivation rounds and every indexed rule decision — it keeps the derivation separate from the model instead of letting a satisfying assignment stand in for a proof: the least model is compared with every classical and supported model, and a supported circular assignment need not have a derivation, so a circle is not a proof. Every non-entailed query keeps an atomic countermodel, and every inclusion-minimal initial-fact support is inspectable with its deletion failures and all seed subsets — not entailed does not mean classically false. Separate native set/truth-table checkers replay the full reports without calling the generators — inference checks model intersection and all proper seed subsets — and Python/JavaScript parity is a separate check, not a replacement for independent verification; the current checkout passes 26 Python and eight Node/Chrome tests, with native coverage of all 1,024 two-atom program/initial-fact combinations and seeded cases, over six atoms and eight rules with no negation or variables. The rule-deletion stage keeps initial facts fixed and inspects all indexed-rule subsets, synchronous closures, every minimal rule support and every minimal deletion set — one broken route need not break the goal, each support retains one-rule removal failures and each deletion retains restoration successes, with both minimal-hitting-set dualities and every support/deletion intersection explicit, including empty-support and already-absent boundary cases. Deployed Chrome checks all three inspectors at 320/390/1280, and the rule controls expose 305 subsets, 35 queries, 36 supports, 27 deletion sets and 36 intersections, with a separate native set/truth-table checker reconstructing the complete rule lattice without calling the generator. Installed-wheel checks reproduce ten inference, eleven rule-audit and twelve update controls outside the checkout through both entry points, including forged-report rejection, and optional clingo 5.8.3 agrees on 11,381 positive-program stable families across 4,458 requests and 6,923 transactions. The third inspector keeps rules fixed and applies fact deletions and insertions: conservative overdeletion, surviving one-step derivations, restored and new delta insertion, with every indexed rule obligation shown — deleting a fact's explicit membership does not necessarily remove its consequence, since another derivation may remain. Update checks cover 36 reports, 81 transaction selections, 546 phase/round records, 1,491 displayed obligations and twelve downloaded exports independently accepted by Python, with fact maintenance accepting at most eight transactions and native coverage of all 4,096 two-atom program/old-fact/new-fact transitions. Facts are fixed seeds and minimal supports are relative only to the given initial facts with rules held fixed — empty-body rules can derive atoms with empty seed support, duplicate indexed rules remain distinct achievers, and synchronous closure does not chain through a new atom until the next round; support is a separate equality test and may admit unsupported-by-derivation cycles. Established semantics, not a new algorithm, causal attribution, ontology translation, formal proof or production rule engine.

- **BisimLab** - Finite agent-protocol equivalence from scratch, where the same traces are not the same choices: two systems can agree on every finite trace and still differ in what they can do next. The default original control has equal finite trace languages but loses a choice after the same action; exact paired-subset BFS checks trace equality separately from branching equivalence, and the inspector shows every strong-bisimulation refinement, every failed left/right step match, executable Hennessy-Milner witness DAGs, truth sets and a quotient graph. A native partition-signature checker never calls the generator, and independent JavaScript matches the full reports. Nine native tests include all 512 three-state/one-action graphs checked against relation enumeration and 100 seeded graphs; three Node/actual-Chrome tests include 593 complete cross-engine reports and every control record at 320/390/1280, with installed-wheel checks covering both CLI entry points outside the checkout. The trace-product stage retains the entire reachable product of two states' possible successor sets, including every action edge after a mismatch and the both-empty sink, so every possibility-mismatch node, canonical BFS discovery word and layer, and a shortest distinguishing path are inspectable — equal finite traces still do not imply branching equivalence. Python bitmasks, independent JavaScript sets and a separate native checker based on explicit subset tables agree on 4,715 trace reports; seventeen native tests include exhaustive unary three-state graphs across every ordered pair, direct word enumeration, seeded multi-action cases and forged closure/provenance checks, and six Node/actual-Chrome tests cover every control record, export and invalid-input clearing at 320/390/1280px, with installed-wheel checks covering six original and seven trace controls, two CLI entry points and six import origins. One to six states, all labels and actions visible: no silent abstraction in the first two stages, probabilities, policy guarantee, source extraction, new algorithm or formal-proof claim. Established theory and seven inspected sources are attributed in the method. The silent-action stage hides selected steps and asks the equivalence question three ways — strong, branching and weak bisimilarity give three different answers on the same pair of systems; the default contrast reconstructs Bisping Example 6.3, credited rather than claimed as new. The inspector records every fixed-point round, removed pair, attack and canonical finite response skeleton, including the before-step requirement that weak matching can lose, with silent reachability and possible-divergence cycle witnesses kept separate. Independent BFS generation, DFS/set replay and candidate-relation test oracles agree on 1,154 reports with 8,461 rounds, 4,014 removed pairs and 8,166 response records; an optional unmodified mCRL2 ltscompare 202507.0 agrees on 350 systems and 5,652 comparisons. All 23 native tests pass on Python 3.10–3.13 and all 9 Node/Chrome tests pass, with an installed wheel reproducing 6 base, 7 trace and 10 silent controls outside the checkout through four entrypoints, checking eight module origins and rejecting 20 forged reports. Actual Chrome checks 30 silent reports at 320/390/1280, 294 rounds, 498 removed-pair selections, 960 attacks, 414 response records and 10 independently verified downloads, plus 18-loop graph geometry. No rooted or divergence-preserving semantics, fairness, liveness or deployed-agent guarantee is claimed; bounded coverage counts, not a proof or speed benchmark. The explicit-divergence stage (0.4) adds unrooted divergence-preserving branching equivalence — the silent stage’s no-divergence-preserving-semantics limit applied to that stage only. Two states can both loop forever yet disagree about where that loop is possible: the inspector enumerates every set partition, retains every state’s internal reachability, complete exit signatures, canonical nonempty-cycle lassos and every failed same-block obligation, then checks the coarsest valid partition explicitly; the opening six-state example reconstructs and credits Luttik2020 Example3/Figure1, where both states are globally divergent but only one can diverge while staying in the class where the visible action remains available. Independent checks include a simple-path/block-insertion verifier without generator imports, a direct relational attack/response oracle with matrix cycle closure, 220 individually rejected non-input leaf mutations and 1,157 complete Python/JavaScript atlases with 8,720 partitions, 37,743 state records and 19,346 failure records; actual unmodified pinned mCRL2 agrees on 353 frameworks / 3,672 unordered-pair-semantics comparisons. All 32 Python tests and 12 Node/Chrome tests pass, with a fresh isolated wheel reproducing 13 new controls through both new entrypoints, checking three new module origins and rejecting 26 forged reports. Actual Chrome checks 39 control-width reports at 1280/390/320, all 843 candidate selections, 4,467 state rows, 2,955 failure rows and 13 trusted completed JSON downloads verified by the native checker, with 47 complete unmasked visual states matching the reviewed implementation after deployment. Bounds: 1..6 states, 0..3 labels and 18 unique edges, at most 203 partitions; exact bounded educational model checking, not a scalable verification engine, learned model, benchmark, formal proof or agent safety/liveness guarantee. [Live divergence inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bisimlab/divergence.html) · [Exact contract and inspected foundations](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/blob/main/DIVERGENCE.md) · [Pull request 4](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/pull/4).

- **ShieldLab** - ShieldLab solves three small protocol-game objectives — stay safe forever, reach a safe goal for the first time, or stay safe while visiting a target infinitely often — as three separate inspectors with different stopping rules. Reach-avoid stops at the first goal while recurrence follows the target’s outgoing moves, so one acknowledgement followed by permanent safe waiting passes first-hit reach-avoid but fails recurrence. Exact tiny safety-game inspector for hand-written agent protocols, where a safe next step can still lose: the fast action reaches a currently safe pending state, but the environment can force a leak on its next turn, while review stays in a closed safe region -- flip pending to controller ownership with no other graph change and the same fast action becomes winning. Every synchronous fixed-point obligation, winning controller move, losing rank and branching invariant/counterstrategy graph is visible, so one-step safety is kept separate from winning the whole game: winning proof graphs fix a controller policy and retain every environment move, losing proof graphs fix a rank-decreasing environment counterpolicy and retain every controller move and stop at unsafe states, and ranks bound forced failure; canonical first choices are not promised optimal. Independent bitmask Python and explicit-set JavaScript, plus a separate dual-attractor and exhaustive positional-policy checker. Eight native tests include all 21,952 three-state graph/ownership/safety combinations and 150 seeded larger arenas; three Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 643 complete reports and all six controls at 320/390/1280, with installed-wheel checks replaying both CLI entry points outside the checkout. A five-step forced chain, safe environment cycle, indefinite waiting and an already-unsafe root make the boundaries explicit. Finite total perfect-information model only: no runtime shield, LLM guarantee, liveness, timing, probability, new algorithm or formal proof; dead ends are rejected and safe waiting need not mean progress. Established theory and seven inspected sources are attributed in the method. v0.2 adds a first-goal inspector that separates safe waiting from forced arrival: a finite reach-before-unsafe mode exposing every synchronous attractor obligation, the exact minimum worst-case first-goal bound and a complete ranked strategy or target-avoiding counterstrategy — a winning successor can still stall, while a slower fixed policy can still win. All 24 native tests pass on Python 3.10-3.13, preserving all 8 original tests; independent deterministic strategy-pair simulation checks 324 two-state and 32,928 three-state cases plus 120 seeded larger cases and the 729-policy boundary, and six Node/Chrome tests compare 457 complete native/JavaScript reports, 1,329 policies and 6,291 root outcomes. Actual Chrome drills every policy/root across 12 controls at 320/390/1280 and verifies 12 event-confirmed downloaded reports through the native CLI; fresh installed wheels reproduce 6 original and 12 new reports through two entrypoints, check 8 module origins and reject 20 forged CLI records. Terminal safe goals stop the play on first hit; empty targets lose and unsafe roots fail immediately; no fairness assumption forces the environment to cooperate, and safety-until-goal treats success as absorbing rather than the original nonterminal infinite-safety objective. [Live first-goal inspector](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/reachavoid.html) · [Contract and eight-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/REACHAVOID.md) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/pull/2). v0.3 adds a safe-recurrence inspector: an acknowledgement can arrive once without ever arriving again, so the separate inspector checks that a supplied finite game stays safe forever and visits a target infinitely often against every environment choice, with targets live rather than terminal — their outgoing moves still count. Every nested greatest/least fixed-point round and successor obligation is exposed, including unchanged rounds and outer restarts, with both players' complete positional-policy atlases, every root outcome, all winning policy IDs and canonical labeled unsafe or nonempty-cycle failure witnesses; complete branching strategy/counterstrategy graphs retain every opponent move and stop only at unsafe, never at a target, so one favorable path or target-containing SCC is not a recurrence guarantee. Reproduced from the exact public source: 39 Python tests, all 74,088 three-state graph/owner/safety-target configurations, 498 complete native/JavaScript/checker reports and 163 individually rejected primitive-leaf mutations; actual Chrome at 1280/390/320 checks 36 complete control reports, every nested obligation and both policy atlases, with all 12 trusted completed downloads independently accepted by the native checker. Fresh installed checks retain all old safety/first-goal controls and entrypoints. Bounds: six states, three moves per state and at most 729 positional assignments per player; established finite safety-and-Büchi theory with original supplied controls, not a new algorithm or a measured agent result, and no fairness, probabilities, partial observation, scalable solver, formal proof or real-agent safety/liveness guarantee. [Inspect safe recurrence](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/recurrence.html) · [Exact method and inspected sources](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/RECURRENCE.md).

- **AbsorbLab** - Inspect first-hit outcomes, strong lumping and exact one-row probability response in supplied finite Markov chains. A disappearing goal edge separates an interior limit of one from actual endpoint success of zero; success-conditioned traffic shows state visits and directed edge counts. The first-hit control separates global path selection from local failure-edge removal, while the lumping control exposes a split that only appears on the second refinement round. Current checks: 42 Python tests, 11 Node tests including actual Chrome for all three inspectors, isolated wheels and three optional SymPy references with distinct limits. Exact arithmetic and replayable certificates support inspection of small supplied chains, not new probability theory, learned behavior, causal intervention or real-agent guarantees.

  - [Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/): Finite Markov first-hit and success-conditioning inspector with every Doob-conditioned transition, conditional timing moment and closed-cycle witness visible.
  - [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/absorblab/blob/main/METHOD.md): Exact contract, input limits and eight inspected sources.
- **DFALab** - Tiny active-learning inspector where a finite sample can make two protocol states look identical, and a closed and consistent observation table can still propose the wrong language — what settles the question is the distinguishing experiment. An original implementation of Dana Angluin's classic L* learner asks globally cached membership queries, repairs observation-table closure and consistency, and processes all prefixes of each exact counterexample; every cell, teacher-side query path, repair witness and conjecture is replayable. Each equivalence query includes the complete reachable target/hypothesis product, all outputs and all edges, and its first mismatch is a shortest shortlex counterexample — no mismatch proves all-word language equivalence for the supplied finite teacher. The final DFA carries a shortest access word and a shortest distinguishing suffix for every state pair, proving reachable-state minimality, which does not prove minimum query count. Eight native tests run on Python 3.10-3.13, including all 17,496 three-state binary teachers, all 648 three-state unary teachers and all 128 two-state binary teachers, with every start and accepting set; four Node/Chrome tests compare 189 complete reports and replay 93 table selections, 45 conjectures and 411 new-query rows at 320/390/1280, and installed module/console generation and verification reproduce all nine controls outside checkout. The inspector works with up to six states and three letters, synthetic teachers only. The bounded-conformance stage adds the classic W-method as a complete experiment inspector: every state-cover word, middle word, distinguishing suffix, canonical factor and acceptance path, separating finite suite agreement, the supplied candidate's reachable-state bound, a bounded equivalence certificate and a separate exact graph audit — a faulty extra-state candidate passes when its bound is understated, and increasing the bound exposes the fault. Six added native tests include 52,488 exhaustive reference/candidate cases; 131 full native/browser-engine reports are cross-checked, and actual Chrome audits 3,444 test selections and 141 probes across eleven controls and three widths. The fault-atlas stage enumerates every complete labeled implementation in a tiny unary/binary state domain, distinguishes equivalent survivors from real faults, and checks every subset of the constructed W-suite under test-count then letter-count cost, retaining all minima, escaping faults and chosen-test deletion witnesses; irredundancy is not confused with minimum cardinality. Tests cover all 60 tiny minimal references with a separate equivalence oracle; eleven complete cross-engine atlas reports match, and actual Chrome audits 6,996 candidate selections and 804 subset selections across eight controls and three widths. All eighteen native checks on four Python versions, ten Node/Chrome checks and installed three-family reproduction preserve active learning and bounded conformance. These are complete deterministic finite synthetic teachers, not real-world protocol benchmarks; L* and its guarantees belong to Angluin (1987), the W-method to Vasilevskii and Chow — no algorithmic novelty, query-efficiency win or industrial scalability is claimed.

[Exact domain, cost and sources](https://github.com/midya8/dfalab/blob/main/FAULT_ATLAS.md).

- **EUFLab** - Proof-producing ground equality, congruence-derived contradictions and total finite countermodels, where the same output is not the same input: f(a)=f(b) does not imply a=b, and a=b implies f(a)=f(b). Native union-find and proof-forest paths with independent relational closure, certificate and model replay; a separate JavaScript relation engine. Every ordered equal pair has a proof DAG, and every non-entailed equality in a consistent input has a separating finite interpretation. The validation counts that follow describe each development stage, not five separate current benchmarks. Nine native tests include partition-enumeration references, 27 exhaustive constant conjunctions, 64 four-term assertion subsets plus all added disequality probes, 80 seeded DAGs, proof tampering and atomic CLI rejection; two Node tests compare 156 semantic reports and native-check every browser proof DAG, and Chrome tests all 251 ordered queries and every displayed proof/model row at 320/390/1280. The finite-model atlas enumerates every compatible term-equality pattern, finds minimum domain size and classifies each pair as forced equal, forced different or flexible — the most separated quotient is not necessarily the smallest model. It covers all 4,140 eight-name patterns with 16 native tests retaining the original nine, six Node/Chrome tests, 76 complete cross-engine atlas reports and all 4,317 admitted control patterns. The domain-cap debugger holds the domain cap fixed and enumerates every inclusion-minimal assertion conflict and deletion repair, separating intrinsic inconsistency from cap-only conflicts; duplicate occurrences retain their identities, minimal repairs need not have minimum size, and a three-cycle shows why explicit difference-edge coloring alone misses functional constraints. It carries 23 native tests, nine Node/Chrome tests and 269 complete cross-engine reports, with Chrome auditing 379 subset selections, 36 core-relaxation actions, 29 repair actions and 104 cap changes. The costed repair planner enumerates every permitted deletion set, every cost-only optimum and a separate cost/count Pareto frontier, with protected intrinsic or cap-only cores explaining infeasibility and each plan including a total minimum-domain model with kept, protected and omitted assertion truth rows; zero-cost nonminimal ties are preserved, not silently filtered to minimal repairs, and deleting does not mean negating — the live control shows an omitted equality that still holds. Fourteen synthetic controls cover different-size/same-cost optima, two-coordinate tradeoffs, duplicate occurrences, hard obstructions and function-driven cardinality, and native and browser engines agree on 358 complete reports. The exact repair-cost sensitivity stage varies one assertion deletion cost over every integer from 0 to 1,000,000, giving exact keep/delete intervals, breakpoint ties and every optimum mask including zero-cost nonminimal repairs, with an independent native verifier rebuilding all affine plan-cost lines. Bounded to eight terms, arity at most two, eight equalities and eight disequalities, one uninterpreted sort and ground conjunctions only: classical congruence closure, not new theory, a scalable solver or a real-code verifier.

  - [Exact contract and six-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/euflab/blob/main/METHOD.md): Ground-equality semantics, input limits and source attribution.

- **BuchiLab** - Tiny finite adversarial repeated-goal games where a winning state is not a winning policy: idle can stay inside the winning region forever and still lose, so every nested greatest/least fixed-point obligation, progress rank, complete positional controller policy and reachable cyclic non-goal SCC is visible, with rank-guided progress and per-policy audits showing why. A separate checker replays with sets/Floyd reachability and every joint-policy eventual lasso, without calling the generator. Nine native tests include 21,952 exhaustive three-state games; three Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 331 complete Python/JavaScript reports and every control record at 320/390/1280, with installed wheels replaying seven original controls through both CLIs outside checkout. Seven original controls distinguish recurrence from one-time reachability, environment starvation from controller choice, full-graph SCCs from non-goal induced cycles, and an empty controller policy from no policy; every fixed-point update includes unchanged final rounds, and the input is bounded to six states and three moves per state, with all environment moves retained in policy audits and full report JSON exportable for independent checking. The generator uses bitmasks/Tarjan/BFS, the browser sets and graph-search closure, and the native checker sets/Floyd closure plus exact deterministic eventual-lasso replay for every joint positional-policy pair — the algorithms share validation, not generator calls. The lasso extension turns a reachable bad component into an exact action-labelled prefix once and nonempty non-goal cycle forever, for every failing fixed controller policy/root — empty prefixes, self-loops, multiedge cycles, unreachable bad components and duplicate-target action choices explicit, with BFS generation independently checked by bounded simple walks and the joint-policy lasso oracle. Original v1 reports and tests stay intact: 16 native tests across four Python versions and five Node/actual-Chrome tests, including 314 complete witness-report comparisons and every record in ten controls at 320/390/1280, with installed wheels replaying both report families outside checkout; input-order anchor, shortest prefix/cycle individually, not globally shortest lasso. No fairness assumption, learned abstraction, deployed shield, general LTL compiler, formal proof or real-agent guarantee; rank-permitted actions are sufficient, not all winning policies; these are per-policy counterexamples, not one environment strategy defeating every controller policy.

- **ArgumentLab** - Exact finite argumentation with four inspectors: extension families, hypothetical attack switches, SCC-recursive CF2/stage2 and ideal/eager cores. Every path keeps its defense and acceptance records; the ideal/eager path retains original attackers through repeated pruning. Empty stable families keep classical skeptical acceptance separate from a nonvacuous answer. Current checks: 53 Python and 18 Node/actual-Chrome tests, plus installed-wheel replay of base, switch, SCC and ideal controls. An actual PyArg 2.0.2 comparison matches 645 finite frameworks and 1,290 ideal/eager families. Supplied attack graphs are assumptions, not truth scores, persuasion or human argument strength. Exhaustive small frameworks only; no scalable/new solver or arbitrary infinite eager-uniqueness claim.

- **Diagnoselab** - Exact logical-consistency diagnosis where one conflict is never enough: given fixed hard CNF and removable soft clauses, enumerate every relative minimal unsatisfiable subset (MUS) and every minimal correction set (MCS), with every candidate's intersections against both families checking minimal-hitting-set duality in both directions, including empty-family and empty-core boundaries. A repair must hit every core, not merely one, and inclusion-minimal is not minimum cardinality or cost — the default control contrasts one removal at cost 5 with two removals costing 2 — while an inconsistent hard background gives an empty relative core and no soft repair. Every soft subset retains its DPLL/unit trace and an independent full truth-table model, with all MUS member-deletion model lists, all MCS reinsertion UNSAT references and unique-hit core witnesses, every cardinality/cost tie and both deletion-order runs, and duplicate identities, tautologies, empty clauses and unused variables kept explicit. Seventeen Python test functions run on Python 3.10–3.13, with 180 all-proper-subset family oracles, 80 complete-trace controls, positive-cost scaling, clause permutation, literal order/repetition and variable complementation, validation/no-mutation and invalid-CLI-output preservation; four Node/actual-Chrome tests compare all 18 retained reports on 74,292 exact scalar leaves and add 90 independent truth-mask controls, while actual browser inspection covers 827 subset outcomes, 1,301 complete DPLL node records, 20 cores, 52 repairs and 304 truth rows, with every table, export and stale/invalid clearing checked at 320/390/1280, and an isolated installed wheel reproducing all 18 reports outside the checkout and verifying four module origins. The repair-policy consequences extension distinguishes every/some repair from every/some surviving model, with all tied minimum-cost/cardinality policies, complete countermodels and negated-query DPLL traces, and a shared-clause intersection baseline with strict counterexamples — no-repair families are inapplicable, not vacuous certainty — adding 26 new exact reports (16,165 scalar leaves), 67 repairs, 78 policy records and 151 full DPLL nodes, with 24 Python and eight Node/Chrome tests preserving all originals, Chrome checking 78 policy records, 67 repairs, 70 selectable per-repair search nodes and 462 literal rows, and native independent replay covering all 151 nodes. Six Boolean variables, at most eight hard and eight removable clauses; established DPLL and MUS/MCS theory with attributed sources, not a novel or scalable SAT or MaxSAT algorithm, no external solver code or dataset copied, costs as supplied removal preferences rather than failure probabilities, and restored consistency as no causal or physical diagnosis and no claim of formal certification, real fault diagnosis or scalable solver; search traces are tested computational witnesses, not DRAT/LRAT exports or machine-checked proofs. CI covers Python 3.10–3.13, Node 22 and Chrome; the browser runs locally after static assets load.

- **SynthLab** - Counterexample-guided bit-vector program synthesis where fitting the examples is not identifying the function: a learner chooses the cheapest grammar expression fitting known examples, and an exact finite verifier returns the first wrong input. Every iteration, eliminated candidate, complete truth table and evaluation tree is inspectable; full-domain semantic signatures safely deduplicate programs without using the target, and a separate verifier enumerates every raw ordered grammar tree to check bounded completeness and canonical minimum cost. Ten synthetic controls separate example agreement, exact finite correctness and minimum AST-node cost inside the explicit grammar. Eight native tests run on Python 3.10-3.13, including 512 Boolean target/grammar pairs and 2,764 raw trees at each of three widths; four Node/actual-Chrome tests audit 30 reports, 90 iterations, 903 candidate signatures and 3,420 expression probes, and installed module and console reproduction runs outside the checkout. The exact finite teaching-set atlas separates CEGIS correctness from eliminating every rival function: the two-bit XOR control has 132 distinct rival functions and 65,536 input subsets, CEGIS stops correctly with four examples but leaves three rivals, and the atlas finds 182 four-row teaching sets — the same number of examples does not mean the same identifying power. Five teaching controls add seven native tests and three Node/Chrome checks, bringing the project to 15 native tests on Python 3.10-3.13 and seven Node/Chrome checks; Chrome audits 15 teaching reports, 1,203 subset selections and 597 rival truth selections. The exact adaptive query-policy stage solves the smallest worst-case number of further input queries needed to identify any function still fitting the initial observations: a full seeded two-bit grammar leaves ten functions after two known observations, two further adaptive queries suffice while every fixed passive separating set needs three rows, and across all balanced tie resolutions policies need two or three further queries — immediate split balance is not the same objective. Seven additional native tests bring the project to 22 on Python 3.10-3.13 and five additional Node/Chrome checks bring it to 12; the adaptive tests compare 541 complete native/JavaScript reports, and actual Chrome audits 15 adaptive reports, 1,583 version-space selections, 111 query partitions, 306 answer branches, 84 target traces and 105 passive mask selections. The frontier covers pure unsigned 1..3-bit expressions built from 0/1/x/y and selected add/and/neg/not/xor operators through AST node cost 5, with canonical representatives by minimum node cost then ASCII prefix-expression order. Classical CEGIS/enumeration, teaching-set and minimum-cover ideas credited to Goldman and Kearns, and decision-tree dynamic programming and generalized binary search are credited; no novel algorithm, unbounded synthesis, cvc5 integration, production-code benchmark, expected-cost or PAC claim.

- **WarpLab** - Soft-DTW alignment, a path-length atlas and an inverse expected-length response in scalar Python and browser JavaScript. Inspect Gibbs cell occupancies, joint edge flows, sequence/temperature gradients and cost Hessian-vector products, then separate every legal length before changing the per-cell penalty. The inverse inspector checks finite reachability and retains the full bisection witness, numerical flattening and actual mean residual; a width stop is not a residual guarantee or interval certificate. Separate exhaustive oracles check complete reports without calling the generators. Current checks pass 37 Python and 12 Node/Chrome tests, with installed module/console reproduction for 8 alignment, 8 length and 9 inverse controls; cross-language checks compare 168 alignment reports, 98 length atlases and 36 inverse reports; CI covers Python 3.10-3.13 and Node 22/Chrome; 15 deployed assets are source-exact and 75 local/live unmasked screenshots match. Trusted downloads of all 25 controls match browser runtime and verify independently. Inputs are bounded scalar synthetic sequences of length 1..7, with at most 8,989 paths. Raw scores can be negative and are not metrics; occupancies are not transport couplings, and Hessian products use independent cost coordinates. Established mathematics is credited to Cuturi/Blondel and Blondel/Mensch/Vert. No learned model, benchmark, speed or novelty claim; no self-corrected divergence or ground-cost-independent theorem claim.

- **HingeLab** - Exact rational ReLU regions and class-change topology: whether a closed perturbation ball is robust depends on which class owns the boundary — for margin(x) = x with class 1 at margin ≥ 0, from x = 1 the opposite-class set [-2, 0) is open at zero, so distance one is only an infimum and the closed ball is robust, while from x = -1 the set [0, 2] contains an actual adversary. Forward exact affine-interval propagation through one or two ReLU hidden layers, with Python Fraction and normalized browser BigInt arithmetic so every root, coefficient, set and distance is rational; complete cells with canonical open-interior activation masks, exact opposite-class intervals with explicit endpoint flags, per-cell zero witnesses and an exact domain Lipschitz constant. An independent checker enumerates every hidden activation mask and splits the true domain into every endpoint/root atom and intervening open interval, checking the whole region list by finite exact exhaustion, importing neither the forward generator nor its set-construction routine. Base main 52871cd2298909dec76496dabbdf34c4408606c7 is exact across all 24 source files: 12 Python tests on each of 3.10-3.13 including 200 seeded two-layer networks, 144 complete Python/BigInt reports, four Node/Chrome tests, eight controls reproduced from a fresh wheel, live Chrome checks 54 region selections over 24 full reports, five deployed assets source-exact and all 24 screenshots matching pixel for pixel. The all-reference atlas computes every robust reference in the domain by exact class-component erosion: for margin x on [-2,2] at radius 1, class-0 references are [-2,-1) while class-1 are [1,2]; the nested-tent class [-1/2,1/2] at radius 1/2 leaves exactly the singleton {0}, which disappears at 513/1024. Merged main a26692d37e0faf7c0ad9af3a845c56f58618f702 is source-exact across 34 files: 20 Python tests on each of 3.10-3.13, seven Node/Chrome tests, 200 new reference reports plus the preserved 144 base reports, a 972-case tiny-network sweep, eight base and eight reference controls, Chrome 47 robust-component selections over 28 reference reports, nine deployed assets and 52 screenshots matching. The radius-profile extension returns the complete piecewise-linear robust interval length on radius [0,32], per class and in total, with exact open/closed existence intervals and an inverse query for the first radius at most a requested length fraction: for margin x on [-2,2] length is max(4-2r,0), so at radius 2 the length is zero but the class-1 singleton {2} still exists — class 0 existence is [0,2), class 1 is [0,2]. Merged main 4b1ceacf5ffeeb25ed8052c48053a926e91dd047 is source-exact across 44 files: 26 Python tests on each of 3.10-3.13, ten Node/Chrome tests, 104 new complete cross-language profile reports plus 200 reference and 144 base reports, eight controls in each report family, Chrome 28 radius reports, 13 deployed assets and all 80 screenshots matching pixel for pixel. A separate mask/atom checker reconstructs the profile from candidate collision radii and opposite-class closed-ball intersection without importing the profile, reference or erosion code. Robust fraction is interval length over domain length, not accuracy or probability; zero length is not absence. Bounded scalar rational ReLU networks with established piecewise-affine analysis and morphological erosion — Sotoudeh/Thakur, Bunel et al., Heijmans §1.2, Pegden's open-ball convention explicitly distinguished — not a new algorithm, training/import, high-dimensional certificate or benchmark.

- **RewriteLab** - An exact, deliberately small arithmetic-optimization lab for inspecting the boundary between rewrite reachability and algebraic equivalence: finite ordered binary trees over `0,1,x,y,+,*` bounded to 3, 5 or 7 nodes (36, 548 or 10,788 grammar terms), with bidirectional commutativity, associativity, identities, zero annihilation and distribution/factoring at every subtree. `x*(1+1)` has two reachable five-node-budget terms costing 5 while `x+x` costs 3, and seven nodes unlock a three-rewrite distributive bridge; `(1+1)*(1+1)` with multiplication cost 8 has one reachable term costing 14 at seven nodes against an addition-only semantic optimum of 7. The verified base release is 25 exact source files with 11 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and three Node/actual-Chrome tests, 72 complete cross-language reports with 111,316 proof rows and 111,342 semantic rows, and live Chrome checking 26 reports, all 3,612 proof and 3,630 semantic selections at 320/390/1280, with all five deployed assets matching main. The budget-barrier atlas separates the first budget that fits both source and target from the first budget that admits a rewrite proof: for `x*(1+1) -> x+x`, first fitting is 5, first reachable is 7, extra budget 2, shortest distance 3, and 13 of its 213 cap-7 semantic candidates need a larger bridge, while five of six weighted constant-product candidates remain unreached through seven nodes and null means not reached through the cap, not impossible at larger budgets; the stage is 35 source-exact files with 20 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and six Node/actual-Chrome tests, 36 complete Python/JS atlases comparing 13,016 candidate rows and 13,745 nested proof rows, and Chrome checking 25 atlases and every 3,844 candidate selection, with nine deployed assets matching main and 57 screenshots matching without masks. The cost atlas keeps all 512 positive integral (+, multiplication, leaf) weight triples in 1..8 over the fixed graph: for constant four, the product vector (2, 1, 4) ties the addition-only vector (3, 0, 4) exactly when addition and multiplication costs agree — 224 positive-gap triples, 288 zero-gap, 64 ties, each semantic group winning at 288 triples because ties credit both — while the five-node bridge source is strictly dominated with a gap at all 512 triples and no crossover is invented for it; the stage has 27 Python tests across 3.10-3.13 and nine Node/actual-Chrome tests, 17 complete Python/JS weight reports comparing 8,704 outcomes and 93 groups, and Chrome checking 25 weight reports, all 4,096 outcome selections and 34,570 expression selections, with 13 live assets matching main and 83 screenshots matching without masks. The continuous-region extension computes complete optimum regions over every strictly positive cost ratio — the parameter triangle from normalizing the three costs to sum one — with exact halfplanes, reduced rational vertices and separate semantic and reachable regions; a boundary-only winner is not obtainable at strictly positive costs, while an interior tie point or segment can be a real winner despite zero area, and area is not a probability. The stage has 35 Python tests on each of 3.10-3.13 with eight Node engine and four actual-Chrome tests, 17 Python/JavaScript region reports agreeing on 93 groups and 215 vertices, independent tests over all 36 three-node sources, 500 random vector sets and 64 outside-grid positive points, and Chrome checking 25 region reports, all 4,096 point selections and 34,570 expression selections, with 16 deployed assets matching main and 111 screenshots matching exactly without masks. This is an explicit bounded rewrite graph, not an e-graph, tensor engine, learned policy or compiler-speed benchmark; exact rational algebra need not preserve IEEE floating-point behavior, and no claim applies beyond the stated grammar, rules and budget.
