- **CPNetLab** - Exact finite qualitative preferences from supplied tables, not learned human tastes: complete binary CP-nets define legal single-variable improvements, not a universal score — CPNetLab enumerates every outcome and improving flip, finds shortest paths or closed no-path cuts, counts every compatible strict ranking, and supplies two actual opposite rankings when a pair is incomparable. One legal flip can violate more rows: in a four-variable fanout, `0000 → 1000` is an improving flip while violated rows rise from 1 to 3, yet all 2,304 compatible strict rankings still put the target above the start, so counting violated rows is not the preference semantics. Dependency cycles and inconsistency are different: a matching two-variable dependency cycle permits four strict rankings, while reversing one table produces the actual improving cycle `00 → 01 → 11 → 10 → 00`, zero strict rankings, and no vacuous dominance verdict. Many rankings need not settle a pair: two independent consistent cycles admit exactly 101,977,088 rankings while leaving `0000` and `1111` incomparable, and the UI displays a complete ranking for each direction. A separate Python checker rebuilds the entire evidence report using Hamming-one pairs, Floyd-Warshall and suffix-removal counting, rather than the generator's conditional-row flips, breadth-first paths and prefix-ideal counting; Python and JavaScript agree on all eight controls, 61 outcomes and 100 edges, and browser JSON is independently checked only after the Python verifier accepts it, not merely because export worked. Reproduction covers all 36 two-variable networks and 576 ordered queries against all permutations, five three-variable fixtures against all 40,320 permutations each, 160 seeded additional networks and strict evidence forgeries, on Python 3.10-3.13 with an installed wheel outside the checkout; actual Chrome checks 24 reports at three widths, all 689 ordered control comparisons, every rendered conditional row, path, ranking witness and no-path cut, all eight evidence exports, and stale/invalid/cycle recovery. The row-subsets extension enumerates every whole conditional-row subset, not one convenient explanation: eight developed controls cover 865 subset graphs with actual improving paths, inconsistency cycles and compatible strict-ranking witnesses; the default 256-subset control has two minimal conflicts and six irreducible withdrawals — three remove one row, three remove two — and a separate control has two-row and four-row minimal path supports, so minimal does not mean minimum, and neither means the right human preference to abandon. The independent Python checker labels Hamming-one pairs, uses Floyd-Warshall paths and checks all proper candidate subsets, reconstructing every mask and all four antichains without importing either analyzer; release checks are 14 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and six combined Node/actual-Chrome tests, all 576 ordered two-variable query atlases (6,400 subset records), all 400 two-variable masks against every possible total order, and 91 accepted seeded row atlases, with actual deployed Chrome selecting all 865 masks, all 46 antichain buttons, every row toggle and all eight exports. Exact scope is zero to four variables and at most 16 outcomes with complete strict binary local tables — no learned human preferences, local ties, missing rows, constraints, cardinal utility, partial-model semantics or scalable-solver claim; CP-net theory and graph/counting methods are established prior work, and these are developed synthetic controls, not human-choice measurements or a new algorithm.

- **EpistemLab** - A public announcement can be truthful and make its own content false: in the default two-world experiment, "p, but agent a does not know p" retains one world and becomes false there — the fact remains true, the ignorance does not. EpistemLab shows every world removed, every surviving information block, and the newly evaluated truth set of every subformula. Eight developed controls separate everybody's knowledge from common knowledge with an actual two-agent failure chain; show distributed knowledge that neither agent has alone; inspect disconnected common-knowledge components; and follow three observers through an 8 → 7 → 4 → 1 world sequence, with repeated updates and eliminated focus worlds explicit — empty is not false, and pointed query values are null when the focus no longer exists. A separate Python verifier rebuilds the complete report with truth-set algebra, partition blocks, greatest fixed points and Floyd-Warshall distances, rather than trusting the generator's recursive pointwise semantics and breadth-first paths; shared strict input validation and written semantics are disclosed, and the verifier is separate from the generator, not unrelated software. JavaScript reproduces the complete control reports, and browser exports count as independently checked only after the Python verifier accepts them. Every modal obligation includes all reachable worlds, canonical shortest paths and an actual shortest failure witness when one exists. Release checks are seven Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and four Node/actual-Chrome tests, all 200 three-world/two-agent partition-and-valuation models, 120 seeded nested requests and strict malformed/forged-evidence rejection, with an installed 0.1.0 wheel reproduced outside the checkout through both module and console entry points; actual Chrome checks 24 responsive reports, 51 round snapshots, 383 formula/world selections, 142 modal obligations, eight exports and stale/invalid/empty recovery. The disclosures extension enumerates every retained-world subset but counts only actual Boolean-definable announcements over the supplied atoms as solutions: an arbitrary what-if restriction can split worlds with identical atomic facts, and no Boolean formula over those atoms can do that — the atlas supplies a retained/removed pair with the same valuation as an explicit obstruction, or complete defining DNF cubes when the restriction is expressible. Eight developed controls cover 324 restrictions and 25 separate knowledge goals; the default goal has two inclusion-maximal successful disclosures retaining two and three worlds, only the latter maximum-retained, while another control has two successful diagnostic restrictions and no Boolean solution, and a stronger truthful Boolean disclosure can also destroy an ignorance goal. The search checks all proper supersets, not just one-bit neighbours: a direct regression has successful masks 1 and 7 with failing intermediate masks 3 and 5. A separate Python checker enumerates world combinations and unions of valuation classes with independent truth-set/fixed-point/Floyd semantics, rebuilding every defining cube, obstruction, post-update query truth set, frontier and stronger-disclosure loss witness without importing the atlas generator; the UI shows the complete restricted model and actual modal obligation paths, and a removed focus has null pointed answers — inspecting another surviving world never turns it into an answer about the eliminated focus. Release checks are 14 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and nine Node/actual-Chrome tests, all 200 small-model atlases (1,600 restriction records) against every one-atom Boolean truth function, 45 nested-goal atlases, malformed and forged-evidence rejection, and an installed 0.2.0 wheel reproduced outside the checkout through both CLI entry points for both eight-control families; deployed Chrome checks 24 responsive reports, 972 mask selections, 75 goal selections, 204 frontier/witness buttons, 2,596 subformula selections including 1,170 modal obligations, 87 world toggles, eight exports and stale/invalid/prior-anchor/empty recovery, with the original sequential-update checks still green. EpistemLab0.3 separates uncertainty about a world from uncertainty about an event: in the original read-p/read-not-p/idle control, the receiver learns p but the observer still does not know whether the receiver learned, and removing idle changes that nested knowledge without making p common knowledge, while a separately labeled public-announcement counterfactual would make all three queries true. Preconditions are evaluated before the event and can become false afterward; the actual event marks a pointed pair without deleting alternative events, equal preconditions do not merge copies, and a nonempty global product can lack the designated pair, in which case post-event focus values are null rather than false. Both supplied world and event relations are equivalence partitions, preserving S5 on every nonempty product. Release checks are 30 Python tests and thirteen Node/Chrome tests, retaining the original public-announcement and Boolean-disclosure workflows; complete native, independent relation-row and browser reports agree on 648 requests, 2,552 Cartesian candidates, 5,984 product-arrow obligations and 9,208 before/after subformula sets, with 1,072 separately altered noninput primitive leaves rejected, and actual pyModelChecking 1.3.4 independently reconstructs products and checks 1,944 snapshots and 13,812 sets through 8,961 ordinary CTL calls. Real Chrome exercises 36 control/width reports at 1280/390/320 with 129 retained-cell interactions, 255 source rows, 1,743 selected relation obligations, 783 subformula selections, 963 modal obligations, 3,219 path rows and twelve trusted completed downloads replayed natively; fresh installed wheels verify eleven module origins and twelve event controls through two entrypoints, with 24 forged CLI atomic-output checks plus 24 direct forgeries. Bounds are four worlds, three informational events and twelve retained pairs, and this models observed sensing or uncertainty whether a read happened, not a fully unnoticed secret where an outsider falsely excludes the actual event. Exact scope is finite supplied S5 models of at most 16 worlds, five atoms, three agents, six queries and four sequential global truthful restrictions, and for disclosures at most eight supplied worlds, five fixed atoms, three S5 agents, six separately evaluated goals, no prior announcements and one Boolean restriction — no beliefs, probabilities, language-to-knowledge inference, real-person knowledge claims, arbitrary public-announcement quantification, fresh atoms, epistemic-announcement search, speaker-knowledge requirement, arbitrary-announcement logic, general planning, shortest-formula claim, privacy guarantee or new-algorithm claim; truth loss is evidence for the supplied model and update, not a proof that a formula fails in every model, and counting retained worlds is a finite combinatorial objective, not probability or real information leakage.

[Product derivation and inspected sources](https://github.com/midya8/epistemlab/blob/main/EVENTS.md).

- **StableLab** - A model can be supported and minimal, yet still not stable: in the program `p <- not p; p <- q; q <- p`, the only classical model `{p,q}` is both supported and minimal, but its candidate reduct contains only a positive cycle and derives nothing, so there is no stable model — StableLab enumerates every candidate interpretation of a tiny normal logic program, shows the actual Gelfond-Lifschitz reduct and its synchronous derivation rounds from the empty set, and lists every original rule's truth test, every support, every proper-subset classical model and every nonempty unfounded subset with all rule-level obligations. Other controls separate even and odd default cycles, constraint filtering, a fact that grounds four derivation layers, exceptions to defaults, one empty stable model versus no model, and 16 stable alternatives among 256 candidates; ten developed synthetic controls contain 300 candidates in total, and when no stable model exists, brave and skeptical answers are explicitly null rather than presenting vacuous universal truth as evidence. A separate Python checker does not call the generator: it enumerates every reduct model and proper candidate subset to test stability, intersects models of the definite reduct for its least closure, reconstructs rounds by bounded proof heights and independently checks every unfounded subset, and browser exports count as independently checked only after `stablelab verify evidence.json` accepts them. Release evidence is nine Python tests on 3.10/3.11/3.12/3.13 and four Node/actual-Chrome tests, all 1,177 two-atom programs through two signed-body rules/constraints, 200 seeded three-atom programs, forged-evidence rejection and an installed 0.1.0 wheel reproduced outside the checkout through both CLI entry points on all ten controls; an optional external clingo 5.8.3 comparison agreed on stable-model families for 1,387 programs. Actual deployed Chrome checks 30 responsive reports, all 900 candidate selections, 6,537 unfounded subset selections and 16,605 rule obligations, 1,926 closure rounds, every displayed rule/reduct/support/query verdict, ten exports accepted by Python, and stale/invalid input and recovery, with all 27 native/fresh feature/main source files and six deployed assets plus root matching, and all 31 feature/main and 31 main/live unmasked screenshot pairs matching. The strong-equivalence extension checks every SE pair — up to five atoms, 243 pairs, 24 normal rules per side — and produces an actual rule context whenever two finite modules differ, including controls with equal classical and stable families but unequal SE sets; eleven controls include an actual two-edge positive cycle gadget, safe transitive-edge removal, redundant rules, contextual inconsistency and empty vocabulary, with an independent three-valued Python reconstruction and proper-subset context model checks rather than another call to the generator. Reproduction is 16 Python tests on 3.10–3.13 and eight Node/Chrome tests with an installed 0.2.0 wheel, both CLI entry points, all 11 comparison controls and forged-verdict rejection; optional clingo 5.8.3 agrees on 5,184 base and 3,514 augmented families across 2,592 comparisons, and deployed Chrome checks 33 comparison reports at desktop/390/320 widths, all 993 displayed pairs and 9,012 rule obligations, 24 context displays and 11 downloaded reports independently verified in Python, with 65 unmasked screenshots matching for final feature/main and for main/live across the base and comparison inspectors and all 11 deployed assets plus root matching final main exactly. The uniform-equivalence extension checks every positive fact subset over the declared vocabulary, with the smallest separator minimum-cardinality only after all smaller inputs agree and equal-size ties using the lowest bitmask; ten controls reach minimum fact counts 0/1/2/3/4, empty vocabulary and the complete 32-input table, and the complete UE-pair atlas retains diagonal and maximal proper SE pairs, exposes every disqualifying proper extension and checks that UE equality agrees with the full input table — the credited Eiter/Fink Example 2 agrees under all eight fact inputs but fails under the displayed rule context, so ordinary, uniform and strong equivalence are separate promises. A separate Python checker reconstructs context candidates through definite-model intersections and proper-subset reduct tests without calling either generator; final feature/main reproduction is 22 Python tests on 3.10/3.11/3.12/3.13 and eleven Node/actual-Chrome tests, an installed 0.3.0 wheel outside the checkout, both CLI entry points, all 10 base/11 comparison/10 uniform controls and forged-report rejection, with optional clingo 5.8.3 agreeing on 20,978 augmented stable families across 10,489 fact contexts and 2,511 comparisons. Actual deployed Chrome checks 30 uniform reports at desktop/390/320 widths, every one of 255 fact selections, 4,539 candidate selections, 41,343 displayed rule obligations and 1,290 UE-pair selections, plus ten exports accepted by Python, with both earlier inspectors also passing, all 56 native/fresh source files and 16 deployed assets plus root matching, and 96 feature/main and 96 main/live unmasked screenshots matching exactly. A main-only CI failure exposed a test-harness race — a download path could exist before its bytes finished writing — and the preserved failure led to a delayed/partial-write regression and transport-readiness checks across all three inspectors; parsing, full evidence equality and independent verification still run once, and semantic failures are not retried. Exact scope is at most eight declared propositional atoms and 32 normal rules or headless constraints for the base inspector, and at most five atoms and 24 normal rules per side for both equivalence atlases, with added inputs restricted to positive facts — no disjunction, variables, aggregates, classical negation, choice rules, optimization, well-founded semantics or scalable-solver claim; stable-model, strong-equivalence and uniform-equivalence theory are established prior work, and these are developed synthetic controls, not real agents' beliefs, with model counts not probabilities.

- **ChannelLab** - One task ties. The information order does not: at a uniform prior over states 0 and 1, two binary experiments both achieve exactly 3/4 optimal classification accuracy, yet neither can simulate the other using a single state-independent randomized signal transformation — and two supplied two-action decision tasks split the other way, B ahead by 1/12 on one and A ahead by 1/12 on the other, so equal accuracy is not equal information. A passing direction constructs a kernel K>=0 with each row summing to 1 and A K=B for both states, found by exact bounded-polytope basis enumeration; a failed direction supplies a knot t with C_B(t)>C_A(t) and payoffs (0,0) versus (-t,1-t), a separating decision problem rather than a failed numerical search. The v0.2 all-prior decision atlas holds the two channels and payoff table fixed and varies p=P(state 1) over the complete closed interval [0,1]: every deterministic signal-to-action policy has an exact affine value and a closed optimality range, including singleton endpoints, with all tied policies, the no-signal value, each information-value line and every additional A-B ranking root retained even when no policy changes — for the default classification task the exact knots are 0, 1/3, 1/2, 2/3 and 1, A wins on (0,1/2), B on (1/2,1), and the supplied uniform prior ties, with the 1/3 and 2/3 knots policy changes rather than ranking changes. The generator intersects complete-policy half-spaces while a separate checker uses per-signal action crossings, Cartesian products of ties and per-signal inequalities for every policy range, and a separate verifier replays every field and checks feasible kernel equations or the strict witness without importing the generator or its elimination/basis solver. Release checks are 30 Python tests including 297 separately altered non-input atlas leaves rejected and 372 separately altered non-input primitive leaves rejected in the default report, with 710 complete Python/checker/BigInt report comparisons covering 13 controls, all 625 ordered pairs of denominator-four binary-output channels and 72 seeded rectangular pairs, 6,994 task policies, 627 feasible directional kernels and 793 strict directional counterexamples; actual SciPy 1.15.3 HiGHS executes 1,420 directional feasibility solves, its 627 feasible cases matching the exact relation with worst equality residual below 2.8e-16 against a 1e-8 tolerance, and atlas parity covers 710 complete cases with 8,486 policies, 3,401 knots and 2,691 open intervals, with 16,972 LPs rebuilding policy-optimal prior ranges and all 6,251 nonempty ranges agreeing at worst tested endpoint difference 0 against a 1e-8 tolerance. Six Node/actual-Chrome tests check 39 control/width reports at 1280, 390 and 320 pixels, 78 directions, 165 posterior rows, 162 hinge rows, 366 task-policy rows, 99 kernel rows and 147 witness-signal rows, with atlas tables adding 444 policy rows, 180 knot rows and 141 interval rows and thirteen original-report plus thirteen atlas trusted completed downloads byte-exact and accepted by their native verifiers; a fresh installed 0.2.0 wheel outside the checkout checks eight module origins, all 13 reports and 13 atlases through console/module entrypoints, with each report type rejecting 26 direct and 52 CLI forgeries. Exact scope is exactly two states, one to three signals per channel and one to three actions, with a full-support prior for the supplied comparison and certain-state endpoints included in the atlas — no channel learning, sequential design, real sensor validation, privacy guarantee, finance, safety promise, multi-state scalar-order shortcut, scalability claim or new Blackwell algorithm; these are supplied teaching problems, not measured sensors, a dataset, an accuracy leaderboard or a real-world recommendation.

- **CredalLab** - Each probability can look valid while their joint model is impossible: three pairwise-disagreement events over three binary variables can each demand probability 1, yet every binary assignment satisfies at most two of them, so no joint distribution satisfies all three — the minimum common interval relaxation is exactly 1/3, and at 2/3 each the unique grouped distribution is (0, 1/3, 1/3, 1/3). The decision layer makes the same gap about beliefs rather than probabilities: on the exact menu (3,0), (0,3), (1,1), the third action defends against each pure rival at a different belief, yet no one common belief makes it best — it remains strict pairwise maximal and interval-undominated and is the unique pure-action maximin choice, its best shared-belief margin is exactly -1/2 at belief (1/2,1/2), and a fixed 50/50 mixture of its rivals has utility 3/2 in both worlds and excludes it by 1/2 everywhere. A separate interior-only tie control changes the third action to (3/2,3/2): it is E-admissible only at the half/half belief, not at an original credal vertex, so the implementation solves the menu-intersection margin LP with a free-below margin variable rather than checking only the original probability vertices. The inspector reports E-admissible, strict pairwise maximal, interval-undominated, pure-action maximin and maximax sets, with every action's common belief, expected utilities, exclusion-mixture weights and world payoffs, and replays every range, ordered-pair, margin and projected-mixture primal/dual certificate; a standalone native checker validates supplied evidence without loading the solver or generator and accepts alternative attaining witnesses. The convention is explicit — no pointwise-admissibility prefilter, strictly positive robust advantage for dominance, all ties retained including zero-mass boundaries — following the strict-only definitions in Nakharutai, Troffaes and Caiado (2019), and randomized rivals are exclusion certificates, not all mixed optimal actions, a mixed maximin solver or advice to randomize. Version 0.1 checks are 16 native tests, 120 complete reports (78 feasible, 42 infeasible, 69 with defined positive-mass conditional bounds), 505 rejected primitive certificate mutations and 690 rebuilt HiGHS LPs including 138 Charnes-Cooper conditional LPs at tolerance 1e-8, with an installed wheel over six module origins, 16 controls and two CLI entry points rejecting 32 tampered reports, and actual Chrome replaying 48 control-width views, 288 certificate selections, 1,671 inequality rows, 897 witness-variable rows and 16 downloaded reports. Version 0.2 adds 15 tests for 31 total, 96 complete Python/BigInt decision reports over 75 feasible menus with 56 excluded actions, 824 rejected primitive decision modifications across three controls and 1,420 input-built HiGHS LPs with largest absolute error 2.220446049250313e-16 at tolerance 1e-8, with an installed decision wheel over ten module origins, 16 controls and both entry points rejecting 32 CLI forgeries, and actual Chrome checking 48 responsive views, 129 action selections, 855 certificate selections, 2,784 independent inequality rows, 1,884 variable rows and 16 downloaded reports natively checked; all six combined Node/Chrome tests pass and the original probability checks still pass. Decision inputs are limited to six worlds/actions and six assessments with separate 20,000 candidate-basis limits, utilities are bounded exact signed rationals on one common state-independent scale, CLI model input is capped at 64 KiB and certificate reports at 8 MiB; classical finite probabilistic satisfiability and linear optimization, not a new inference theorem, learned probability model, credal network, calibration benchmark or production safety system.

- **AuctionLab** - Enumerates finite allocations, efficient ties, VCG pivot prices, report-independent bundle menus and seller-inclusive cooperative-core deficits. In the two-item control, welfare is 12, each winner pays 4, revenue is 8 and the seller with the bundle bidder has a deficit of 2. Selected unilateral reports are scored using true values; they do not prove truthfulness over all valuations or define a collusion strategy. A separate disjoint-bundle Python verifier replays complete certificates. 24 Python and five Node/actual-Chrome tests; 16 developed controls; an exhaustive 196-profile finite-type audit; input-built HiGHS reference and installed-wheel replay. At most four items and three bidders with supplied monotone integer values, zero seller reserve and quasilinear utility. Established VCG theory, not a new theorem or a real auction.

- **MatchLab** - Stable is not cost-optimal: in the developed three-by-three default, the unconstrained cost minimum is matching (1,2,0) at cost 3, blocked by (A2,B1); the cheapest stable assignment is (2,0,1) at cost 13, a stability premium of 10 — and neither side's deferred acceptance selects it. A four-by-four control has four stable assignments in an incomparable diamond lattice.

- **ParetoLab** - Nondominated is not supported: in the developed default, three parallel paths (0,12), (7,7) and (12,0) are all nondominated, yet the middle one needs t >= 7/12 against one extreme and t <= 5/12 against the other to minimize t*cost1 + (1-t)*cost2 — the intersection is empty, so no positive weighted sum selects it, and no finer weight grid can repair that. A hard second-cost ceiling of 7 instead makes (7,7) the lexicographic optimum. Allowing a whole-path lottery with probabilities 7/12 and 5/12 on the two extremes yields expected costs (5,7), two below the deterministic first cost — but the first extreme violates the realized ceiling with probability 7/12, and the inspector shows that probability rather than calling the mixture safe. Against each rival path the weighted-score comparison is an exact linear inequality in t; reduced rational half-lines intersected with [0,1] define point cells and open intervals with constant optimizer identities, and the midpoint is a witness, not a grid approximation. Positive-weight support means optimal for some 0<t<1; a dominated path may minimize at t=0 or 1 but is never promoted to the frontier, and a nondominated collinear point optimal at a single interior weight does count as supported. The reverse-node dynamic program extends every nondominated child suffix along every outgoing edge, pruning strictly dominated suffixes because adding the same prefix preserves dominance, while equal-cost identities remain — and the complete full-path census stays separate from the local trace, including extensions of already-pruned child suffixes. An independent Python checker imports input validation only and rebuilds the evidence by forward path enumeration, a complete objective-line arrangement and separate 2x2 determinant mixture reconstruction; the browser replay shares evidence construction and is not labeled an independent checker. Release checks are 20 Python tests, a 729-profile exhaustive audit over all 9 vectors in 0..2, 52 complete native/browser parity reports with 101 paths, 176 weight cells and 130 thresholds, 579 rejected forgeries per engine, 556 input-built HiGHS graph-MILP/probability-LP solves with 2,403 rows and maximum objective error 8.9e-16 at 1e-8 tolerance with SciPy 1.15.3, an installed wheel over 6 module origins with 12 controls, 2 entrypoints and 24 CLI forgeries, and actual Chrome over 48 responsive views with 220 path selections, 208 weight cells, 180 budget states and 324 label rows, with 12 inputs and 12 certificates downloaded and replayed by Python. The documentation review is published in PR #2, main 3f0c8c45d8c8f79ef9cf83a89bac41beb2e5dde2; fresh checkouts of the head and merged main matched all 31 reviewed files and passed 20 Python tests, 4 Node/Chrome tests, installed-wheel reproduction and generated-asset checks, and five live assets match main; of 40 live screenshots, 39 match local pixels exactly and one 768px hero differs at 25 pixels, with no content or layout difference on inspection. Scope is 2..7 topologically ordered nodes, at most 12 forward arcs, two additive nonnegative integer costs 0..20 and at most 64 complete suffix paths per node; 12 controls are developed and 40 DAGs are seeded — supplied costs, not learned preferences, and the unsupported-efficient-point phenomenon is classical, illustrated with a hand-specified teaching control, not a new theorem or an empirical claim about AI agents.
  - Case study: [paretolab.md](paretolab.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/paretolab](https://github.com/midya8/paretolab)
  - Live: [Exact finite biobjective planning inspector](https://midya8.github.io/paretolab/)
  - [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/paretolab/pull/2)
- **RiskLab** - A root optimum is not a continuation optimum: in the developed `reversal` control at confidence alpha 1/4, a first observation reaches two decisions with probabilities 3/4 and 1/4, and enumerating all four policies gives expected loss, static CVaR and nested CVaR of 3/2, 8/3, 8/3 for p0; 11/8, 5/2, 26/9 for p1; 9/8, 5/2, 22/9 for p2; and 1, 7/3, 8/3 for p3 — the unique static optimum p3 is not the nested optimum p2, and at the second reached decision p3's conditional static CVaR is 10 while the best continuation has 28/3, so a decision maker reusing the same conditional objective would change the plan. In the `ignored_branch` control both complete policies have nested root value 10 even though one chooses loss 2 where loss 1 is available, and only backward Bellman selection chooses the locally optimal continuation. The tail is exact, not a conditional mean: with loss 0 at probability 3/4 and loss 8 at 1/4, confidence 1/2 gives CVaR 4, while neither conditioning on loss at or above VaR (mean 2) nor above VaR (mean 8) gives the right answer. Release checks are 16 Python tests with a separate path-enumeration and bounded-simplex-vertex tail-fill checker that rejects 1,608 altered non-input primitive leaves in the reversal report, 821 complete Python/checker/BigInt report comparisons covering twelve controls, 80 seeded trees and all 729 combinations of a four-leaf loss/confidence/branch-law grid with 1,121 full policies, 9,896 input-built SciPy 1.15.3 HiGHS primal/dual LP solves with worst absolute objective error below 1.8e-15 at 1e-8 tolerance, an installed wheel over six module origins with all twelve controls through both entrypoints and 24 direct plus 48 CLI forgery rejections, and actual Chrome over 36 control/width reports at 1280, 390 and 320 pixels with 216 policy selections, 1,518 leaf rows, 2,334 node rows, 696 continuation rows, 120 nested chance proofs and twelve trusted downloads checked by the native verifier. Scope is at most 15 nodes, eight terminal leaves, four decision nodes, depth six and 64 full deterministic policies, with loss magnitude at most 100 and one confidence alpha in [0,1) — complete deterministic contingent policies on a supplied tree, not randomized policies or a general risk-sensitive MDP, and no financial advice, new CVaR algorithm, empirical ranking or real-world decision guarantee.

  - Live: [Exact finite scenario-tree decision inspector](https://midya8.github.io/risklab/)

- **RectLab** - A cover is not a partition: in the developed default, the 4×4 matrix `0101 / 1101 / 1110 / 1011` has 11 positive cells and 41 nonempty all-one rectangles; every cover needs at least four rectangles, but the maximum fooling set is only three, so a valid largest fooling set does not certify the minimum cover size. The unrestricted optimum has 68 unordered minimum covers; restricting factors to maximal rectangles leaves the size at four and reduces the count to 13 — both counts are correct for different candidate families. Version 0.2 adds random access by canonical zero-based decimal index, plus every factor's exact optimum inclusion count: R0 appears in exactly one of the 68, R12 in 40, and under the maximal-only restriction R12's count is nine out of thirteen while R0 is ineligible and no default factor is mandatory — while in the unique-cover overlap control all three selected factors are mandatory. The overlap control `1011 / 1100 / 1110 / 0001` has a unique minimum cover of three rectangles, yet every disjoint partition needs four, and its ordinary arithmetic rank is also four. That rank is displayed as a contrast, not used as a Boolean-rank lower bound. The multiplicity grid shows the overlap directly: for binary factors B and C, the Boolean product uses OR over componentwise AND while the ordinary product records coverage multiplicity instead — amber cells are where the factors overlap, and thresholding positive multiplicities recovers the input. XOR is not used. Switching to the partition witness removes the overlap, at the price of a fourth factor. Membership is relative to the selected family: mandatory means in every optimum, optional means some but not all, never optimal means none, and summing all factor inclusion counts yields family count times minimum factor count. Release checks are 18 Python and 6 Node/Chrome tests, with an exhaustive class of all 512 binary 3×3 matrices, 64 complete Python/JavaScript reports over nine developed controls and 55 seeded inputs, 964 legal rectangles and 24,575 fooling-subset audits retained, all 170 primitive noninput fields in a three-positive-cell certificate forged individually and rejected per engine, and 159 input-built SciPy 1.15.3/HiGHS cover/partition/fooling MILP models over 53 nonempty inputs, plus an installed wheel over six site-packages module origins with 18 forgery failures and 54 select/rank pairs, and actual Chrome over 36 control-width views at 1440/768/390/320 with 108 witness-family selections, 520 optimal-set inspections, 2,988 membership rows and 192 maximum-fooling selections, with nine input and nine certificate downloads checked natively. Scope is at most five rows, five columns and twelve positive cells; counts concern distinct unordered rectangle sets, not ordered matrix-factor columns — a bounded exact tool, not a new ranking algorithm, statistical sampler or certificate-authenticity system.
  - Case study: [rectlab.md](rectlab.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/rectlab](https://github.com/midya8/rectlab)
  - Live: [Exact finite Boolean factorization inspector](https://midya8.github.io/rectlab/)
  - [Exact method](https://github.com/midya8/rectlab/blob/main/docs/method.md) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/rectlab/pull/1)

- **OrderLab** - No improving swap, still not the best order: the developed default uses ballots `0>1>2` at weight 3, `1>2>0` at weight 2 and `2>0>1` at weight 2, and strict majority margins form the cycle `0>1>2>0` with margins `3,3,1`. Start at `1>2>0`: cost 10, and its two adjacent swaps worsen cost by 3 and 1, so strict descent stops immediately — yet the unique global optimum `0>1>2` costs 8. Choosing the cheaper direction independently for every pair gives a lower bound of 7, but not a transitive order: the local/global gap is 2, and the extra cost above the pair bound is 1, which the report calls `cycle_tax` — not a cycle count, and not a causal attribution to individual cycles. The local policy takes the most negative adjacent delta, breaking equal improvements by the leftmost position, and never traverses zero-cost plateaus; that policy controls only the trace, it does not prefilter the global family. Every order's ballot inversions and weighted cost, every pair's disagreement and lower-bound excess, and every adjacent neighbor's exact cost delta are visible, and the complete order census marks local, strict-local and global status independently; the six-candidate all-tie control keeps all 720 optima instead of picking one by ID. Weights are supplied multiplicities, not learned reliability. Python places the first candidate in each exact subset recurrence while JavaScript independently computes values by placing the last candidate and scores full orders directly from ballots, and a native replay checker enumerates every restricted permutation and sums ballot inversions directly without importing either solver or the report generator. An optional input-built binary linear-ordering formulation with SciPy/HiGHS, every triangle constraint and explicit 0..1 bounds and integrality, checks one optimum cost per non-singleton profile as a numerical reference at tolerance 1e-8, not the exact all-family certificate. Release checks are 18 Python tests over 76 complete developed/seeded profiles with 10,427 full orders, 1,542 subsets, 3,883 transitions and 22 strict cycles, 1,100 changed derived primitives across three controls all rejected, two Node tests with 76 complete JS/Python reports and native replay, 65 HiGHS solves with 422 triangle constraints and zero observed optimum cost discrepancy, an installed wheel over six module origins with sixteen controls, both CLI entry points and 32 rejected tampered reports, and actual Chrome over 48 control-width views with 5,091 order selections and 846 subset selections, plus a visible row oracle rebuilding 12,861 ballot, 70,464 pair, 24,132 swap and 2,019 transition rows from ballots and sixteen trusted-click exact reports verified natively. Scope is one to six candidates, one to sixteen complete strict ballot rows, positive integer weights up to 1,000 and total weight up to 10,000, with cost bounded by 150,000 and order counts by 720; no tied or partial input rankings. Classical Kemeny aggregation — the candidate-subset recurrence is credited to Betzler et al., Theorem 4, and Dwork et al. establish the local/global distinction — not Borda, a learned ranker, a fairness model, a real election recommendation, a held-out evaluation or a new theorem.
  - Case study: [orderlab.md](orderlab.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/orderlab](https://github.com/midya8/orderlab)
  - Live: [Exact finite rank-aggregation inspector](https://midya8.github.io/orderlab/)
  - [Method and attributed theory](https://github.com/midya8/orderlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Documentation review](https://github.com/midya8/orderlab/pull/2)
- **FireLab** - Balance is not an execution: in the developed default, the net starts at (A,B,R)=(0,0,2) and is asked for (0,1,1), and transition t0: A+R -> A+B has net effect (0,+1,-1), so counts (1,0,0) balance exactly — yet t0 cannot fire, because there is no A token to enable it: canceling the shared A term in the incidence matrix must not cancel its enabling guard. Two helper transitions turn R into A and A back into R, and the canonical shortest goal sequence t1 -> t0 -> t2, length 3 with one shortest word, reaches the same goal using counts the proposal omitted; supplying all three counts makes that multiset executable, while removing the helpers makes the balanced target globally unreachable. The inspector keeps three questions separate — does this count vector land exactly at the goal, does any legal ordering execute exactly this vector, and does any legal sequence reach the goal with any counts — because a fixed-count failure is not a global impossibility proof. Every partial firing multiset is retained too: for every subcount vector k the row m(k)=initial+Ck is kept even when negative, with prefix counts enumerating legal words reaching the row and suffix counts enumerating legal residual words, and a suffix can exist without a legal prefix; the boundary counts (2,2,2,2) on four enabled no-ops give 8!/(2!^4)=2520 legal words, not 8! labeled permutations, and a count-realizable multiset can still end at the wrong goal with its goal-reaching count at zero. Release checks are 18 native tests over all 14 original controls, an exhaustive class of 4,704 ordered two-transition/initial-marking/count cases, 350 seeded requests with full independent replay, 114 complete Python/JavaScript reports with 629 marking rows and 806 lattice rows, 197 noninput primitive forgeries rejected per engine, an optional real PM4Py 2.7.23 reference over 164 requests with 771 enabling states, 1,541 transition results and 2,834 accepted count words, an installed wheel over six module origins with 14 controls, two entrypoints and 28 forged CLI failures, and actual Chrome over 56 views at 1440/768/390/320 with 268 state selections, 664 move rows, 496 lattice rows and 1,016 remaining-count decisions, plus 14 trusted completed report downloads each independently accepted by the native CLI. Scope is 2–4 places, at most 4 transitions, integer arc entries 0–2, each transition strictly conserving the token total, initial total 0–3, each supplied count 0–3 with sum at most 8, at most 20 markings and 81 subcount rows; ordinary untimed interleaving only. Established Petri-net enabling, marking-equation and conservation theory — Bonet, Cabasino/Giua/Seatzu and linear algebraic techniques — with TINA, PM4Py and the Hamburg tool catalog as prior tooling, not a new theorem, a scalable planner or a real resource protocol.
  - Case study: [firelab.md](firelab.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/firelab](https://github.com/midya8/firelab)
  - Live: [Exact finite resource-planning inspector](https://midya8.github.io/firelab/)
  - [Sources and prior work](https://github.com/midya8/firelab/blob/main/SOURCES.md) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/firelab/pull/1)
- **CompileLab** - A projected model is not a sum of hidden completions: for `x0 OR x1`, keep only x0. Assignment 0 has one satisfying hidden completion and assignment 1 has two, so there are three full models but two projected retained models; with every literal weighted 1/2 the full mass is 3/4 while the retained-only projected mass is 1, and the two fiber completion masses are 1/4 and 1/2 while both projected retained masses are 1/2 — different counting spaces, not inconsistent estimates. Every retained row exposes all satisfying evidence-compatible full extensions, their weights, their count and the lexicographically first full witness, with witness strings always following x0..x(n-1) rather than the chosen BDD order, and a zero-mass extension still establishes existential truth. The same lesson runs through the diagrams: the developed default `(x0 iff x2) and (x1 iff x3)` has four full models with weighted mass 1/4 when every literal weighs 1/2, yet its ordinary noncomplemented reduced ordered BDD uses 6 to 9 reachable nonterminals across all 24 variable orders, and a five-variable multiplexer control spans 7 to 13 nonterminals over all 120 orders while retaining 16 full models and mass 1/2 — canonical means unique up to graph isomorphism for a fixed variable order, not across orders, and node sizes exclude terminals and are not timing or memory measurements. A declared variable that does not occur in the formula remains part of the original assignment space: a skipped variable contributes two choices to an unweighted count but `(w_false + w_true)` to weighted mass, and every edge audit includes the gap variables between its test and child. Conditioning pins the specified values and removes those variables from the free diagram universe; its diagram mass excludes the pinned literals while the separately named full evidence-event mass includes them, with exact nonnegative rational arithmetic throughout and zero-weight models still logical models. Release checks are 15 native tests over all 12 developed controls, an exhaustive class of 512 subsets of nine clauses × four keep subsets = 2,048 configurations, 180 seeded native inputs independently replayed, 102 complete Python/JavaScript reports with 90 nonterminals and 1,394 trace rows, all 719 primitive noninput forgeries rejected per engine, an optional actual BSD-licensed dd 0.6.0 reference over 574 CNFs with 340 full models and 175 projected assignments checking complete model sets, counts and exact masses from its own models, an installed wheel over five site-packages module origins with 12 controls × two entry points and 24 forged CLI failures, and actual Chrome over 48 views at 1440/768/390/320 with 144 diagrams, 924 assignment paths, 128 fiber selections and 160 extension rows, plus 12 trusted completed downloads independently accepted by the native CLI. Scope is at most five variables, ten clauses, ten signed literals per clause, exact nonnegative rational literal weights with numerator/denominator at most 1,000 and a full permutation atlas of at most 120 orders; ordinary noncomplemented ROBDDs only. Ordered reduction, fixed-order canonicity and order sensitivity are credited to Bryant 1986, with Darwiche/Marquis 2002 placing BDDs within knowledge compilation and Bryant/Tan/Heule 2025 certifying projected compilation with formally verified checkers — this bounded replay claims none of those guarantees, and no new Boolean algorithm, formal proof, probability calibration or real-agent benchmark.
  - Case study: [compilelab.md](compilelab.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/compilelab](https://github.com/midya8/compilelab)
  - Live: [Exact Boolean knowledge-compilation workbench](https://midya8.github.io/compilelab/)
  - [Sources and prior work](https://github.com/midya8/compilelab/blob/main/SOURCES.md) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/compilelab/pull/1)

## Python utilities

Dependency-free, standard-library-only tools: a CLI for day-to-day use plus an importable library for automation, backed by unit tests and CI.

- **sre-toolkit** — Error-budget and burn-rate math, multi-window burn-rate alerting, rule-based alert triage, and Markdown SLO definitions, status reports, and postmortems. 254 unit tests.
  - Case study: [sre-toolkit.md](sre-toolkit.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/sre-toolkit](https://github.com/midya8/sre-toolkit)
- **loglens** — Access-log analysis for incident work: error rates, failing paths, 5xx sources, latency percentiles, incident timelines, and Markdown incident reports. Parses nginx/Apache combined and JSON logs. 241 unit tests.
  - Case study: [loglens.md](loglens.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/loglens](https://github.com/midya8/loglens)
- **flowkit** — Workflow runner: JSON-defined DAGs of shell commands with retries and exponential backoff, per-attempt timeouts, fail-fast or continue-on-error semantics, parallel scheduling, conditional steps, end-phase hooks, dry-run plans, and text/JSON run reports. Fail-fast stops scheduling new normal steps but lets running steps finish, and `continue_on_error` permits independent work to proceed while dependents of the failed step still skip — and a failed command may already have changed files or external state, because retries and timeouts are execution controls, not a rollback or transaction system, and a timeout consumes an attempt. End-phase hooks still obey selection, conditions and hook dependencies, so `finally` is not a guarantee that a cleanup command executes, and a failed or skipped hook skips its own dependents without stopping unrelated hooks. `--dry-run` lists selected step names, dependencies, conditions and hook gates without executing anything, but it does not show expanded shell commands or prove that commands are safe, and environment values are not shell-escaped — unset references without defaults expand to empty strings unless `--strict-env` rejects them first. The nightly-ETL example is a template, not a runnable flow: its scripts are not included, and its cleanup command deletes the configured scratch directory. Standard library only, with a 123-test suite covering flow validation, ordering, retries, timeouts, fail-fast and skip behavior, hook gates and dependencies, conditions, selection, environment interpolation, output capture and CLI exit codes (0 for success or a valid dry-run, 1 for step failure, 2 for invalid input or a pre-run/report-writing error).
  - Case study: [flowkit.md](flowkit.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/flowkit](https://github.com/midya8/flowkit)
- **uptimekit** — Synthetic uptime monitoring and availability reporting in pure Python, no dependencies and no database: HTTP, TCP connect, and TLS certificate-expiry probes, an append-only JSONL history, availability roll-ups with latency percentiles, incident detection with a consecutive-failure flap guard and a sustained-outage gate, SLO measurement (error budget consumed, met-or-breached status, --fail-slo exits 1 on a breach), a watch mode for hosts without cron, and a self-contained HTML status page. 98 unit tests running fully offline, CI on Python 3.11.
  - Case study: [uptimekit.md](uptimekit.md)
  - Repo: [midya8/uptimekit](https://github.com/midya8/uptimekit)


### CutLab response-path extension

[Open the exact response-path inspector](https://midya8.github.io/cutlab/path.html) · [Source and method](https://github.com/midya8/cutlab/blob/main/PATH.md)

Vary binary unary costs with a rational parameter and retain the complete optimum family on every closed region, open cell and breakpoint, including point-only optima and persistent ties. Every assignment keeps its affine line and every line comparison keeps its exact inequality. Independent graph-cut audits check all boundary and cell witnesses.

18 synthetic path controls retain 214 assignment lines, 6,084 inequalities and 92 full flow/cut witnesses at boundary points or cell midpoints. Mixed-sign slopes include a label that leaves and returns: no nested-label or scalable parametric-maxflow guarantee is claimed. The inspector enumerates all 2^n assignments for at most six variables. Original flow/cut functionality and tests remain.

The default path gives the numbers: open-cell masks 7, 0 and 1 with breakpoints 1/4 and 2. Path checks include 120 independent path oracles, 50 endpoint-inequality audits, 40 objective transformations, 40 parameter reversals and 60 concave active-slope checks; the 18 full Python/BigInt path reports agree on 90,732 exact scalar leaves, and Chrome checks all 214 lines, 6,084 inequality rows and 92 point/cell summaries with flow reports, region tables and envelope geometry. A midpoint solve alone would not establish an entire region; the complete inequality intersection does.

18 original synthetic controls retain 827 subsets, 1,301 search nodes, 20 cores and 52 repairs. 17 Python tests on 3.10-3.13, four Node/Chrome tests, 74,292 exact cross-language scalar checks and installed-wheel reproduction. Exhaustive six-variable/eight-soft-clause bound, established SAT theory, no scalable solver, real fault diagnosis or certified proof claim. No model/API.

## ArgumentLab: defense, not a vote

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/argumentlab) · [Live](https://midya8.github.io/argumentlab/) · [Case study](argumentlab.md)

Nine exact abstract-argumentation families, every defense witness, complete labellings and grounded steps. Independent Python/JS full-report checks over 531 small directed graphs. Version 0.3 adds SCC-recursive CF2/stage2: every induced graph, external component-defeat edge, restricted child and recursive membership obligation, checked on 725 frameworks with Python/JavaScript agreement on 3,052 induced subproblems. Eight arguments maximum, established semantics, explicit classical empty-family vacuity. No real-world truth or scalable-solver claim.

### ArgumentLab SCC recursion

[Live SCC inspector](https://midya8.github.io/argumentlab/scc.html) · [Source and exact method](https://github.com/midya8/argumentlab/blob/main/SCC.md) · [Verified PR3](https://github.com/midya8/argumentlab/pull/3)

Version 0.3 makes established SCC-recursive CF2/stage2 semantics inspectable. Every reached induced graph retains every candidate, external component-defeat edge, restricted child and recursive membership obligation; the child drill preserves selected arguments and SCCs are recomputed after deletion. The default cascade splits one component into several and then deletes again. A six-cycle separates maximal conflict-free sets from maximal ranges; global stage and stage2 are kept distinct. Independent set/reachability reconstruction checks every evidence record; Python/JavaScript full reports agree on 725 frameworks, 3,052 induced subproblems, 16,375 candidates and 38,184 obligations, with 39 Python tests and 15 Node/Chrome tests. Six arguments, exhaustive enumeration, supplied attacks as assumptions; no new theorem, scalable solver, human persuasion result or universal ranking of semantics.


### ArgumentLab ideal and eager cores

[Live ideal inspector](https://midya8.github.io/argumentlab/ideal.html) · [Method and inspected foundations](https://github.com/midya8/argumentlab/blob/main/IDEAL.md) · [Merged PR4](https://github.com/midya8/argumentlab/pull/4)

Version 0.4 computes the greatest admissible subset of the intersection of all preferred (ideal) or semi-stable (eager) extensions by simultaneous pruning on the original graph. The default eight-argument control cascades {a3,a4,a7} to {a3,a7} to {a7} in two rounds; one-pass pruning and deleting outside attackers both give the wrong answer. Native bitsets, an independent complete-ternary-labelling/set checker and JavaScript sets agree on 645 full reports, and PyArg 2.0.2 matches 1,290 ideal/eager families. The release has 53 Python tests and 18 Node tests, with 377 rejected primitive-leaf mutations and 20 selected forged CLI rejections; live Chrome checks 42 reports, 84 core views, 117 trace states and 4,266 candidate selections at 1280/390/320. Established finite ideal/eager semantics for 0–8 supplied arguments; no truth scoring, persuasion, scalable solver or infinite-framework eager-uniqueness claim.


### HornLab: a circle is not a proof

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/hornlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/hornlab) · [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/hornlab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Bounded positive propositional Horn inference with synchronous derivation rounds and every indexed rule decision. Compare the least model with every classical and supported model: a supported circular assignment need not have a derivation. Inspect an atomic countermodel for each non-entailed query and every inclusion-minimal initial-fact support, including its deletion failures and all seed subsets. Not entailed does not mean classically false.

Separate native set/truth-table checkers replay the full reports without calling the generators — inference checks model intersection and all proper seed subsets — and Python/JavaScript parity is a separate check, not a replacement for independent verification. The current checkout passes 26 Python and eight Node/Chrome tests, with native coverage of all 1,024 two-atom program/initial-fact combinations, all 4,096 two-atom program/old-fact/new-fact transitions and seeded cases. Six atoms/eight rules, no negation or variables; established semantics, not a new algorithm or production rule engine.

Case study: [hornlab.md](hornlab.md)

[Rule-deletion inspector](https://midya8.github.io/hornlab/rules.html) · [Method and primary sources](https://github.com/midya8/hornlab/blob/main/RULES.md) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/hornlab)

Keep initial facts fixed and inspect all indexed-rule subsets, synchronous closures, every minimal rule support and every minimal deletion set. One broken route need not break the goal. Each support retains one-rule removal failures; each deletion retains restoration successes. Both minimal-hitting-set dualities and every support/deletion intersection are explicit, including empty-support and already-absent boundary cases.

Deployed Chrome checks all three inspectors at 320/390/1280, and the rule controls expose 305 subsets, 35 queries, 36 supports, 27 deletion sets and 36 intersections. A separate native set/truth-table checker reconstructs the complete rule lattice without calling the generator. Installed-wheel checks reproduce ten inference, eleven rule-audit and twelve update controls outside the checkout through both entry points, including forged-report rejection, and optional clingo 5.8.3 agrees on 11,381 positive-program stable families across 4,458 requests and 6,923 transactions. Established bounded monotone explanation, not causal attribution, ontology translation, a new algorithm or formal proof.


### BisimLab: same traces, different choices

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bisimlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab) · [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Live divergence inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bisimlab/divergence.html)

Finite agent-protocol equivalence from scratch: every strong-bisimulation refinement, every failed left/right step match, executable Hennessy-Milner witness DAGs, truth sets and a quotient graph. The default original control has equal finite trace languages but loses a choice after the same action — equal traces do not imply the same branching behaviour — so exact paired-subset BFS checks trace equality separately from branching equivalence. One to six states, all labels visible: no silent abstraction, probabilities, policy guarantee, source extraction, new algorithm or formal-proof claim.

Nine native tests include all 512 three-state/one-action graphs checked against relation enumeration and 100 seeded graphs; a native partition-signature checker never calls the generator, and independent JavaScript matches full reports. Three Node/actual-Chrome tests include 593 complete cross-engine reports and every control record at 320/390/1280. Installed-wheel checks cover both CLI entry points outside the checkout. Established theory and seven inspected sources are attributed in the method.

#### BisimLab: the loop must stay inside

[Live divergence inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bisimlab/divergence.html) · [Source and exact contract](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/blob/main/DIVERGENCE.md) · [Reviewed change](https://github.com/midya8/bisimlab/pull/4)

Two protocol states can both loop forever and still disagree about where that loop is possible. The explicit-divergence inspector compares ordinary branching equivalence with unrooted divergence-preserving branching equivalence, enumerating every set partition of a supplied finite protocol and retaining every state's internal reachability, complete exit signatures, canonical nonempty-cycle lassos and every failed same-block obligation, then checking the coarsest valid partition explicitly. The opening six-state example reconstructs and credits Luttik2020 Example3/Figure1: both states are globally divergent, yet only one can diverge while staying in the class where the visible action remains available. A global cycle flag would miss the distinction.

Independent checks include a simple-path/block-insertion verifier without generator imports, a direct relational attack/response oracle with matrix cycle closure, 220 individually rejected non-input leaf mutations, and 1,157 complete Python/JavaScript atlases containing 8,720 partitions, 37,743 state records and 19,346 failure records. Actual unmodified pinned mCRL2 agrees on 353 frameworks / 3,672 unordered-pair-semantics comparisons. All 32 Python tests and 12 Node/Chrome tests pass; a fresh isolated wheel retains all prior controls/entrypoints, reproduces 13 new controls through both new entrypoints, checks three new module origins and rejects 26 forged reports. Actual Chrome checks 39 control-width reports at 1280/390/320, all 843 candidate selections, 4,467 state rows, 2,955 failure rows and 13 trusted completed JSON downloads verified by the native checker, with 47 complete unmasked visual states matching the reviewed implementation after deployment. Bounds: 1..6 states, 0..3 labels and 18 unique edges, at most 203 partitions; exact bounded educational model checking, not a scalable verification engine, learned model, benchmark, formal proof or agent safety/liveness guarantee.


### ShieldLab: a safe next step can still lose

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab) · [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/METHOD.md)

Exact tiny safety-game inspector for hand-written agent protocols: every synchronous fixed-point obligation, winning controller move, losing rank and branching invariant/counterstrategy graph is visible. Changing only who controls a state flips the result — a one-step-safe action need not preserve safety forever — so the inspector exposes both quantifiers and every round rather than presenting a single attractive trajectory. Winning proof graphs fix a controller policy and retain every environment move; losing proof graphs fix a rank-decreasing environment counterpolicy and retain every controller move, stopping at unsafe states. A five-step forced chain, safe environment cycle, indefinite waiting and an already-unsafe root make the boundaries explicit.

Eight native tests include all 21,952 three-state graph/ownership/safety combinations and 150 seeded larger arenas. Independent bitmask Python and explicit-set JavaScript, plus a separate dual-attractor and exhaustive positional-policy checker. Three Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 643 complete reports and all six controls at 320/390/1280. Installed-wheel checks replay both CLI entry points outside the checkout. Finite total perfect-information model only: no runtime shield, LLM guarantee, liveness, timing, probability, new algorithm or formal proof; dead ends are rejected, and safe waiting need not mean progress. Established safety-game theory is attributed in the method; tests are evidence rather than proof.

ShieldLab 0.2 separates safe waiting from forced arrival. The finite reach-before-unsafe inspector exposes every synchronous attractor obligation, the exact minimum worst-case first-goal bound and a complete ranked strategy or target-avoiding counterstrategy: a winning successor can still stall, while a slower fixed policy can still win. All 24 native tests pass on Python 3.10-3.13, preserving all 8 original tests; independent strategy-pair simulation checks 324 two-state and 32,928 three-state cases, and six Node/Chrome tests compare 457 complete native/JavaScript reports, 1,329 policies and 6,291 root outcomes. Actual Chrome drills every policy/root across 12 controls at 320/390/1280. Terminal safe goals stop the play on first hit; empty targets lose; no fairness assumption forces the environment to cooperate. [Live first-goal inspector](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/reachavoid.html) · [Contract and eight-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/REACHAVOID.md).

ShieldLab 0.3 asks whether a target is visited again, not just once. The separate safe-recurrence inspector checks that a supplied finite game stays safe forever and visits a target infinitely often against every environment choice, with targets live rather than terminal. Every nested greatest/least fixed-point round and successor obligation is exposed, with both players' complete positional-policy atlases and canonical labeled unsafe or nonempty-cycle failure witnesses; one favorable path or target-containing SCC is not a recurrence guarantee. Reproduced from the exact public source: 39 Python tests, all 74,088 three-state graph/owner/safety-target configurations, 498 complete native/JavaScript/checker reports and 163 individually rejected primitive-leaf mutations; actual Chrome at 1280/390/320 checks 36 complete control reports and both policy atlases. Bounds: six states, three moves per state and at most 729 positional assignments per player. [Inspect safe recurrence](https://midya8.github.io/shieldlab/recurrence.html) · [Exact method and inspected sources](https://github.com/midya8/shieldlab/blob/main/RECURRENCE.md).

Case study: [shieldlab.md](shieldlab.md)


### AbsorbLab: inspect the chain, not only the answer

[First hit](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/) · [Strong lumping](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/lumping.html) · [Row response](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/response.html) · [Source and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/absorblab)

The row-response control keeps eventual success at one while a goal edge remains positive, then drops to zero when the last exit disappears. The inspector separates the interior limit from each endpoint's actual support and direct solve. A second control has a curved rational response, and success-conditioned traffic counts visits and directed edges.

The first-hit inspector makes global path selection visible: equal direct/risky branches become 4/5 and 1/5 given eventual success, which local failure-edge removal does not explain. The strong-lumping inspector shows why equal immediate goal mass can hide a later split, and checks every partition and quotient obligation against the original transition matrix.

The current suite has 42 Python tests and 11 Node tests, including actual Chrome for all three inspectors. Full native/browser comparisons cover 213 first-hit, 527 lumping and 146 response reports; each workflow checks twelve trusted completed downloads by native replay. Installed wheels test all three workflows outside the checkout. Optional SymPy references check different numerical or algebraic claims; their support and independence limits are spelled out in the case study. These are finite descriptive checks, not performance benchmarks or proofs.

The chains are supplied, bounded to six states and use exact rational arithmetic. Conditioning is selection, not causal intervention. Strong lumping concerns the original transition matrix; response endpoints are separate chains. Undefined conditionals remain undefined, and the established math is credited rather than claimed as new theory.

Case study: [absorblab.md](absorblab.md)


### AbsorbLab: the last exit changes everything

[Live row-response inspector](https://midya8.github.io/absorblab/response.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/absorblab) - [Derivation and seven-source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/absorblab/blob/main/RESPONSE.md)

AbsorbLab's 0.3 extension moves probability mass between two destinations of one nonterminal row and watches the whole exact rational response, not just a sensitivity estimate. In the default original control, success stays at one while the last exit gets arbitrarily unlikely — then drops to zero the moment that exit disappears. The inspector keeps those two facts apart: the interior limit as the exit vanishes, and each endpoint's actual support with its own direct solve. A retry control makes the shape concrete: success is `(1/4+t)/(1/2+t)`, first derivative one and second derivative minus four at zero. And success-conditioned traffic now covers visits, every directed edge count and timing, so you can see where the successful paths actually go.

All 42 Python and eleven Node tests retain both previous workflows. Native rank-one generation, independent Cramer/cofactor replay and BigInt recomputation agree on 146 full reports, 1,909 samples, 61,255 conditional edge cells and 15,435 derivative pairs, and 24 CLI and 24 direct-checker forgeries are rejected. Actual Chrome checks 1,293 root/sample views at 1280/390/320 pixels, twelve trusted completed downloads with native replay and twelve extra dense six-state views with long fractions and terminal roots. Fresh installed wheels check fourteen module origins, both response entrypoints and 24 direct plus 24 CLI forgeries with atomic preservation. The invoked SymPy 1.14.0 reference checks 928 symbolic success/failure curves and 10,290 derivative pairs, with support verified separately. The open transfer interval has fixed positive support and each endpoint is a separate chain; zero-success conditioning is undefined, terminal visits and outgoing edges are excluded, and restricted visits do not assert finite lifetime in closed nonterminal classes. The plot joins sampled points — the displayed rational formula defines the full interval. These are supplied one-to-six-state chains and established finite Markov, Doob-transform and rank-one inverse methods, not learned behavior, causal intervention, new probability theory, general parameter synthesis, a scalable solver or a guarantee about real AI agents.

Distinct from 'A tangent is not a map', DenoiseLab's principle, which is about a local linear approximation against the exact global partition — here the retry control's derivatives (one and minus four at zero) are the local picture, and the inspector shows the whole rational response over the interval instead of stopping at the tangent; and from 'A tight bound is not an attained bound', STNLab's principle, which is about a computed bound needing a schedule that attains it — here the interior limit is not a bound waiting for a witness: success stays one for every positive exit mass and is zero when the exit is gone, so the limit is a statement about approaching, and each endpoint gets its own chain and its own solve.

### BranchLab: always possible is not inevitable

[Live workbench](https://midya8.github.io/branchlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab) · [Method and proof limits](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Live fairness inspector](https://midya8.github.io/branchlab/fair.html)

Explicit finite CTL inspector for branching-time reasoning about supplied models: in the default, AG EF p holds while AF p fails — a goal remains reachable everywhere, but an infinite wait is still allowed. Twelve original controls separate nested path quantifiers, different existential witnesses, strong-until failure, time-zero success and finite versus lasso counterexamples, and no fairness assumption silently removes an inconvenient branch. Python and browser JavaScript expose every unique subformula's truth set, synchronous least/greatest rounds, all-state obligations and the complete 2^n subset-image atlas; a separate native checker derives truth by enumerating all fixed points and independently enumerates finite prefixes and first-repeat lassos.

Fifteen native tests run on Python 3.10–3.13 with four outside-checkout installed-wheel checks; all 144 two-state graph/labelling cases pass and a direct path/lasso oracle covers 2,744 three-state cases. Genuine pyModelChecking 1.3.4 agreement covers 212 requests and 3,630 subformula truth sets, and complete browser/native records agree on 276 requests; actual Chrome checks 36 control/width reports, 192 subformulas, 558 state selections, 237 rounds, 1,110 subset rows and 12 completed exports replayed natively. Classical bounded CTL, not symbolic scale, LTL/CTL*, verified AI agents or new theory; runtime needs no paid API or external package, and the optional GPL test reference is not vendored.

BranchLab 0.2 asks which infinite paths an assumption really leaves. The separate state-justice fair-CTL inspector compares ordinary and fair truth at every subformula, exposes every induced region and its recurrent components, and shows all justice hits, backward rounds and a replayable finite-prefix/closed-walk witness. Its original four-state hub needs the repeated walk s0 → s1 → s0 → s2 → s0 to meet both justice constraints; neither first simple cycle is enough. A trap can still satisfy a local atom while having no fair infinite continuation. Twenty-nine Python tests and seven Node tests pass, retaining the original ordinary-CTL workflows. Native Tarjan generation, an independent Boolean-closure/recurrent-subset checker and a separately written browser Kosaraju engine agree on 278 full reports, 2,808 region records, 4,135 subformulas and 2,643 fair lassos; 519 changed noninput primitive leaves are rejected. Actual NetworkX 3.4.2 checks all regions, 4,632 components and all lassos as a graph reference, not a full fair-CTL oracle. The existing actual pyModelChecking 1.3.4 ordinary reference still matches 212 requests and 3,630 subformula sets; its fairness route is explicitly excluded after three reproducible disagreements. Real Chrome exercises 36 fair reports at 1280/390/320 pixels, 216 regions, 612 region/start selections, 465 subformula selections, 390 witness rows and 2,628 graph edges, with twelve trusted downloads independently replayed. Fresh installed wheels confirm nine module origins, twelve fair controls through both entrypoints, 24 forged CLI atomic-preservation checks and 24 direct forgeries, retaining ordinary installation checks. All 46 exact public files, feature/PR and main CI, Pages, deployed assets, behavior and 43 unmasked screenshot comparisons were verified. Bounded explicit checking for supplied total graphs of at most six states and four state-based justice sets; every constraint must recur infinitely often on the same path. Fair EG searches inside the operand region before checking recurrence; intersecting ordinary EG with globally fair states is not sound. An empty justice set excludes every path, while no justice constraints recovers ordinary CTL. These are generalized-Büchi execution assumptions, not strong/compassion fairness, an implemented scheduler, new model-checking theory, a scalable symbolic solver or a guarantee about real AI agents. [Fairness method and checks](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab/blob/main/FAIRNESS.md) · [Pull request 2](https://github.com/midya8/branchlab/pull/2).

### ProvLab: one fact is not two chances
 · [Live chase workbench](https://midya8.github.io/provlab/chase.html)
[Live workbench](https://midya8.github.io/provlab/) · [Source and tests](https://github.com/midya8/provlab) · [Method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Implementation PR](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/pull/1) · [Live containment lab](https://midya8.github.io/provlab/containment.html)

A finite relational-provenance workbench for symbolic data reasoning: shared query variables perform real joins, and reusing a token is one event, not a fresh independent draw. The default query derives `answer(amy)` through `R(x) ∧ R(x)` or `R(x) ∧ S(x)`, with provenance `p² + pq` — bag multiplicity `10` at `p=2, q=3`, and exact answer-presence probability `1/2` at `P(p)=1/2, P(q)=1/4`, not the ordinary substitution `3/8`, because the first route already succeeds whenever `p` exists and the second adds no new successful worlds. Fourteen original controls cover token reuse, duplicate union-all branches, overlapping events, projection collisions, join chains, repeated variables, tagged constants, empty answers, Boolean heads, bag zero, probability 0/1, triple reuse and an empty relation.

A separate native verifier imports no join generator: it enumerates active-domain valuations and re-queries possible databases, and Shannon recursion checks the marginal independently of the generator's world-mass sum. SQLite token-carrying joins feed actual SymPy 1.14.0 polynomial checks over 164 requests, 3,062 possible-database queries and 233 polynomial outputs. Twenty-one native tests run on Python 3.10–3.13, including 64 exhaustive two-value cases, 150 seeded queries and all 93 non-input primitive mutations of the default report; browser/native parity compares 164 full reports and 6,310 output/world records, and actual Chrome runs all 14 controls at 1280, 390 and 320px with 14 trusted certificate downloads. Classical provenance semirings and tuple-independent base-event semantics, not new theory, arbitrary SQL, scalable inference, calibrated probabilities or a guarantee about an AI agent.


ProvLab 0.2 compares safe positive conjunctive queries over every finite database under set semantics: a head-preserving reverse homomorphism proves containment, while a failed direction produces a concrete frozen canonical database and a missing output tuple. The inspector exposes every variable-map candidate, its atom images and ordered head constraints, both canonical evaluations, and every safe atom-subset minimum; constants stay fixed, Boolean heads and ternary relations are explicit, and repeated atom occurrences remain visible. The default repeated-fact rewrite preserves the answer set but changes a multiplicity probe from 4 to 2 and natural provenance from f0² to f0 — set-equivalent is not bag-equivalent. Thirty-two Python tests include 235 single-field corruption rejections and 80 direct finite-instance checks; 112 cross-language comparisons cover 17,403 candidate maps and 1,662 subset rows; actual SQLite DISTINCT/ALL/token joins ran 1,344 queries and SymPy checked 362 polynomials built from those token rows. Bounded single-CQ syntax under set semantics; classical Chandra-Merlin query-containment and core theory, not new theory, a bag-equivalence decision procedure, arbitrary SQL optimization, a language model, or a verified-AI-agent guarantee. [Containment method and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/blob/main/CONTAINMENT.md) · [Pull request 2](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/pull/2).

ProvLab 0.3 adds constraint-aware containment under full single-head rules and relational keys: a finite chase makes query rewrites inspectable — full rules add facts, keys merge query symbols in both the database and the ordered output tuple — with both containment directions, every chase step, terminal dependency checks, source derivations and legal saturated counterexamples visible. A clash between distinct literals is labeled as target-query unsatisfiability and vacuous containment, not a successful nonempty rewrite. Every dependency subset is tested: the default needs a key plus a diagonal rule, neither alone suffices, and a symmetry rule is irrelevant; another control separates a one-rule minimum support from a two-rule inclusion-minimal support. Forty-four Python tests, 114 complete native/JavaScript reports, an independent ground replay, 344 primitive evidence mutations rejected, actual SQLite dependency/query checks and installed-wheel reproduction; actual Chrome checks 14 controls at 1280/390/320px, both directions, 171 trace states, 141 subset selections and 14 trusted certificate downloads replayed by Python. Bounded classical database theory, not existential chasing, arbitrary EGDs, SQL NULL, bag-preserving optimization or global query minimization; the original provenance and unconstrained-containment labs remain available. [Chase method and checks](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/blob/main/CHASE.md) · [Pull request 3](https://github.com/midya8/provlab/pull/3).
