### ChainLab: the question comes before the path

[Prefix inspector](https://midya8.github.io/chainlab/) · [Path workbench](https://midya8.github.io/chainlab/paths.html) · [Source and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/chainlab)

MAP minimizes whole-path 0/1 loss, not expected wrong positions. The rational control gives different supported MAP, expected-matches and product-marginal paths; its unconstrained state modes form an impossible string. The supplied model's numbers make the trade concrete: MAP's B/C/B/C/B carries posterior 0.05823316 with 2.880904 expected wrong positions, supported expected matches B/A/C/A/C carries 0.05686832 with 2.531237, and supported product marginals B/C/B/A/C carries 0.03639572 with 2.535156. The unconstrained modes' B/C/C/A/C would have only 2.456165 expected wrong positions, but it is unsupported, with posterior 0, so it is not a choice at all. That model is the first qualifying one from a disclosed deterministic search, seed 314159, candidate 1904, picked to show disagreement rather than sampled as a typical case. Locked and independent controls then hold state marginals fixed while whole-path entropy changes from ln(2) to 5 ln(2). These are supplied-model decision and dependence examples, not real-state accuracy or calibration results.

The prefix inspector makes the evidence boundary visible: smoothing and Viterbi use the displayed window, while filtering and forecasts use only the appropriate prefix. The original floating trainer, synthetic data, initialization, training-only iid control, generating reference and test scores remain unchanged. Browser edits do not retrain or become new benchmarks.

The suite has 58 Python and 10 Node tests, including actual Chrome for both inspectors. Exact rational reports have independent tiny exhaustive references and a standalone verifier; entropy remains approximate with a disclosed Decimal80 tolerance. Installed replay checks ten modules, original/evidence commands, both path commands and thirteen rational controls outside the checkout. Bounded inputs, rational-work refusals, browser-scaling limits, different tie rules and rounded-model provenance are explicit in the case study.

### SubgameLab: a threat is not a credible plan

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/subgamelab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/subgamelab) - [Reviewed implementation](https://github.com/midya8/subgamelab/pull/1) - [Exact contract](https://github.com/midya8/subgamelab/blob/main/CONTRACT.md)

SubgameLab's developed default is the old entry-deterrence shape, and it is where the distinction bites: P1 stopping pays (1, 4); entering lets P2 choose (-1, 0) or (3, 2). Complete plans 00 and 11 are both pure Nash — P2's unreached fight cannot improve its root payoff — but only 11 is subgame perfect, because if P2's node is actually reached, switching gains 2. The threat "I will fight" is stable at the root precisely because it never has to be carried out; credibility is checked where the node is reached, not where the game starts. The same-player control shows the gap from the other side: P1 gains 1 by stopping at the root, 0 by entering then stopping, and 3 by entering then continuing, so plan 00 survives changing either node alone yet a joint two-node change gains 2 and it is not Nash — the browser calls the weaker property root one-node stability rather than letting it borrow the Nash name. And a chance control reaches terminal (2, 2) with probability 1 while its probability-0 branch still lets P2 choose (0, 0) or (0, 5): both complete plans are root Nash with identical root outcomes, and only the latter is subgame perfect. Zero reach does not erase the subgame, because subtree values are evaluated from the subtree root with no division by its zero reach probability. Ties need whole continuation plans too: when both P2 actions pay P2 3 while P1 receives 1 or 4, and P1's outside option is 2, the retained continuation decides whether P1 should stop or enter, and both subgame-perfect plans survive; the all-zero three-decision control retains all 8 complete plans.

The evidence is checked without trusting the producer. Backward induction combines every pair of child-subgame equilibrium plans before selecting every maximizing current action and never takes coordinatewise maxima from incompatible continuation plans. A separate verifier imports validation only: it enumerates terminal paths, multiplies chance/choice indicators, sums exact terminal payoffs and enumerates every full unilateral assignment in every subtree, and strict recursive comparison rejects extra, missing, reordered, mistyped or forged evidence. The JavaScript engine uses BigInt rational arithmetic and matches every complete report; its browser replay uses the same engine and is explicitly not an independent verifier. Actual Nashpy 0.0.43 builds a fresh normal-form matrix for each subtree directly from input paths and checks every pure strategy pair — 533 input-built subtree matrices and 2,353 pure-pair checks covering all 1,233 profile classifications — with exact rational entries scaled to integers, the largest absolute scaled entry 18 and every nonzero payoff difference at least 1; Nashpy uses NumPy isclose, so this is supplemental numerical reference evidence, not exact certification, and Gambit was researched but its local build did not finish, so no Gambit validation is claimed. 14 Python tests include an exhaustive class of all 2,916 two-decision games, and 65 complete Python/BigInt reports span ten developed controls and 55 seeded trees, retaining 1,233 profiles, 12,820 subgame/player audits and 48,250 full-player alternatives; both engines reject 317 noninput forgeries each; the installed wheel replays five site-packages module origins, ten controls, two entrypoints and 20 CLI forgery failures; actual Chrome checks 40 views at 1440/768/390/320 with 388 plan selections, 1,209 continuation selections and 2,837 alternative rows. The scope stays narrow — two players, at most six binary player decisions, two binary chance nodes and 17 nodes, complete pure contingent plans with singleton information sets and exact canonical rational inputs with numerator/denominator at most 1,000 — with no hidden information, mixed-equilibrium enumeration, imperfect recall, CFR, learning, preference inference, formal verification, new theorem, scalable solver or real-market advice. Subgame perfection is classical Selten theory, not our invention.

Case study: [subgamelab.md](subgamelab.md)
### TransportLab: a requested marginal is not an actual marginal

[Open the browser workbench](https://midya8.github.io/transportlab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/transportlab) - [Derivation and limits](https://github.com/midya8/transportlab/blob/main/MARGINALS.md)

TransportLab's browser normalizes the raw source and target weights separately, differentiates that normalization, and then follows exactly N row/column updates - so the direction the solver sees is not the direction the user typed. On the early-weight control, one update gives a full objective tangent of 0.010665526, while a candidate built from the requested marginals gives -0.019162331: not just a different size, a different sign. The gap is exactly the actual marginal-tangent residual terms the candidate drops - the plan's row and column masses move as the weights move, and the requested marginals never see that. A normalization-only control makes the same point from the other side: its raw directions are nonzero, yet the normalized and plan tangents are zero, so a direction that only rescales the weights changes nothing. Null sensitivity does not establish feasibility.

The evidence is checked without trusting the producer. The suite now has 62 Python tests and six Node/actual-Chrome tests, including the original 46 Python tests. Independent Decimal80 positive-kernel finite differences check 33 control snapshots and 60 generated rectangular cases; native analytic tangents and independent browser scalar dual numbers agree on full reports for all 12 controls plus 80 generated cases. Actual Chrome checks every supplied history step, responsive editing and two real JSON downloads replayed natively, and an installed-wheel check repeats the old demo and fixed-weight sensitivity outputs plus all new controls outside the source tree. The separate API accepts 1-4 weights per side, fixed support and 1-500 updates, with positive raw weights at least 1e-4, costs and raw weights in [0,100], epsilon in [0.01,100] and directions in [-100,100]; it does not change the earlier solver's bounds, and it does not differentiate adaptive stopping, rounding or changing support.

Case study: [transportlab.md](transportlab.md)
### AlignLab: same preferences, different policies

[Preference inspector](https://midya8.github.io/alignlab/identification.html) · [Original optimizer inspector](https://midya8.github.io/alignlab/) · [Source](https://github.com/midya8/alignlab) · [PR #3](https://github.com/midya8/alignlab/pull/3)

Exact population comparisons fix merit ratios inside each connected component, and nothing else: with A beating B at 2/3 and C beating D at 2/3, giving each pair an independent merit scale between 1 and 4 preserves both observed preferences while the first pair's policy mass ranges from 1/5 to 4/5 — and with supplied utility (1,0,0,0), expected utility ranges from 2/15 to 8/15. The comparisons are exact fractions, not estimates, and they still leave the policy open. Adding the single exact population comparison p(B > C)=1/2 joins the graph and fixes the policy up to a common scale; one noisy win would not do that, which is why the inspector takes population probabilities, not finite win counts. A contradictory input returns a closed cycle whose odds product differs from 1, with no policy family or feasible scale dimension at all — the data can refuse the model, not just underdetermine it.

The enumeration is complete for the reported objectives, not sampled: with at most six components there are at most 64 corners, and holding all but one scale fixed leaves an objective of the form (a*s+b)/(c*s+d) with a positive denominator, whose derivative cannot change sign — so moving that scale to an endpoint cannot worsen an extremum. The checks do not trust the producer: a separate Python reference enumerates every simple path and rebuilds the whole report, and the browser has its own BigInt fractions, JSON parser, path search and corner enumeration. 12 full controls retain 107 corner records, covering inconsistent and redundant cycles, isolated items, no edges, fixed bounds, constant utility, skew references, tiny positive 2^-128 reference mass and all 64 corners; 65 Python tests retain all 47 earlier tests, and eight Node/actual-Chrome tests retain all four earlier tests, comparing every report field, selecting all 107 corners, checking 36 layouts and verifying two actual JSON downloads. An installed wheel runs every audit/verify round trip outside the checkout and reconstructs the old learned fixture, original gates, 35 floating witnesses and all 1,005 trajectory states. The bounds are assumptions, not confidence intervals; utility is a supplied evaluation axis, not inferred human utility; beta is fixed at 1 to keep the arithmetic rational. And the separation is the point the old optimizer inspector could not draw: the noise-control policy sits about 4e-15 from its reward optimum, yet its synthetic utility is 1.8548647, below the reference policy's 2.0375 — a solved reward objective says nothing about safety or preferences beyond the supplied comparisons. The learned-reward policy is the other side of that split: it reaches utility 4.5704247 against a finite-policy optimum of about 4.5768334, on learned reward running 3.4641183 to 6.2462098. The case study owns up to one more thing: the earlier README prose was stale. It had utility 4.6154, control utility 2.5800 and KL 1.0216, against what the code actually gives — utility 4.5704247, noise-control utility 1.8548647, KL 1.0750099. The fix went into the prose, not the training: the original training, data, tests and gates were left alone, and the old/current table in FRONTIER.md keeps the discrepancy on the record instead of quietly rewriting it. Since then the upstream README has been corrected too — the November 11 plain-language review (PR #4) put the right numbers in, 4.5704 for utility, 1.8549 for the noise-control policy, KL 1.0750 — so the stale prose survives only in the old/current table, where it belongs.

Case study: [alignlab.md](alignlab.md)
### PPOlab: clipping is not a policy box

[Exact policy-face inspector](https://midya8.github.io/ppolab/clip.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/ppolab) - [Proof, schema, sources and reproduction](https://github.com/midya8/ppolab/blob/main/CLIP_FACE.md)

The usual story about PPO's clipped surrogate is that it keeps the new policy on a leash — the objective clips, so the update cannot wander far from the old policy. An exact-rational optimizer shows that story is wrong, and it does so by laying out the whole optimal policy face instead of one preferred update. Take the supplied control: old policy p=(1/10,9/10), advantages A=(9,-1), epsilon=1/5. The best clipped score is 9/25, but put an actual hard ratio box on that same objective and the optimum drops to 1/5 — a strict gap of 4/25. The clip still lets the policy move: every unconstrained optimizer has q0 in [7/25,1], exact total variation runs from 9/50 to 9/10 with both endpoints attained, and a positive exact optimal family approaches a zero-probability vertex, so old-to-new KL is unbounded without any rounded logarithms. A separate forced-zero control has no fully supported exact optimizer at all, which is a different fact from an unbounded family — an empty set is not an unbounded one. The clip changes what the objective rewards; it does not fence the policy in.

Nothing here is asserted on trust. The rational producer solves a separable concave objective with coordinate supporting-line bounds, and zero dual gap identifies every optimizer as a box-simplex face, retaining all vertices, attainable marginal ranges, nearest/farthest total-variation witnesses and every dual knot. The independent native verifier never imports the optimizer: it enumerates every clipped cell's vertices, checks the original min expression, and replays every certificate leaf, while a separately written browser BigInt engine reproduces the complete evidence. The suite now has 64 Python tests, all 50 earlier tests unchanged, and seven Node/actual-Chrome tests, all four earlier tests unchanged. The new checks cover 351 exhaustive signed-advantage reports, denominator-24 simplex controls, six-action and tiny-probability cases, permutations and positive scaling, all-leaf tampering and strict schemas, with the unchanged NumPy loss checked on correctly old-probability-weighted batches. Actual Chrome visits 135 policy selectors, 60 layouts and 2,658 exact table cells, clears stale results on trusted edits, and downloads two real JSON Blobs for native regeneration and independent replay; both the earlier and extension installed-wheel gates reproduce outside the checkout. The scope stays narrow — one supplied state with 2–6 actions and a full old-policy expectation, no empirical minibatches, shared neural parameters, entropy or value losses, dynamics, optimizer trajectories or new training performance. Closed-simplex optima can have zeros while finite softmax logits cannot, and the clipping limitation is established PPO mathematics, credited to Schulman et al., OpenAI Spinning Up and prior PPO analyses — not a new algorithm or a new failure benchmark. The lesson travels anyway: whenever a clipped objective is described as keeping a policy close, ask what the objective actually constrains, because a penalty on the score is not a bound on the policy, and the optimum can sit a long way from the old policy while the clip stays satisfied.

Case study: [ppolab.md](ppolab.md)
### CPNetLab: minimal is not minimum

[Live whole-row atlas](https://midya8.github.io/cpnetlab/scopes.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/cpnetlab) - [Contract](https://github.com/midya8/cpnetlab/blob/main/ROW-SUBSETS.md)

When a CP-net says no, the usual question is which table row to blame, and the usual answer points at one convenient row. The row-subsets atlas refuses the shortcut: it enumerates every whole conditional-row subset, not one explanation. Eight developed controls cover 865 subset graphs with actual improving paths, inconsistency cycles and compatible strict-ranking witnesses. The default 256-subset control has two minimal conflicts and six irreducible withdrawals — three remove one row, three remove two — so a minimal withdrawal is not a minimum one. A separate control has two-row and four-row minimal path supports: two minimal ways to keep a path, of different sizes. Minimal does not mean minimum, and neither means the right human preference to abandon.

Nothing here is asserted on trust. The independent Python checker labels Hamming-one pairs, uses Floyd-Warshall paths and checks all proper candidate subsets, reconstructing every mask and all four antichains without importing either analyzer. Whole-row withdrawal removes all justified flips, not an arbitrary single edge, and all outcomes and the supplied model remain intact. Release checks are 14 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and six combined Node/actual-Chrome tests, covering all 576 ordered two-variable query atlases (6,400 subset records), all 400 two-variable masks against every possible total order, and 91 accepted seeded row atlases, with an installed 0.2.0 wheel reproduced outside the checkout through both CLI entry points for both eight-control families and forged-evidence rejection. Actual deployed Chrome selects all 865 masks, all 46 antichain buttons, every row toggle and all eight exports, with stale/invalid/row-limit recovery and eight controls at three widths, and the original complete-model checks remain green. The scope stays bounded — at most eight rows, sixteen outcomes and 256 subsets — and this is what-if constraint evidence, not automatic preference revision: nothing here says which preference a person should give up, only which withdrawals the tables actually allow.
