### EpistemLab: the fact stays, who saw it changes

[Live event inspector](https://midya8.github.io/epistemlab/events.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/epistemlab) - [Product derivation and inspected sources](https://github.com/midya8/epistemlab/blob/main/EVENTS.md)

EpistemLab0.3 separates uncertainty about a world from uncertainty about an event. In the original read-p/read-not-p/idle control, the receiver learns p but the observer still does not know whether the receiver learned; removing idle changes that nested knowledge without making p common knowledge. A separately labeled public-announcement counterfactual would make all three queries true. The inspector keeps every world/event candidate, precondition result, inherited fact, product partition and both component conditions for every possible arrow, and every subformula, modal obligation, path and before/after pair-level change is inspectable.

Preconditions are evaluated before the event, and can become false afterward. The actual event marks a pointed pair; it does not delete alternative events, and equal preconditions do not merge copies. A nonempty global product can lack the designated pair, and then post-event focus values are null, not false or a vacuous dynamic-box verdict. Both supplied world and event relations are equivalence partitions, preserving S5 on every nonempty product.

Nothing here is asserted on trust. All 30 Python tests and thirteen Node/Chrome tests pass, retaining the original public-announcement and Boolean-disclosure workflows. Complete native, independent relation-row and browser reports agree on 648 requests, 2,552 Cartesian candidates, 5,984 product-arrow obligations and 9,208 before/after subformula sets, with 1,072 separately altered noninput primitive leaves rejected. Actual pyModelChecking 1.3.4 independently reconstructs products and checks 1,944 snapshots and 13,812 sets through 8,961 ordinary CTL calls; that is an additional implementation check, not an independent event parser or a theorem proof. Real Chrome exercises 36 control/width reports at 1280/390/320, with 129 retained-cell interactions, 255 source rows, 1,743 selected relation obligations, 783 subformula selections, 963 modal obligations, 3,219 path rows and twelve trusted completed downloads replayed natively. Fresh installed wheels verify eleven module origins and twelve event controls through two entrypoints, with 24 forged CLI atomic-output checks plus 24 direct forgeries. The scope stays bounded — four worlds, three informational events and twelve retained pairs — and this models observed sensing, or uncertainty whether a read happened, not a fully unnoticed secret where an outsider falsely excludes the actual event.

Case study: [epistemlab.md](epistemlab.md)
### VectorLite: a skipped check is not a saved cost

[Live block buckets](https://midya8.github.io/vectorlite/buckets.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/vectorlite) - [Algorithm, costs and source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/vectorlite/blob/main/BUCKETS.md)

VectorLite0.3 adds an optional block-bucket backend to the exact cosine/L2 retrieval audit. Fixed binary signatures are partitioned into r+1 disjoint blocks; for radius r, any code within r changed bits must share at least one block, so the backend unions exact-key postings and verifies full Hamming distance before the unchanged cosine/id rescoring. The original signature scan stays the default and the reference. On a constructed 512-row, 24-bit, radius-2 control the bucket path needs 3 lookups, 11 posting visits and 9 full Hamming checks against 512 scan checks. On a dense duplicate control it would visit 1,536 postings, so auto scans instead after 3 lookups.

A skipped check is not a saved cost. The candidate union is only a superset — duplicates are removed by row index and exclusions apply before the full XOR/popcount check — and the result is exact relative to the old threshold filter, not exact cosine nearest neighbours. The auto rule probes posting lengths without traversing them and scans when their sum is at least N, but it is a heuristic, not a general sublinear guarantee. Index construction still stores N times the number of blocks in posting references, and sparse query checks do not erase build, memory, union/sort or reporting costs. The counts are structural, not elapsed time, measured RAM or total operations. There is no online insertion or removal and no in-place threshold change; the CLI/browser limits are 128 bits, 1,024 rows and 128KiB, while the generic native signature API allows up to 4,096 bits with a 64-block declared cap. The r+1 exact-block method is a credited specialization of established multi-index hashing, not a new algorithm.

Nothing here is asserted on trust. 59 Python tests retain all 50 prior tests and 8 Node/actual-Chrome tests retain all 3 prior tests. Independent native and BigInt small-cube oracles each cover 5,376 query/radius/mode comparisons, and another 480 random excluded-query checks, 360 random cosine rankings and 105 original-spec backend rankings agree with scans. All 34 full reports, 1,908 selected Chrome rows, 24 responsive views and 2 trusted JSON exports are checked, and an installed 8-module wheel reproduces all 35 old audits, 34 new reports and both new CLI entrypoints. The separate BigInt browser receives only input signatures, builds its own Map postings and shows partitions, occupied keys, query bucket lengths, every checked/admitted/rejected row and per-row XOR/matching blocks; strict input rejects duplicate keys, invalid hex, unknown keys and bad exclusions, and JSON input/report exports replay native exactly, with no numeric tolerance. Source feature/PR/main checks and Pages deployment were verified before this showcase update: 46 public files and 12 live assets matched source, and 24 local/public plus 24 public/deployed unmasked pixel pairs matched exactly.

The earlier contract is retained underneath. The original 12-vector corpus, 35 reports, scan, projections and geometry browser engine are unchanged, and the combined suite has 59 tests and 8 checked module origins. The same discipline that produced the v0.2 lessons still applies: two original synthetic vectors, the same query, 8 planes, seed 0 and strict threshold give recall 1 with self included and 0 with its id excluded, and lowering the threshold creates nested candidates and cannot lower recall for a fixed query, planes, k and score/id order. Distinct from 'Keep the exact answer next to the approximation', VectorLite's first principle, which is about measuring an approximation's error against an exact baseline — here the baseline is the scan itself and the question is what the index skips and what it still pays — and from 'A self-query is not a held-out query', the second, which is about the benchmark protocol contributing to the number — here the protocol is fixed and the question is whether the faster path is actually cheaper.

Case study: [vectorlite.md](vectorlite.md)
### DenoiseLab: a tangent is not a map

[Live two-pixel map](https://midya8.github.io/denoiselab/plane.html) - [Live scalar paths](https://midya8.github.io/denoiselab/path.html) - [Live reconstruction inspector](https://midya8.github.io/denoiselab/) - [Geometry, proof and limits](https://github.com/midya8/denoiselab/blob/main/PLANE.md)

DenoiseLab started as a comparison with the controls allowed to win: on all 1,797 official UCI test digits, nearest neighbor (0.0312412) and PCA ridge (0.0317004) both beat the neural net (0.0353774) at 16 scattered missing pixels, while the network is lower at 32 and on the 4-by-4 block — no universal ranking is inferred. The question the response work then asked was different: change one observed pixel and say exactly what the retrieval does. The v0.2 answer was local — full chain-rule matrices through the three tanh layers and sigmoid for the network, an observed-only linear-system derivative for PCA — exact at its point, with saturated PCA rows at zero local response and nonconstant clipping-boundary rows left null rather than given an invented derivative, and actual edited reconstructions kept separate from tangent predictions. A derivative says what happens if you nudge one pixel a little. It says nothing about which fitting row wins one pixel-value away.

So the v0.3 scalar path and the v0.4 two-pixel map replace the local object with a global one. For a single observed-pixel edit, the complete retrieval path covers every open interval plus the complete endpoint tie, with Fraction/BigInt witnesses, a separate intended-source verifier, all-row affine dominance, and coincident rows and point-only winners visible. The two-pixel map goes further: exact rational closed minimum cells over two observed values — area, segment-only and point-only cells, with explicitly proved empty regions — and the Fraction/BigInt witnesses retain all 3,062 original fitting-row coefficients. A source-matched verifier checks supporting halfplanes, maximal lower-dimensional cells and nonnegative infeasibility combinations without rerunning clipping, and shared boundaries come from complete all-row exact tie queries rather than an interior representative. The map answers, for every possible edit, which row is retrieved; the degenerate cases are the point, not the edge case — point-only and segment-only winners exist, empty regions are proved empty rather than unsampled, and a map that omitted them would be a sample with a border drawn around it.

Nothing here is asserted on trust. The suite grows to 52 Python and fourteen Node/real-Chrome tests at v0.4, including independent boundary-pair enumeration of 238 small-source cells and 2,110 rational queries, forty complete cross-language maps, 160 browser queries and 30,720 actual canvas colors, with responsive 320/390/1280 layouts and a ten-module/nine-command installed reproduction that retains full refit and evaluation. The v0.3 suite has 44 Python and eleven Node tests covering 5,344 independent rational oracle queries, 40 complete cross-language paths, 160 browser queries and 30,720 actual canvas colors; v0.2 has 35 Python and eight Node tests covering 120 full cross-language responses, 480 browser views and 8,192 heatmap pixel checks. The map is a fixed two-coordinate slice only — not general robustness, causal recourse, uncertainty, better accuracy or binary64 tie equivalence — with the original models and scores unchanged and the derivations in RESPONSE.md and PLANE.md.

Distinct from 'A control that cannot win is not a control', DenoiseLab's first principle, which is about whether the rival methods are built strong enough to beat the model — here no rival is compared, and the question is what a local characterization does and does not tell you about the whole input plane — and from 'Score only what was missing', the second, which is about what the score counts — here the score is not in question, and the question is local versus global description of the response. The tangent is not wrong about any destination. It is only local.

Case study: [denoiselab.md](denoiselab.md)
### CalibrLab: one temperature, two outcomes

[Live score ledger](https://midya8.github.io/calibrlab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/calibrlab) - [Equations and input limits](https://github.com/midya8/calibrlab/blob/main/LEDGER.md)

CalibrLab fits one temperature on validation logits and then asks the test split whether anything improved — and the answer depends on which logits you hand it. The fixture is built to be overconfident on purpose: a one-hidden-layer MLP on six Gaussian classes in 16 dimensions, 20 training samples per class, training accuracy 1.0000 against test accuracy 0.5563. Validation NLL picks T=3.85, and the test scores move the right way across the board: ECE 0.2765 to 0.0651, MCE 0.3487 to 0.1216, Brier 0.7149 to 0.5964, NLL 2.0030 to 1.2060, accuracy unchanged. The caller example in examples/logits.json goes the other way: validation selects the upper grid boundary, T=10, and test NLL worsens from 0.0878 to 0.5762. Same procedure, opposite outcomes — neither one establishes improvement on new data. The best temperature on the test grid, 3.30 with NLL 1.1984, is computed and shown only as a reference, never a fit target.

The numbers are reported with their rounding kept visible rather than smoothed over. Subtracting the displayed rounded NLLs gives an oracle gap of 0.0076; the unchanged full-precision gap is 0.007543134492483228, which rounds to 0.0075. Fitting uses stable log-sum-exp from logits with no probability floor, so a wrong-class margin of 1000 has NLL 1000 rather than a clipped loss near 27.6, and unrepresentable losses fail explicitly instead of silently capping. The optional bootstrap gives nominal 95% percentile intervals by resampling identical test indices before and after scaling, with paired after-minus-before deltas — conditional IID test-row intervals, not guaranteed coverage and not uncertainty from fitting T or training the model. 56 Python tests retain the earlier 45, with four Node/Chrome tests checking independent native/browser arithmetic and actual UI behavior.

Distinct from 'A repair that cannot change the decision', CalibrLab's first principle, which is about what temperature scaling guarantees — positive scaling preserves argmax, so accuracy cannot move and the suite asserts the drift is zero bit-for-bit — here no guarantee is in question, and the question is whether the fitted temperature helps at all, which no guarantee answers; and from 'Fit the repair where the failure is visible', the second, which is about which split may fit T — here the split discipline is taken as given, validation the only split where the failure is both visible and usable, and the question is what the honest fit is worth once made, the 0.0076 gap to the oracle being the price of fitting honestly.

Case study: [calibrlab.md](calibrlab.md)
### RetrievalBench: every ranking is a vote

[Live exact fusion inspector](https://midya8.github.io/retrievalbench/fusion.html) - [Source and CLI](https://github.com/midya8/retrievalbench) - [Contract, bounds and inspected sources](https://github.com/midya8/retrievalbench/blob/main/FUSION.md)

RetrievalBench used to score one ranking at a time. The fusion layer lets it combine several: fuse-search runs the original exhaustive BM25 index on each query variant you supply, then merges the admitted prefixes with standard reciprocal rank fusion. Source scores never enter the fusion — only ranks do — and the arithmetic keeps every awkward case visible: a rank that is missing versus one beyond depth, a zero-weight candidate, a duplicate ranking, an exact fraction, a gap that falls inside the cutoff versus outside it. Final k stays a separate decision from source depth, exact ties break on printable ASCII document-id order, and zero-score candidates stay eligible.

Nothing here is asserted on trust. 80 Python tests retain all 68 existing tests unchanged, and seven Node/actual-Chrome tests retain all three existing tests. Independent Python Fraction and source-first BigInt arithmetic reproduce 230 complete reports — 2,006 candidate rows, 9,674 candidate/source contribution cells, 6,416 raw entries and 16 actual original-BM25 query-variant pipelines — backed by 432 exhaustive permutation/depth/constant comparisons, 200 conservation/order/weight-scaling controls and 100 random actual-BM25 pipeline comparisons. Installed-wheel checks verify nine module origins, every report and pipeline, both new CLI commands and invalid-output preservation. The browser replays supplied rankings exactly; it does not claim exact cross-language BM25 logarithms. Standard RRF is credited to Cormack, Clarke and Buettcher, SIGIR 2009 — no new method, semantic-relevance improvement, calibrated confidence, speed, causal effect or vendor parity is claimed.

Distinct from 'Measure the retrieval before you blame the answer', RetrievalBench's first principle, which is about whether the retrieval layer is measured at all before the generator is judged — here the retrieval is measured, and the question is what a second ranking adds to the first — and from 'A chunk hit is not a document hit', the second, which is about collapsing chunk hits to their parent document before counting — here nothing is collapsed, and the question is whether a repeated ranking is new evidence or the same evidence counted twice. A duplicate source adds influence, not independent evidence, and the fusion shows exactly how much.

Case study: [retrievalbench.md](retrievalbench.md)

### TokenForge: same merges, different work

[Live merge inspector](https://midya8.github.io/tokenforge/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/tokenforge) - [Exact contract and sources](https://github.com/midya8/tokenforge/blob/main/INDEXED.md)

TokenForge's optional occurrence-indexed backend trains with the exact original merges — the same 44 merges on the 1,227-byte corpus — but does the counting differently: live pair sets, linked positions and a lazy heap replace repeated recounting of the whole corpus, while the unchanged scan stays the default. The distinction that matters is frequency versus gain: five equal bytes have four adjacent matches but only two first-round replacements, so counting matches is not counting work. The work witnesses make that concrete: the original full-corpus scan visits 30,664 adjacent positions, while the index records 985 initial edges, 1,014 removals, 568 insertions, 446 snapshot positions/replacements, 652 heap pushes and 117 heap pops, 73 of them stale — different operation units, not a speedup ratio — and optional tracing copies 13,857 pair records and 82,178 token entries separately.

49 Python tests retain all 40 originals, and four Node/actual-Chrome tests check 27 full reports, 160 merge selections, 3,033 displayed pair rows, 24 responsive views and trusted exports. Independent Python and JavaScript small-row oracles each cover 2,555 settings, plus 250 native seeded multirow cases and 39 overlap/boundary cases, and an installed wheel reproduces the original and new CLI modes outside the checkout. Trusted-click downloads can be replayed through the native bounded CLI. The browser validates semantic histories, not native heap operations or Python regex parity, and the work credits established BPE, indexing and heap ideas rather than claiming an imported complexity theorem, production quality or a global compression optimum. Structural work is not a speed, RAM or complexity result. The README's encode/decode example uses actual encoded ids inside the advertised 300-token vocabulary.

Case study: [tokenforge.md](tokenforge.md)
### SetGuard: every inclusion has a witness

[Live rank ledger](https://midya8.github.io/setguard/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/setguard) - [Rank ledger equations and numerical limits](https://github.com/midya8/setguard/blob/main/LEDGER.md)

SetGuard's v0.2 ledger makes every inclusion checkable instead of asserted: five fixed public synthetic cases show pooled and classwise thresholds, every equality tie, absent-label infinity boundaries, each unlabeled candidate's inclusion or exclusion reason, 19 sampled alpha points and each held-position rank check. The rank diagnostic behind it is leave-one-out in the most literal sense — take each score position out once, threshold the others, count inclusion — and for every finite multiset the count is at least k among n+1 positions, where k=ceil((n+1)(1-alpha)). The demo's distinct-score sequence covers 19/21 positions, and the tests exhaust 243 five-position sequences with tied scores at five alpha values, so the boundary the method leans on is checked exactly where ties make it hardest to get right.

The numbers are checked, not rounded into shape: 41 Python and four Node/actual-Chrome tests check 95 case/alpha witnesses, 160 independent Fraction games, 80 integer-grid games, 4,199 pooled positions, 8,341 pooled/classwise deletion records and 2,736 candidate-label decisions. Python computes ranks as Fraction decimals; the browser independently recomputes them with BigInt rationals, and scores stay binary64 throughout. An installed wheel reproduces the original demo and all 95 witnesses outside the checkout, importing six modules and executing six CLI commands. The original three empirical arms are unchanged — 200 calibration rows (seed 11), 2,000 test rows (seed 29), target 0.9, observed coverage 0.9235 at mean set size 3.017 — and the shifted arm still drops to 0.076 coverage when truth is set to the least-probable class.

What the ledger cannot do is as explicit as what it can. The audit checks deterministic rank mechanics; it cannot establish exchangeability, training/calibration independence or a population guarantee from one dataset. Classwise mode on the same fixture makes that concrete: class counts are [43, 37, 50, 35, 35], classwise coverage is 0.9285 at mean size 3.1095 against pooled 0.9235 and 3.017, and class d's realized coverage is only 0.8859, below the 0.9 target — one finite split, which does not prove or refute the population coverage theorem and is not evidence that classwise mode always improves coverage or size. Distinct from 'A set is a coverage claim, not a probability claim', SetGuard's second principle, which is about what a prediction set promises — here the set's promise is unchanged and the question is whether each inclusion in it can be witnessed, not what the set means — and from 'A guarantee is a contract with its assumption', the first, which is about the exchangeability assumption the coverage bound rests on — here the audit is mechanical rank arithmetic that holds whether or not the assumption does, and the ledger shows the arithmetic, not the assumption — and from 'A null threshold is not a missing threshold', the third, which is about absent labels getting an infinity boundary — here the infinity boundary is one witness type among many in the ledger, not the claim itself.

Case study: [setguard.md](setguard.md)

### AttnLens: one buffer, not the whole matrix

[Online inference workbench](https://midya8.github.io/attnlens/online.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/attnlens) - [Numerical and storage contract](https://github.com/midya8/attnlens/blob/main/ONLINE.md)

AttnLens 0.3 adds an opt-in inference backend that consumes causal key blocks on the actual trained and initialized checkpoints, carrying a running maximum, normalizer and weighted-value numerator into the real head projections and residual stack — actual blockwise online-softmax inference, not a sketch of it. Independent JavaScript computes full Q/K/V, every accumulator transition and all vocabulary logits against a separately computed dense causal control, so the online path is checked against the straightforward one it replaces. Block size and forward/reverse order are editable, and downloaded inputs and full audits replay through the installed native CLI.

The point of the layer is what it refuses to build: the core observes one live score-list buffer, bounded by the selected block size, without constructing full score/probability matrices. That is a structural count, not process memory, bytes, speed or GPU IO — Q/K/V, head outputs and residual arrays remain, and the debug trace, dense control and browser deliberately add quadratic audit storage, so the arithmetic work is still quadratic. Standard online normalization and blocked attention are prior work, not a new method, FlashAttention kernel, KV cache, linear attention or training backend.

The checks are exact, not approximate: 76 Python tests retain the original 59 byte-for-byte. Independent 80-digit Decimal controls cover 648 exhaustive signed-value/ternary-score cases and 80 seeded Q/K/V sets; all 24 block sizes and both orders are checked on all 22 original checkpoint/fixture pairs, totaling 1,056 actual original-forward comparisons. Six Node/actual-Chrome tests retain all four originals, covering 106 full-model and 80 standalone reports, 5,585 query rows, 11,254 accumulator states and 775,344 numeric fields, and actual Chrome checks 66 full native reports, every displayed tensor and state over 2,112 head/query views, 198 layouts and two real downloaded files replayed natively. Screenshot comparisons do not substitute for numerical controls.

The edges are stated, not smoothed over. Binary64 block/order changes can alter rounding or near-tied predictions, and the unchanged legacy engine uses a finite -1e30 future sentinel, which can differ from exact causal skipping for extreme bounded custom weights — that counterexample is tested, and legacy error/prediction is reported separately rather than quietly rewriting old semantics. The original native modules, tests, checkpoint, intervention JavaScript/app/CSS and training remain unchanged; the original page only gains a link. Distinct from 'A visible attention pattern is not a contribution', AttnLens's second principle, which is about whether a displayed pattern measures dependence — here the online backend changes how the same attention is computed, not what it means — and from 'A weight is a measurement on a task, not an explanation', the first, which is about quoting a learned number with its task — here nothing is learned or quoted, the weights are fixed and the question is whether a blockwise pass reproduces the dense one — and from 'The loss you report and the gradient you step must be the same function', the third, which is about a training-time number disagreeing with its gradient — here the computation is inference-only and the check is forward equivalence, not a loss matching its step.

Case study: [attnlens.md](attnlens.md)

### BellmanLab: the optimum can sit between the policies

[Live occupancy inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bellmanlab/) - [Live two-budget inspector](https://midya8.github.io/bellmanlab/coupled.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/bellmanlab) - [Exact contract and source ledger](https://github.com/midya8/bellmanlab/blob/main/OCCUPANCY.md)

BellmanLab 0.4 adds one supplied expected-cost budget to the discounted MDP, and the budget can make every high-reward deterministic policy infeasible while a randomized stationary policy stays optimal. The live inspector shows the whole cost/reward polygon with its clipped feasible vertices, the exact occupancy flow, the state-conditional policy and an independently checked primal-dual certificate — the optimum is a vertex of the feasible polygon, not a policy you can name in advance. The wait/exit control makes the distinction exact: mixing two deterministic policies half-and-half produces a local exit probability of 1/3, not 1/2, because the mixture re-rolls the policy at every visit while the randomized action re-rolls only the action. A separate one-state control attains reward 1 under a budget of 1/2 while the best feasible deterministic policy earns 0, and when no policy can meet the budget the planner returns a checked minimum-cost lower bound instead of a policy that fits.

Two budgets are a different problem from one budget twice. BellmanLab 0.5 keeps both expected costs visible and enumerates the full feasible occupancy vertices, not only their two-dimensional projection: one original three-action control needs a mixture of three deterministic policies, with weights 5/12, 1/3 and 1/4, to attain reward 3/2 under ceilings 1/3 and 1/4, while the best feasible deterministic policy earns zero. A different control has ceilings that are separately attainable but jointly impossible, with a checked gap of 1/6. Visit rates reweight an initial policy mixture into local action probabilities, so a feasible reward/cost projection alone is not a full policy witness — the projection can look fine while no policy behind it exists.

The checks are exact, not approximate. The one-budget layer has 88 Python tests and four Node tests, retaining all 70 earlier tests and three reproducible SVGs: a separate checker rebuilds every report from determinant/Cramer solves and gift wrapping rather than the generator's helpers, the browser runs a separately implemented BigInt engine, and across 861 exhaustive, seeded and control inputs the complete browser reports equal native output, while 1,722 actual SciPy HiGHS LP solves agree within 1e-8. An independent full-flow vertex oracle covers 28 cases, and 169 non-input primitive-leaf forgeries are rejected. Real Chrome checks 36 reports at 1280, 390 and 320 pixels, every policy and vertex selection in those controls, 12 completed JSON downloads verified by the native checker with selected unmasked visual checks, with all five deployed assets byte-exact to merged source. The two-budget layer adds 103 Python tests and eight Node tests, with 958 rejected non-input primitive-leaf mutations and isolated installed-wheel checks of both new CLI entry points: an independent BigInt browser engine agrees on 822 complete reports, including a dense four-state case beyond JavaScript's safe integer range, and actual SciPy 1.15.3 HiGHS checks all 822 problems — 692 feasible and 130 infeasible — with worst reward error below 4.5e-10. Actual deployed Chrome checks 36 control reports, 108 policy selections, 117 vertex selections, three extra dense reports and twelve trusted completed downloads replayed by the native checker, with all ten deployed assets matching merged source. Both layers stay inside small supplied models — at most four states and eight total actions, one budget in 0.4 and exactly two ceilings in 0.5 — and neither is a new algorithm, a learned model, a scale benchmark or a per-trajectory safety guarantee. Budgets are expectations under the supplied initial distribution, not trajectory caps.

Distinct from 'A mixture is not a randomization', BellmanLab's second principle, which states the lesson — mixing policies re-rolls the policy at every visit, randomizing actions re-rolls only the action, and the certificate is what says the randomized optimum is really optimal — here the subsection is the release record: the two inspectors, the 0.5 two-budget extension and the full check counts behind both — and from 'A small residual is not a small error', the first, which is about a residual measured in different units than the error — here every number is exact and the gap is in the policy class, not in a tolerance: no deterministic policy can reach the optimum — and from 'Rectangular uncertainty is a different problem, not a different solver', the third, which is about the shape of an uncertainty set changing the problem — here the budgets are ceilings on one supplied model, not a different uncertainty set, and the 0.5 gap is two ceilings on the same model, not two problems.

### KVLens: a cache is state, not a shortcut

[Live attention and cache inspector](https://midya8.github.io/kvlens/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/kvlens) - [Equations, state contract and sources](https://github.com/midya8/kvlens/blob/main/CACHE.md)

KVLens runs one untrained causal multi-head attention block down four paths and counts the actual operations on each, so a cache saving is a counted number, not a complexity claim. The historical full path recomputes every old query's attention at each step, although this single-block model only uses the last row. A final-query-only no-cache path drops that unnecessary work — 21,376 counted operations against the historical full path's 34,944 — and the optimized cache, at 8,832, is measured against that stronger baseline instead of the historical one: 58.68% saved, against 73.08% for the historical cache (9,408) against the historical full path. The two numbers are named comparisons with different prefill policies, not an isolated effect and not a measured speedup. The old 32-token historical fixture still stands at 500,224 versus 45,888 (90.8% to one decimal) with the same original CI gate, and the fixture is vocabulary 16, width 8, two heads, seed 1, prompt [0,1,2,3] and eight predictions. One prediction offers no reuse within either matched pair, and zero predictions perform no work and have no savings percentage at all.

A cache is state, and the state is the part that broke. Prefill on occupied state previously appended new rows while restarting positions at zero; it now fails before mutations or counter changes. Owner, shape and token checks reject incompatible state, and deep forks keep parent and sibling rows independent. The separate scalar JavaScript engine computes seven complete seeded model weight inputs locally: edit the prompt and prediction budget, then follow every prediction, head and position through Q/K/V, scaled score terms, softmax weights, weighted values, output projections and all vocabulary-logit contributions, with complete cache states before and after each step and seven-component operation ledgers visible. All model weight matrices are available, with full audit and full model JSON downloads. A strict bounded JSON audit CLI rejects duplicate or unknown fields, nonfinite numbers, invalid IDs and types, excessive depth and oversized input, with exit 2, stderr and no partial output — and browser edits clear stale tables and disable exports until a valid recomputation.

The checks are exact, not approximate: 55 Python tests on 3.10–3.13 retain all 28 unchanged originals, including hand-computable attention, per-component counter reconciliation, 36 randomized configurations, zero/one-prediction controls, forks, occupied-prefill atomicity and strict errors. Three Node/actual-Chrome tests compare 32 complete native reports, 97 predictions and 66,940 full-report numeric entries, with Chrome checking every displayed cell across 227 head-step views and 1,279 attention-position rows, every weight table, two complete JSON exports, invalid clearing, keyboard and 320/390/1280 layouts. An isolated installed wheel verifies five module origins, all 32 reports, the original complete curve and equivalence, lifecycle and fork checks, the audit and all four original CLI modes outside the checkout, and full model and spec regeneration at normalized tolerance 1e-12 restores checked-in bytes. The limits are stated with the numbers: counts charge two operations per dot-product term including accumulation and exclude embeddings, positional functions, score scaling, softmax, bias adds, validation and report copying, so they do not measure latency, bandwidth, throughput or hardware instructions; retained K/V state has 2 × positions × width elements, and multiplying by eight gives an ideal packed binary64 payload, not actual Python or browser memory or traffic; and the final-query shortcut applies to this single block — it does not skip representations deeper layers need for their own K/V. No cache eviction, batching or paged storage is claimed, and this is standard attention and cache reuse, not a new algorithm, trained language model, production cache or GPU benchmark.

Distinct from 'Make the fast path prove it equals the slow path', the shared KVLens/SpecLab principle, which is about proving the fast path equals the slow one before counting anything — here the subsection is the release record: the four counted paths, the occupied-prefill bug fix and the lifecycle checks, with the equivalence already established by construction and swept checks — and from 'A saving is a curve, not a number', KVLens's first, which is about the saving varying with run length at a fixed baseline — here the run length is fixed at the default eight predictions and the number moves because the baseline changed — and from 'A saving measured against busywork is not a saving', KVLens's third, which states the baseline lesson — here the subsection carries the numbers behind it, the 21,376-operation no-cache path and the fourteen-point headline drop, plus the state bug and the checks the principle leaves out.

### DecodeLab: a wider beam is not a better beam

[Live probability inspector](https://midya8.github.io/decodelab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/decodelab) - [Numerical and stopping contract](https://github.com/midya8/decodelab/blob/main/AUDIT.md)

DecodeLab decodes from scratch so the strategy is the only thing being compared: stable staged sampling, complete finite continuation trees and beam traces with an exhaustive oracle alongside. The sampling fix is small and exact. For probabilities (0.6, 0.3, 0.1), temperature 0.5 and top-p 0.7, the old unnormalized nucleus powers sum to 0.46 and keep all three events; normalizing first gives the first event 0.7826 mass, so it alone is the nucleus. Temperature scaling subtracts the largest log probability before division so no event underflows together, normalized top-k precedes normalized top-p, and every intermediate distribution, retained mass and cutoff-prefix mass is returned, with lexical ties explicit.

Then the search is checked against the oracle, and the oracle disagrees with intuition. On the first synthetic table the horizon-3 oracle is `b + EOS` at probability 0.3822: width 1 ends at the cap with `a a a`, log-score gap 1.7066336993, while widths 2 and 3 attain the oracle. On the second table the oracle is `a + EOS` at 0.1940719831: widths 1 and 3 attain it, but width 2 prunes it and finishes capped with `b b a`, log-score gap 0.6275452747. A wider beam is not guaranteed to improve — under the documented shared finished/live beam semantics, width 2 can do worse than width 1 and width 3. Full beam traces retain every expanded candidate, parent, emission and pruning decision, and finished paths remain in the shared finished/live beam, so each failure is inspectable step by step rather than asserted. Bounded continuation trees return every EOS-terminated and cap-censored outcome with filtered sampling probability, original-model log score, total/EOS/cap mass and expected generated length; the conditioned prompt is not scored and capped paths carry no invented EOS factor.

The checks are exact where they can be: 54 Python tests include all 36 unchanged original tests, with unshifted 90-digit Decimal powers checked against production log/exp, rational Cartesian path products and rational forward mass/expected-length propagation on 40 random tables. 32 full audits and 120 staged cases are retained; independent scalar JavaScript checks 7,927 numerical values; actual Chrome inspects 96 distribution views, 231 displayed outcome rows and 956 candidate rows, plus JSON export, stale-on-edit behavior and 320/390/1280 layout bounds. Python 3.10–3.13 CI and an installed wheel outside the checkout reproduce the audit, and nine original files remain byte-exact, including the original 36-test file, tokenizer, metrics/reports, corpus and offline demo. A strict JSON table CLI and editable browser let readers change transition weights, temperature, filters, prompt and cap, inspect all distributions and candidate steps, paginate every outcome and export the complete current audit; edits hide stale results until recomputation. These are original synthetic Markov tables and a corpus-trained n-gram stand-in, not a neural model or language-quality benchmark — diversity and repetition metrics do not establish factuality, coherence or safety, and exhaustive enumeration is exponential and bounded. Temperature, nucleus sampling and beam search are prior work: the contract and demo link Holtzman et al., Higham's shifted-softmax discussion, Hugging Face generation documentation, OpenAI GPT-2 sampling, LabML and Raschka. No new decoding algorithm is claimed.

Distinct from 'A wider beam is not guaranteed to be a better beam', DecodeLab's second principle, which states the lesson — beam width is non-monotone because pruning can discard the oracle, so run the oracle and read the gap instead of ranking widths by intuition — here the subsection is the release record: the nucleus fix with its numbers, both counterexamples with their witnesses, and the full check counts behind them — and from 'Hold the distribution fixed and vary only the strategy', the first, which is about comparison design — holding the distribution fixed so the strategies are what differ — here the distribution and the strategies are fixed and the question is what the search itself guarantees, and the answer is in the two tables, not in the comparison setup.

### CTCLab: what the beam forgot

[Exact prefix inspector](https://midya8.github.io/ctclab/prefix.html) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/ctclab) - [Method and limits](https://github.com/midya8/ctclab/blob/main/PREFIX.md)

CTCLab 0.2 asks what prefix pruning throws away. Exact supplied rational CTC frame laws make every step inspectable: every positive blank/nonblank transition, every surviving prefix, the first-cut loss and the final transcript. The counterexample is a selected five-frame control. Width 3 chooses the true MAP transcript with full mass 47403/226100; width 4 chooses a lower-mass transcript, 11691/56525, even though it retains more total path mass. The wider beam did not lose the transcript by running out of room — it pruned the prefix the transcript needed, and strict permanent path pruning does not restore discarded contributions. The sufficient bound L > R + D certifies a unique MAP only under the supplied independent-frame law; a false bound makes no failure claim.

The checks are exact where they can be: 34 Python tests and 6 Node tests, 120 seeded path cases, 384 exhaustive binary-law cases and 270 altered report leaves; an independent browser BigInt engine agrees on 155 complete reports, and a separate path-enumeration checker reconstructs the entire evidence report. Actual Chrome exercises 36 control/width reports and 114 frame views at 320/390/1280 pixels, with 12 native-verified downloads, and an isolated installed wheel checks both prefix CLI entrypoints across all 12 controls and refuses 24 forged reports. The original floating-point target atlas remains intact. These are synthetic controls selected during development, not held-out speech evaluation: at most 4,096 complete paths, and exact arithmetic removes rounding error, not search approximation.

Distinct from 'The best path is not the likeliest transcript', CTCLab's principle, which is about the original mode — a greedy path losing to summed transcript mass on two uniform frames, 0.25 to the empty output against 0.75 to A — here the computation is already the summed mass, and the failure is in the pruning: width 4 keeps more total path mass than width 3 and still returns the worse transcript, so the question is what a wider beam discards, not which summary of the distribution you report — and from 'A wider beam is not guaranteed to be a better beam', DecodeLab's second principle, which is about beam search pruning the oracle under shared finished/live beam semantics — here the beam is a prefix beam over CTC frame laws, the oracle is the exact MAP transcript, and the wider beam's loss is permanent: the pruned prefix is gone, not merely unscored.

### MixtureLab: the mean can sit in a gap

[Conditional inspector](https://midya8.github.io/mixturelab/conditional.html) - [Algebra and sources](https://github.com/midya8/mixturelab/blob/main/CONDITIONAL.md)

MixtureLab conditions on whatever coordinates you actually observed, under the same frozen model — no refit, no new parameters. Each component contributes its observed marginal as evidence, a Schur-complement conditional mean and covariance, and log-space posterior weights; the numerical witness keeps the complete within- and between-component covariance, raw-unit Jacobians and the zero-dimensional limit, so the conditioning is inspectable end to end rather than a black-box slice. The native Cholesky solves and the scalar browser loops add no ridge and no conditional-covariance clipping, and a numerical breakdown is refused outright instead of patched over.

The point of the exercise is what the conditional mean is not. It can sit in a low-density gap between components, and the control that shows it is deliberately simple: a separate diagonal mixture where changing only the posterior component weights pushes the target variance from 1.09 to 25.1 while each component's target variance stays 0.1. Conditioning does not promise variance reduction, and the mean is not the mode — neither is assumed anywhere in the derivation. These are mathematical controls, not experiments on the wine data, and the original source has no naturally missing features: the masks are analytical queries of a fixed density, not a missingness-mechanism model, a causal intervention, a parameter-uncertainty estimate or an empirical coverage guarantee.

The checks are exact where they can be: 45 Python tests and eleven Node/real-Chrome tests, including 100 separate precision-block Gaussian oracles and direct scalar Gaussian formulas, all 5,696 original model/row/four-mask density factorizations and moment decompositions, and 160 complete native/scalar-JavaScript references with all 160 replayed through the UI. Source-context retention after invalid edits, omitted-value poisoning, keyboard controls, clipboard refusal and success, actual Blob downloads and 320/390/1280 layouts are all exercised, with actual page pixels inspected. An installed reproduction imports twelve modules and runs nine commands while retaining all 24 original fresh refits and audits, and all original data, fitted artifacts, browser data and previous geometry references stay byte-exact.

Distinct from 'Likelihood is not identity', MixtureLab's first principle, which is about a score improving while the identification gets worse — here no score is being chased at all, the conditional mean is computed exactly and still lands in a gap, so the question is what a summary statistic means, not whether the fit is good — and from 'A stopping rule is not a mode certificate', MixtureLab's second principle, which is about a stopping rule certifying nothing — here nothing stops early and nothing is being optimized, the mean is the exact conditional expectation and the gap is in reading it as a location, not in the procedure that produced it.

### DriftLens: the arithmetic is part of the test

[Live exact-orbit inspector](https://midya8.github.io/driftlens/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/driftlens) - [Method, nine sources and limits](https://github.com/midya8/driftlens/blob/main/ORBIT.md)

A p-value from DriftLens is only as honest as the arithmetic underneath it, and that arithmetic is spelled out rather than left to defaults. Statistic sums use math.fsum. Comparisons count values within 1e-12 below the observed as exceedances -- a prespecified conservative near-tie treatment, with no clipping and no data-dependent tie tolerance. Monte Carlo p-values use the +1 formula, not a zero-capable tail fraction, while exact mode uses exceedances over total. Monte Carlo samples each allocation uniformly and independently with replacement from a local seeded RNG, so duplicate allocations can recur and the observed allocation can come up again; the finite resolution is reported, and it limits how small a p-value can be.

The kernel arithmetic has its own edge cases. RBF divides coordinate differences by bandwidth before squaring, opposite extreme floats use a division-first fallback when appropriate, and unrepresentably large squared distances safely become zero kernel values. Exact tail counts are identical under complement labels for equal sizes. The cached imbalanced-group scoring is checked allocation by allocation on small fixtures against direct scoring -- near-constant kernels, exact p-values, group swapping, tied-zero results -- with the same 1e-12 near-tie rule throughout, and no bitwise equivalence is claimed for arbitrary floating inputs.

The package also says no to work it cannot bound. At least two rows per sample, finite numeric rectangular matrices, up to 500 pooled rows and 4,096 dimensions; draw count 1..10,000, exact allocation count at most 10,000, draws times N squared at most 20,000,000. JSON parsing and schema checks run before any expensive kernel work. And it is an offline local analysis package, not a service: no eval, no subprocess, no network request, no file mutation by the library -- the CLI reads only its input path and prints JSON, and no observations are uploaded.

The load-bearing assumption is exchangeability under the null, which is necessary for label permutation validity. Sequentially overlapping windows, repeated users, sampling bias or changes in preprocessing can violate it, and no part of the package checks that for you. The statistic, the families, the blocks and the witnesses all sit on top of that assumption; the arithmetic above only makes sure that, given the assumption, the number you get is the number the method actually produces.

None of this changes the statistic. The bullet above covers the RBF statistic, the supplied-Gram mode, Holm families, blocked permutations, witness diagnostics and imbalanced-group scoring; the conventions here are the layer underneath -- the sums, the tie rule, the +1 formula and the work limits that decide whether a p-value means what it says.

### DriftLens: the same data, a ninety-one-times smaller null

[Live exact-orbit inspector](https://midya8.github.io/driftlens/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/driftlens) - [Method, nine sources and limits](https://github.com/midya8/driftlens/blob/main/ORBIT.md)

The mixture demo in the case study runs the same rows through two different nulls. Reference has eight zero rows and two five rows; current has two zero rows and eight five rows. The pooled MMD squared is 0.648886 under both schemes, and both p-values come from the same 499 Monte Carlo draws at seed 13 and bandwidth 1. What differs is the space of allocations the p-value counts over: unrestricted, any 10 of the 20 pooled rows can be the reference group, which is C(20,10) = 184,756 allocations; blocked, each value stratum keeps its own reference count, so the space is the product of the within-block combinations, C(10,8) x C(10,2) = 45 x 45 = 2,025. Same data, same statistic, same draws -- and the unrestricted p is 0.014 while the blocked p is 1.000.

That gap is not a disagreement about the data. In the blocked scheme every allocation shuffles labels only within strata where the features are identical, so every allocation produces the same MMD squared as the observed one: all 499 sampled draws reach the observed value, and with the +1 formula the p-value is 500/500 = 1.000. In the unrestricted scheme the labels can cross strata, so most allocations dilute the difference; 6 of the 499 sampled draws reach the observed value, and the +1 formula gives 7/500 = 0.014. The two numbers answer different questions -- whether the labels matter at all, against whether they matter once the stratum counts are held fixed -- and the blocked answer is not a sharper version of the unrestricted one. It is a different null, and it says nothing on its own about which null the analysis should have used.

The restriction also changes what can be computed exactly. Exact mode caps at 10,000 allocations, so the unrestricted space of 184,756 can only be sampled, while the blocked space of 2,025 fits inside the exact limit. The reported p-values here both use the same 499 Monte Carlo draws, so that difference does not show up in these two numbers -- but on a slightly smaller problem the same restriction would be the difference between an enumerated tail and a sampled one. A block is a statement about the null made before any data is seen: prespecify it, or the smaller space is just a way of shopping for a preferred answer.

Distinct from 'A p-value cannot be smaller than its own resolution', DriftLens's first principle, which is about the floor -- how small a p-value can go given how many allocations exist -- here both p-values sit well above the 1/500 floor, and the difference comes from which allocations are allowed to count, not from how many draws were taken -- and from 'An unbiased estimate need not be a possible value', DriftLens's second principle, which is about the statistic -- here the statistic is identical in both schemes, 0.648886 either way, and nothing about the estimate changes; only the null it is measured against does.

None of this changes the data. The bullet above covers the mixture demo, both p-values and the blocked scheme; the space sizes are the layer underneath -- why the same rows and the same statistic give two different answers, and why the smaller space is a different question rather than a sharper one.

Case study: [driftlens.md](driftlens.md)

### KernelLab: the cost is stated up front

[Live target-response inspector](https://midya8.github.io/kernellab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/kernellab) - [Derivation and numerical limits](https://github.com/midya8/kernellab/blob/main/RESPONSE.md)

KernelLab is small on purpose, and the size is written down rather than discovered. At most 128 training rows and 128 query rows, width at most 8. The dense factor and the leave-one-out pass cost O(n^3), training storage is O(n^2); prediction refactors from scratch and then costs O(m*n^2), with an optional full covariance at O(m^2*n) work and O(m^2) output storage. Those bounds keep the example small and the arithmetic manageable. They do not guarantee conditioning -- a matrix that will not factor still fails outright. There is no sparse or variational model, no learned kernel, no ARD, no kernel optimizer, no sampling, no continuous, batch or noisy acquisition optimizer and no scalable production claim. The cost of a bigger problem is the honest answer: use something else.

The saved model is just as plain. An artifact carries version kernellab_v1, the raw training rows and targets, and the parameters -- nothing else. Prediction validates the artifact and refactors it instead of trusting a serialized alpha or factor, so loading a model costs the same as fitting one. That is a deliberate trade: a small transparent JSON format over fast repeated inference. It also means an artifact contains training data, and anyone handling one should treat it that way.

Two details in the case study are easy to misread. Ask the same query twice and you get the same latent function value with independent observation noise -- the noise is never added off the diagonal, so a repeated query is not a second observation of the same thing. And the log marginal likelihood is a training-model density computed from Cholesky log diagonals, not a determinant product and not an external performance score; its value includes the explicit training jitter, so it moves when the jitter moves.

The bands carry the same kind of qualification. They are pointwise observation bands, not simultaneous intervals and not distribution-free ones: each point's band is its own statement, and nothing here promises the whole curve stays inside at once.

None of this changes the model. The bullet above covers the covariance, the five explicit parameters, the LOO formula, the seed-19 fixture and the expected-improvement demo; the bounds, the artifact and the reading of a repeated query are the layer underneath -- the price of the computation and what the saved numbers actually contain.

### KernelLab: a smaller variance is not a better model

[Live target-response inspector](https://midya8.github.io/kernellab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/kernellab) - [Derivation and numerical limits](https://github.com/midya8/kernellab/blob/main/RESPONSE.md)

The seed-19 fixture hands both length scales the same data -- 15 training inputs in [-3, 3], truth sin(1.5x)+0.15x, Gaussian noise SD .12 -- and the two fits disagree about everything except the data. Length .8 reports observation RMSE .6242366399, mean NLL .1040604253 and 100/101 pointwise 2-SD coverage; length 5 reports 1.0038088074, 12.1309060441 and 22/101. The wide kernel is the worse model on every score the entry quotes, and it is also the more confident one: its inner/outer mean latent variance is .00269/.02796, against .00866/.58142 for length .8. The model that misses more is surer of itself in both regions, and the gap is widest on the outer figure -- length 5 claims .02796 against length .8's .58142, about twenty times tighter.

That is what a length scale does. A long one smooths the function flat, and a flat function has little room to move, so the posterior tightens around the wrong shape. The variance is a statement about the model, not about the world: it says how much the function could move under the stated assumptions, and here the assumptions are what is wrong. Reading the smaller number as the better fit mistakes the tightness of a belief for the quality of it.

The same fixture says this the other way round, and the entry already quotes those numbers: training LML -7.418945 for length .8 against -210.975199 for length 5, and LOO mean NLL .069448 against 15.090203 -- both rank the arms the same way the test scores do. The latent variance is the one number that points the other way, which is why it gets its own warning. On this fixture the model with the smaller posterior variance is the worse model by RMSE, by NLL, by coverage, by LML and by LOO. A number that measures confidence is not a number that measures accuracy, and here they disagree in the open.

Distinct from 'Name the numerical assumptions', KernelLab's first principle, which is about the stabilizers and priors declared before any computation -- here the declarations are fixed and the question is what a computed quantity means after the fact, and the variance is exact under assumptions that are themselves wrong -- and from 'A suggestion is not an evaluation', KernelLab's second principle, which is about a proposal awaiting execution -- here nothing is proposed and nothing is left to run; the fit is finished, the variance is in hand, and the misreading happens in the reading, not in the running.

None of this changes the model. The bullet above covers the fixture, the RMSE/NLL/coverage scores, the LML and LOO figures and the wide arm kept visible; the latent variances are the layer underneath -- the one number that ranks the arms backwards, and the reason a tighter posterior is not evidence of a better fit.

Case study: [kernellab.md](kernellab.md)

### WarpLab: the work is part of the report

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/warplab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/warplab) - [Mathematical contract](https://github.com/midya8/warplab/blob/main/CONTRACT.md)

WarpLab shows every number in the alignment, and the case study writes down what that showing costs. The base inspector's dynamic messages run in O(nm). Split the paths by visited-cell count and the length-resolved prefix and suffix messages cost O(nmK), with K = n+m-1; the conditional occupancy and edge convolutions that fill the atlas cost O(nmK²). Enumerating every path is exponential, and it stays bounded on purpose -- the largest grid has 8,989 paths, and a larger request fails rather than silently truncating. The cost of the inspection is part of the report, not a footnote to it.

The atlas carries a second layer the bullet doesn't mention: an entropy decomposition. The total path entropy splits into the entropy of the length mixture and the entropy inside each length, and the inspector shows both, along with the expected-length gradient and the negative length-variance curvature -- the slope of the expected-length response, the rate at which the expected length moves when the penalty changes. Length counts both endpoints, so a positive penalty favours shorter paths and a negative one rewards longer ones; either way the penalty changes the objective, not the accuracy of the alignment.

Temperature gets the same treatment. The controls run the same stretched sequences at 0.05 and at 8, and the two temperatures give different path distributions -- which is the point, not a measurement. A colder temperature concentrates the Gibbs distribution and a warmer one spreads it; neither setting is a better alignment. The bullet records the range 0.05..8, but the contrast is the content: temperature is a dial on the distribution, and turning it does not score the result.

Two boundaries keep the instrument honest. Unlike CTC there is no blank or label emission model here, and unlike optimal transport there are no coupling marginal constraints to satisfy. The case study names the neighbours it is not, instead of letting a resemblance do the work.

Distinct from 'Occupancy is not a coupling', WarpLab's first principle, which is about occupancies summing to expected path length rather than one -- here nothing is summed, the question is what the inspection costs and what the dials move, not what the numbers add up to -- and from 'A narrow bracket is not a small residual', WarpLab's second principle, which is about a bisection stopping rule certifying nothing -- here nothing stops early, the costs are exact bounds and the temperature contrast is a designed control, not a stopping decision.

None of this changes the alignment. The bullet above covers the Gibbs occupancies, the joint edge flows, the Hessian products, the 8,989-path tie and the inverse inspector; the costs, the entropy split and the temperature contrast are the layer underneath -- what it takes to expose every number, and what the dials actually move.

### WarpLab: three controls, three exact answers

[Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/warplab/) - [Source](https://github.com/midya8/warplab) - [Mathematical contract](https://github.com/midya8/warplab/blob/main/CONTRACT.md)

The case study's controls table lists six answers the implementation has to reproduce, and three of them are exact counts. Three zeros against themselves at temperature one have 13 equal-cost paths, and the raw score is -log(13), not zero -- a score that comes back zero for two identical sequences has dropped the log-partition term, the part that counts how many alignments exist. Seven zeros against seven zeros have all 8,989 legal paths tied, and the score is -log(8989) at temperature one. Both numbers are counts before they are scores: 13 and 8,989 are the Delannoy counts of monotone right, down and diagonal paths on a 3 by 3 and a 7 by 7 grid, and the score is the negative log of the count because every path carries the same weight.

The one-row grid is the control that catches a different failure. One row means one legal path, and with one path the entropy is zero and the cost Hessian is zero -- there is nothing to vary, so there is no spread to measure and no curvature to report. An implementation that returns nonzero entropy or a nonzero Hessian here is inventing variation where the alignment has none. The three controls sit next to each other in the table for a reason: the first two check that a tie is counted exactly, the third checks that a degenerate case is reported as degenerate, and all three have answers that leave no room for a tolerance to hide in.

Distinct from 'Occupancy is not a coupling', WarpLab's first principle, which is about occupancies summing to expected path length rather than one -- here nothing is summed, and the two tie figures are controls on the implementation rather than an argument that raw soft-DTW is not a metric -- and from 'A narrow bracket is not a small residual', WarpLab's second principle, which is about a bisection stopping rule certifying nothing -- here nothing stops early and nothing is bracketed; the answers are exact counts, and the check is whether the implementation reproduces them.

None of this changes the alignment. The bullet above covers the Gibbs occupancies, the joint edge flows, the Hessian products, the 8,989-path tie and the inverse inspector; the three exact answers are the layer underneath -- the counts behind the scores, and the one case where the honest answer is zero.

Case study: [warplab.md](warplab.md)

### HamiltonLab: the map is the check

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/hamiltonlab)

Before any chain runs, HamiltonLab writes down what leapfrog will do to a quadratic -- as a matrix, not a hope. `quadratic_map` derives the per-coordinate 2x2 map for U(q) = sum(k_i q_i² / 2), composes it over L steps, and returns the full one-step and trajectory maps, the determinant, a strict linear-stability indicator, the Hamiltonian quadratic defect, and the conditional Gaussian proposal moments. These moments are before Metropolis acceptance, not the accepted-chain kernel or a mixing bound. At epsilon = sqrt(2), k = m = 1, one step has diagonal 0 and off-diagonals sqrt(2) and -1/sqrt(2); its fourth power is the identity, and the computed four-step map has first row [1, 1.256074e-15], conditional proposal variance 1.577722e-30 and determinant 1. The corresponding one-step proposal variance is 2, not the target variance 1: ignoring Metropolis would change the target. A proposal-map moment is not a post-acceptance guarantee.

Mass gets the same explicit treatment, because the convention is easy to get backwards. Mass means momentum covariance M, with kinetic energy p²/(2m), not inverse mass. For diagonal precision [1, 100] at T = pi/2, identity mass [1, 1] gives frequencies 1 and 10, phases pi/2 and 5pi, a stiff-coordinate conditional variance of 3.749399e-33 -- roundoff near zero -- a lag-one correlation of -1, and a standardized numerical-map error of 1.000863. Precision mass [1, 100] gives frequencies 1 and 1, phases pi/2 and pi/2, stiff conditional variance .01, lag-one correlation 6.123234e-17, and error .001926. Both error figures are measured at epsilon pi/32 with L 16. Setting m = k equalizes the two frequencies; setting m = 1/k instead gives frequencies 1 and 100. No mass estimation or adaptation is performed -- the table is the reference, and the wrong convention would read it backwards.

The map's error is checked the same way, by refinement rather than by assertion. A fixed-duration T = 1.3 fixture with precision [1, 4] and identity mass compares the second coordinate's standardized numerical-map Frobenius error at 8, 16, 32 and 64 steps: .018919629, .004712811, .001177146, .000294221. Doubling the steps at fixed duration gives ratios close to 4, consistent with second-order leapfrog error. That is a deterministic development check, not a universal numerical bound or a benchmark.

The limits are stated with the instrument. The map uses floating-point composition, not symbolic or interval arithmetic, and only separable diagonal quadratics are covered -- arbitrary position-dependent Hessians, adaptation, stochastic gradients and accepted-kernel moments are not supplied. Precision and mass each lie in (0, 1e4], step size has nonzero magnitude at most 10, steps run 1 to 1000, and nonfinite composed results fail instead of becoming misleading JSON.

Distinct from 'A proposal that returns home is not a move', HamiltonLab's second principle, which is about the chains -- four leapfrog steps returning home so the sampler accepts everything and goes nowhere -- here the map is the instrument that shows the return before any chain runs, and the one-step proposal variance of 2 against a target variance of 1 is a separate fact about the proposal, not about the chain -- and from 'Acceptance is not exploration', HamiltonLab's first principle, which is about high acceptance hiding trapped chains on a separated mixture -- here nothing is accepted or rejected, the map and the mass table are computed before Metropolis ever sees a proposal.

None of this changes the sampler. The bullet above covers the chains, the resonance, the rank diagnostics and the window audits; the map, the mass convention and the refinement ratios are the layer underneath -- the exact arithmetic the sampler is checked against, written down before a single transition is drawn.

### BoostLab: the saved model is a contract

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/boostlab)

A fitted BoostLab model is a file before it is a prediction, and the file has a version. Prediction still accepts `boostlab_v1` unchanged, so an old artifact keeps working; Newton artifacts use `boostlab_v2`, which records the leaf mode, L2 and cap alongside the model. Strict schemas check the stored leaves against that cap instead of `[-1,1]`, so a v2 file that claims Newton leaves outside its own cap is refused rather than silently reinterpreted. Both versions store the same skeleton: ordered feature names, the initial logit, the rate, and the recursive trees.

Before any leaf is used, prediction validates the file: the schema, finite scalars, feature indices, leaf ranges, depth and tree count. JSON round trips preserve tested predictions, so a model that survives a save and a load predicts the same way it did before. Inputs become binary64 on the way in, which means large integer distinctions can disappear, and NaN, Infinity and silent normalization are rejected outright rather than repaired.

The bounds are part of the contract too. A request allows 1-16 features, 1-2000 rows per request or split, 1-200 rounds, depth 1-3, `min_leaf` 1-1000, patience 1-200 and a rate in (0,1]. There are no missing-value, categorical, weighted, stochastic, multiclass or histogram modes, and prediction carries no dynamic code, remote model download, networking or automatic deployment. This is not a compatible clone of gbm, sklearn or XGBoost, and a file from one of those is not a BoostLab model.

None of that is a claim about the fit. A model that loads is not a model that generalizes: the development fixture was inspected, the Newton comparison does not isolate a causal benefit, and the checks above say nothing about whether the trees are any good. They say only that what was fitted is what gets predicted, in the version it was saved in, within the bounds it was built for.

Distinct from 'A cap is a choice, not a guarantee', BoostLab's second principle, which is about the cap itself -- the bound placed on the Newton step inside the solve, and what it does and does not promise about the fit -- here the cap appears only as a number written into the artifact and checked on load; the question is what prediction refuses, not what the bound guarantees -- and from 'Two metrics can disagree, and the choice is the claim', BoostLab's first principle, which is about which metric selects the model -- here nothing is selected: the schema and bounds checks run on every prediction, whichever prefix validation picked.

None of this changes the fit. The bullet above covers the fitting, the selection split, the Newton refinement and the inspector; the versions, the validation and the bounds are the layer underneath -- what a saved model promises to be, and what prediction checks before it trusts the file.

### SubmodLab: the expectation is not the rounding

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/submodlab)

The rounding inspector turns a fractional point into one set, and it writes down every number that step touches. It exposes the exact multilinear extension, every excluded-item gradient and excluded-pair Hessian context, the independent-product count probabilities, and both endpoints of every deterministic cardinality swap. A two-item half-probability control has expected count 1 yet exceeds budget 1 with probability 1/4, and a duplicate-coverage control with expectation 3/4 rounds to a singleton worth 1. The expectation is a number about the distribution; the rounded set is one draw from it, and the inspector keeps the two apart instead of letting the average stand in for the outcome.

Not every table gets rounded. Nonmonotone submodular controls are accepted, while synergy and wrong total probability mass refuse rounding outright -- the inspector will not produce a rounded set from a table whose extension it cannot trust. That refusal is part of the output, not a missing feature: a rounding that silently ran on a synergy table would certify a set the theory does not cover.

The checks are counted, not asserted. The extension adds nine Python tests, bringing the suite to 33; 21 reports match 10,682 exact native/BigInt leaves, checked by independent product, derivative and path oracles, with full-record Chrome checks and installed reproduction alongside. [Live rounding inspector](https://midya8.github.io/submodlab/rounding.html). [Rounding method and sources](https://github.com/midya8/submodlab/blob/main/ROUNDING.md).

The scope stays narrow. This is bounded deterministic rounding, not continuous optimization, randomized marginal preservation, a general matroid method or a learned-quality claim. At most six items, 64 subsets and eight facility rows, as in the rest of SubmodLab.

Distinct from 'A guarantee is not an optimum', SubmodLab's principle, which is about the greedy worst-case factor against the actual optimum -- the default control's greedy 5 against the exact 6, with a finite-k factor of 3/4 -- here nothing is greedy and nothing is optimal: the question is what a randomized rounding's expectation does and does not promise about the single set it returns, and the inspector refuses tables where that promise cannot be kept.

None of this changes the selection. The bullet above covers the greedy/OPT gap, the prefix certificate and the controls; the extension, the gradients, the swap endpoints and the refusal cases are the layer underneath -- the arithmetic of turning a fractional point into one set, written down before any rounding is trusted.

### SubmodLab: the audit counts the rest too

[Case study](submodlab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/submodlab)

The headline controls are the ones the entry names: the six-row default, the duplicate-representative matrix, the synergy table and the nonmonotone table. The rest of the audit stays in view too -- zero and full budget, all-zero optimum ties, fractional values, modular additivity and seeded rectangular controls. The duplicate-representative comparison is descriptive, not a causal learned-mechanism result: it shows what singleton top-k does on that matrix, not what any learned system would do. And the boundary is stated, not implied: exhaustive enumeration is exponential, not a scalable optimizer. The inspector stays inside six items, 64 subsets and eight facility rows.

The Chrome numbers in the entry are totals, and the case study breaks them down. Actual Chrome verifies 360 subset rows and bars and 2,056 full records: 360 subsets, 33 greedy steps and their candidate tables, 57 prefixes, 812 edges and 794 diminishing-return witnesses. Summary selections, assumption failures, certificates, export, stale and invalid behaviour and 320/390/1280 containment are checked alongside. The two parsers are not papered over either: native JSON rejects duplicate keys and bounds input to 32KiB, while browser JSON.parse retains the last duplicate key and bounds input to 32,768 characters -- the difference is stated explicitly rather than hidden behind the numerical agreement. METHOD.md records the exact links and the documentation shorthand conflicts.

Distinct from 'A guarantee is not an optimum', SubmodLab's principle, which is about the greedy worst-case factor against the actual optimum -- the default control's greedy 5 against the exact 6, with a finite-k factor of 3/4 -- here nothing is greedy and nothing is optimal: the question is what the rest of the audit covers once the headline controls are set aside, and whether the two parsers agree on what they were handed -- and from the first SubmodLab subsection, 'the expectation is not the rounding', which is about what a fractional point does and does not promise about the single set it returns -- here nothing is rounded: the tables are exact, the enumeration is complete, and the question is what the controls around the headline cases actually check.

So the entry stays as written. It states the greedy/OPT gap, the prefix certificate, the headline controls and the rounding inspector; the edge controls, the Chrome breakdown and the parser difference are the part underneath them -- the reason the headline numbers can be trusted to mean what they say, and the limits that keep them honest.

### DiffuseLab: the start is an approximation

[Source repository](https://github.com/midya8/diffuselab) · [Design contracts](https://github.com/midya8/diffuselab/blob/main/DESIGN.md)

Every sampler in DiffuseLab starts from a standard normal, and that starting point is an approximation, not the truth. The terminal alpha-bar is about 0.006123, not zero, so the finite forward marginal at the last step is not quite a standard normal -- the reverse chain begins from a distribution the forward process never exactly reaches. Nothing in the demo hides that: it is stated as a boundary, not smoothed over, and no claim is made that the mismatch is too small to matter.

The dials are literal in the same way. Eta=0 adds no step noise, and the sampler is deterministic given the initial latent -- but that means one fixed output per seed, not one fixed output for every seed; change the latent and the samples change. And eta=1 matches DDPM arithmetic only on the full grid with matched random draws: a sparse grid at eta=1 is a different discretization, not a cheaper way to run the same sampler. The bullet records the call savings; the point here is that each setting has to be read as the sampler it actually is.

The equations are pinned to their sources rather than summarized. The forward marginal, posterior variance and epsilon reverse mean follow Ho, Jain and Abbeel (2020) -- Eq. 4, Eq. 7 and Eq. 11, with the simplified noise loss and Algorithms 1-2 -- and the DDIM sampling follows Song, Meng and Ermon, Eq. 12, 16 and Appendix D.3. One notational trap is named outright: the DDIM paper's alpha means cumulative alpha-bar in this code. The snapshot training losses, meanwhile, use different random minibatches, so they are not a monotone learning curve -- a loss that goes up between snapshots is not evidence the model got worse.

The checks are counted, not asserted. The 33 tests include all-parameter central finite differences, independent posterior-mean algebra, schedule indexing, final-step determinism, serialization, bounds, CLI, a mode-collapse counterexample, DDPM/DDIM equivalence, skipped-step formulas and counted predictor invocations, and CI on Python 3.10-3.13 regenerates the figure from the actual demo on 3.11. Nearest-neighbor metrics depend on sample size and are not likelihoods or distributional guarantees, and the demo does not isolate causal contributions of architecture, schedule or optimizer -- the fixture is a development fixture, and the audit says so.

Distinct from 'Choose the metric the failure cannot pass', DiffuseLab's first principle, which is about a one-sided metric letting a collapsed generator pass -- here no metric is being gamed and nothing collapses: the question is what the starting distribution and the sampler dials actually mean before any metric is computed -- and from 'A cheaper sampler is not the same sampler', DiffuseLab's second principle, which is about a cheaper procedure changing the samples and the cost/fidelity tradeoff -- here nothing is cheaper or dearer: the full-grid eta=1 match and the sparse-grid difference are statements about discretization, not about saving calls, and the approximation at the start applies to every sampler equally.

None of this changes the samples. The bullet above covers the predictor, the fixture, the controls and the DDIM call savings; the starting approximation, the literal dials, the pinned equations and the test inventory are the layer underneath -- what the sampler assumes before it runs, and what the audit actually checks.
