### CausalLab: the arithmetic split is the contract

[Source repository](https://github.com/midya8/causallab) · [Model and algorithm](https://github.com/midya8/causallab/blob/main/DESIGN.md)

The arithmetic is split three ways on purpose, and each piece carries its own contract. The original SCM engine enumerates finite worlds in floating point and keeps rare evidence in log space. Response bounds use rational arithmetic for the supplied binary-float values. The frontdoor functional accepts integer/fraction probabilities. Nothing here is one arithmetic pretending to cover everything: each computation states what it is exact about, and the rest is outside its contract.

Identity and validity are kept apart. A SHA-256 hash is an identity checksum, not evidence that a mechanism is valid. Queries return full joints, including zero-probability rows, normalized model identity and optional bounded posterior noise-world traces — a zero row is a stated absence, not a row that went missing. Possible rare events may have underflowed stored probability but finite logs, and impossible evidence errors explicitly instead of being quietly reinterpreted. Query output is capped at 4,096 rows, with a tighter trace-output preflight.

The bounds say what they are and what they are not. This is exact for supplied binary-float values, not decimal text or empirical causal truth: these are feasible-coupling ranges for assumed point action marginals, not confidence intervals or uncertainty propagation for unknown marginals. Bounds also cover changed/unchanged fractions, effect variance, covariance, sufficiency and necessity. The stable/flip mechanisms attain the equal-marginal endpoints, and a third SVG shows all 24 joint-mass segments in six attaining witnesses. Exact arithmetic does not validate the causal graph, latent assumptions or compatibility of the joint with that graph — that is the caller's claim, and the computation does not make it for them.

Distinct from 'A strong association does not say what an intervention would do', CausalLab's first principle, which is about an association standing in for an intervention — here the intervention is already the question, and the point is which arithmetic the answer is computed in, not whether the association can stand in for it — and from 'Adjusting for more variables is not adjusting better', CausalLab's second principle, which is about choosing controls for a backdoor adjustment — here nothing is being adjusted for, and the choice is between arithmetics, not between control sets — and from 'A mediator can identify an effect', CausalLab's third principle, which is about identifying an effect through a mediator when no backdoor set can block the confounding — here nothing is being identified: the criterion has already passed or failed, and the question is what the numbers that follow are allowed to claim.

None of this changes the answers. The bullet above covers the reversal, the two mechanisms, the response bounds, the graph checks and the frontdoor functional; the arithmetic split, the identity-versus-validity line and the bounds' extra quantities are the layer underneath — what the numbers are computed in, and what they refuse to claim.

Case study: [causallab.md](causallab.md)

### ShapleyLab: exact is a property of the enumeration

[Public repository](https://github.com/midya8/shapleylab) · [Derivation, six sources and limits](https://github.com/midya8/shapleylab/blob/main/LEDGER.md)

A local explanation is a choice of score function, point and reference game, and ShapleyLab's game is the empirical-background replacement game. For each mask, excluded coordinates are replaced from one complete background row, then scores are averaged over rows. That preserves dependence among the missing features and breaks the dependence between fixed and missing ones: it is not conditional imputation, and it never factors the background into independent coordinate marginals. The empty coalition shows the difference plainly — for the product scorer against background rows (0,0) and (1,1), v(empty) is 0.5, not 0.25: the average of two complete rows, not the product of two independent averages.

"Exact" here is a property of the enumeration, not of the explanation. Every coalition value is enumerated — 2^d values by integer bit mask — but that says nothing about symbolic arithmetic or about attribution to an unknown population. Averages are taken by fsum of contributions divided by B, so a raw sum cannot overflow first; when every background output is identical, that float is returned unchanged, preserving equality with the single full-mask score. Attribution increments, means and the efficiency residual must stay finite or the computation fails — nothing is renormalized, and no numerical residual is allocated to an arbitrary feature to make the books balance.

The boundary is stated where the tool stops. The CLI evaluates only an explicitly serialized polynomial scorer; there are no network requests, dynamic imports or model downloads. The call budget is validated before the callback is ever invoked, and the callback must be deterministic — the tool does not spend extra calls trying to prove that. Lists are copied where output could otherwise alias input, and callbacks receive immutable tuples. A malicious or stateful callback is outside the model contract: no attribution algorithm can make an untrustworthy score function safe.

Distinct from 'The reference is part of the question', ShapleyLab's first principle, which is about the background defining which question is asked — here the background, the point and the score are all fixed, and the point is what the computation does with that game: how masks are valued, how averages are taken, and what "exact" covers — and from 'A zero error bar is not a zero error', ShapleyLab's sampled principle, which is about an estimate misreporting its own uncertainty — here nothing is sampled, every coalition is enumerated, and the guarantees are about the arithmetic, not the diagnostic — and from 'Together changes the share', ShapleyLab's Owen principle, which is about grouping changing which arrivals are allowed — here no grouping is involved, the players are fixed, and the question is what the numbers mean once the game is set.

None of this changes the answers. The bullet above covers the formula, the counterexamples, the group and permutation modes and the Owen inspector; the replacement semantics, the averaging rules and the contract boundary are the layer underneath — what the numbers are computed over, how they are combined, and what the tool refuses to claim.

Case study: [shapleylab.md](shapleylab.md)

### StableLab: the comparison is not the proof

[Source repository](https://github.com/midya8/stablelab) · [Exact contract](https://github.com/midya8/stablelab/blob/main/CONTRACT.md)

The clingo comparison is the one number in the StableLab write-up I would not lean on too hard. On the base inspector it agreed with an optional external clingo 5.8.3 run on stable-model families for 1,387 programs; on the uniform-equivalence atlas it agreed on 20,978 augmented stable families across 10,489 fact contexts and 2,511 comparisons. Both are finite cross-checks against one installed solver version, and the case study says so plainly: the base check does not validate the UI or the proof evidence, and does not prove general solver compatibility; the 0.3.0 check is not a general solver proof. What the comparison does establish is narrower — that the generator's answers match an independent solver on the programs actually enumerated — and the rest of the evidence has to carry the rest of the claim.

The matching screenshot pairs say the deployed files are the files that were checked. The case study also records what was actually looked at: desktop and phone pixels, circular support, stable alternatives, seeded closure, empty and no-model cases, and error states on the base inspector; desktop and phone overview, fact and candidate details, and UE and contract pixels on the uniform inspector. A matching screenshot proves the deployment is the thing that was tested; the inspection list is what proves somebody looked at it.

The three stages landed as three separate merges: the base inspector in PR #1, merged normally after all final checks passed; strong equivalence in PR #2; uniform equivalence in PR #3, with the download-readiness regression from the CI race fixed in PR #4. That history matters because the scope narrowed as the claims got stronger — the base inspector covers at most eight atoms and 32 rules, while both equivalence atlases cover at most five atoms and 24 rules per side — and because the strong-equivalence claim itself is restricted to finite normal-rule contexts over the same declared vocabulary, with no fresh-atom context UI, no update-semantics preservation, and no scalable-solver or new-theory claim.

Distinct from 'Support is not grounding', StableLab's principle, which is about a supported model that no derivation grounds — here every model is enumerated and grounded, and the question is what the outside checks certify: the solver comparison confirms answers on enumerated programs without proving the solver, and the screenshot pairs confirm the deployment without reading the screens.

None of this changes the answers. The bullet above covers the evidence inventory — the counts, the controls, the atlases and the CI race; the comparison limits, the inspected pixels and the merge history are the layer underneath — what the cross-checks actually certify, what was looked at by eye, and how the three stages landed.

### DigitLab: the attack is measured against the rows that were right

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/digitlab)

The entry reports the attack table as correct counts after the attack: at normalized L-infinity radius 0.1, the MLP keeps 898 of 1,797 rows under FGSM and 768 under a 20-step, three-start PGD candidate pool, against 1,734 clean. Those are the numbers the case study states, but it states them a second way that reads differently: FGSM fools 836 of the 1,734 rows the model had right, and PGD fools 966 of them. An attack can only take a row the model got right in the first place, so the fooled count is the measurement — and the comparison that matters is against the control. One fixed random-sign draw at the same radius leaves 1,708 correct, 26 fewer than clean. The gradient is doing the work, not the noise: 26 rows from a random sign, 836 from one gradient step, 966 from the candidate pool.

The radius itself is easier to misread than the counts. Pixels live in [0,1], normalized by 16 from block counts in 0..16, so epsilon 0.1 is 1.6 original block-count units — each pixel moves by at most 1.6 counts out of 16. That is the size of the perturbation in the digit's own units, and it is also the scope: the continuous attack domain is not restricted to valid integer-count handwriting, and no human label-preservation, imperceptibility or real-world OCR threat claim is made. A radius of 0.1 sounds small in normalized units and reads differently once it is 1.6 counts — still small, but now a number about the digit rather than about the normalization.

The partition has the same shape: exact about rows, silent about writers. UCI reports 30 training writers and a different 13 test writers, but the numeric files carry no writer IDs, so that assertion is source-reported, not independently verified here. The protocol is exact where it can be — the hash partition gives 3,062 fitting and 761 validation rows, the 1,797 official test rows stay separate, and exact feature-hash overlap is zero across all three partitions, so no identical row leaks — but zero overlap rules out identical rows, not similar handwriting, and it does not prove writer-disjoint validation. The counts are exact; the writer separation is a sourced claim, and the case study says which is which.

Distinct from 'At the boundary, the rule decides', DigitLab's first principle, which is about the v0.3 radius infima — whether the boundary itself is an error, decided by the tie rule — here nothing sits at a boundary: the fooled counts are interior numbers, and the question is which denominator they are read against, not what happens at the threshold — and from 'A sum that closes is not a correct split', DigitLab's second principle, which is about the v0.5 attribution — a completeness identity that holds by construction whatever the split — here no total is guaranteed by construction; the fooled counts are read off the table, and the control is what separates the gradient's work from the noise.

None of this changes the audit. The bullet above covers the counts, the versions, the exact box and radius proofs and the attribution checks; the fooled rows, the 1.6 units and the writer caveat are the layer underneath — what the attack numbers are counted against, what the radius is in the digit's own units, and what the partition guarantees about rows without guaranteeing it about writers.

### FlowLab: the slice is measured against the known conditional

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/flowlab)

The bullet describes how a conditional slice is built — joint log density along one free coordinate, trapezoid normalization of a piecewise-linear density on a finite interval — but it never says what the slices actually return. Same trained flow, x fixed at -1.5, 0 and 1.5, free y on [-4, 5], grids of 65, 257 and 513 points, 2,505 model evaluations in total. On the finest grid the medians are 0.726272, -0.632395 and 0.722021, the variances 0.256504, 0.277220 and 0.334000, and the 5%-95% quantiles [-0.080939, 1.582895], [-1.507665, 0.223181] and [-0.117114, 1.771246].

Refining the grid from 257 to 513 moves the medians by 0.00000649, 0.00000070 and 0.00001986. That stability is a statement about the grid, not about the conditional — refinement inside one interval cannot measure missing tail mass.

The check that means something is against the known generator, whose full-line conditional means are 0.75, -0.6 and 0.75, each with variance 0.25. The flow's interval medians land within 0.033 of those means, and its variances read 0.256504, 0.277220 and 0.334000 against the generator's 0.25. The train-fitted Gaussian's full-line conditional means are 0.005363, 0.019952 and 0.034542, with variance 0.924248: far from the generator on both counts, on the same fixture where its test NLL already trails. The comparison is descriptive — the flow curves are interval-renormalized approximations, the references are full-line formulas — so it does not establish calibrated coverage or model quality elsewhere.

Distinct from 'Agreement between two approximations is not evidence', FlowLab's second principle, which is about two grids agreeing and both being wrong — the narrow peak neither can see — here the grids are not the check: the check is the generator's closed-form conditional, a reference that does not share the method — and from 'A narrower window is a new distribution', FlowLab's third principle, which is about the inner window getting a new normalizer — here the window stays fixed at [-4, 5] throughout, and the question is how the slice inside it compares with the full-line truth, not what narrowing it would do.

None of this changes the audit. The bullet above covers the coupling layers, the fixture, the NLL table, the window audit and the missed-peak counterexample; the slice table is the layer underneath — what the conditional slices actually return on the disclosed fixture, and how far they sit from the known conditional.

### VariLab: the fitting work is budgeted before it runs

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/varilab)

The bullet states the spin caps — enumeration, mean-field and curvature at 12 spins, elimination and MAP at 64 — but it never says what a fitting run is allowed to cost. Reference work is `2**n*(n+edges+n*n)`, capped at 786,432, and requested fitting work is `sweeps*n*(n+edges)`, capped at 500,000, with at most 1,000 sweeps. JSON files are capped at 256 KiB, and fields and weights are finite with magnitude at most 100, on strictly indexed nonduplicate edges. An error returns no partial results — there is no half-fitted answer to inspect.

The counts are operational limits, not timing promises: they bound how much work a request may ask for, not how long it takes, so the same request costs the same count on any machine. The elimination planner works the same way — a hub-first star needing 65,536 bucket assignments is refused before numeric tables are allocated — but that cap is on bucket assignments; these caps are on fitting work.

For portable regeneration, the curvature figure fixes the captured broken-symmetry binary means to 0.9949015284526289. The figure carries the complete rational certificate and omits the separate libm-dependent float gradient diagnostic — that diagnostic stays in the API — and no curvature values are rounded for sign decisions, so the figure's sign decisions do not depend on which machine's libm evaluated them.

Distinct from 'Optimizing the bound is not recovering the marginals', VariLab's first principle, which is about a higher ELBO going with worse marginal means — here no fitting runs at all, the question is what a fitting request may cost before it starts — and from 'Locally best choices need not fit together', VariLab's second principle, which is about exact marginal modes giving a suboptimal joint decision — here the numbers are budgets, not decisions, and the failure mode is an error with no partial results, not a worse answer.

None of this changes the audit. The bullet above covers the ELBO/marginal divergence, the saddle at J=3, the joint-MAP gap and the elimination caps; the work budgets are the layer underneath — what a fitting request is allowed to ask for, counted as work rather than timed, with nothing partial returned when it errors.

Case study: [varilab.md](varilab.md)

### BanditLab: the budget is checked before anything runs

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/banditlab)

The bullet gives the default state limit — 4,096 — and the three counts that straddle it, but it never says the limit is a knob. `state_limit` can be set anywhere from 1 to 20,000, and the exact count, `binomial(H+2K,2K)` posterior/time states, is computed before any state is generated or any polynomial integrated. Two arms at horizon 12 need 1,820 states and three arms at horizon 8 need 3,003, both under the default; three arms at horizon 12 need 18,564, which is refused unless the limit is raised. A refusal returns no partial plan — nothing half-built to inspect — and the audit CLI refuses the same way, before solving, if its preflight fails. There are `2*K*decision_states` directed action/outcome edges in the graph that does get built. And the counts are only counts: they do not bound Fraction bit lengths, serialized bytes, arithmetic operations, wall time or RAM, so a request that fits the state budget can still be expensive in any of those — the budget is a bound on states, not a promise about cost.

The same before-anything rule governs a custom policy. A kernel is a list indexed by every returned state ID: each decision row carries K rational `[numerator,positive_denominator]` probabilities that sum exactly to one, each terminal row is `[]`, and every entry is an exact non-boolean integer with numerator 0..1,000,000 and denominator 1..1,000,000. Unreachable rows are still required and validated — a row with zero occupancy keeps its conditional value, and occupancy itself is summed history probability, not a count of histories or the posterior probability of an unknown environment. Randomization is independent conditional on the current information state: no hidden private memory, no correlated coins across time, no history-dependent input beyond the state. Named policies break ties by first index, and `fixed` commits to the best initial mean arm. The audit CLI takes exactly `version`, `priors`, `horizon` and `policy`, and shares the strict JSON, size and state-budget rules of `solve`.

The shape limits work the same way: internal posterior parameters may grow past the supplied 1..16 starting shapes — the limit applies to what you hand in, not to where the posterior ends up. Terminal mass is one, including at horizon zero. A six-module wheel runs outside the source checkout and reproduces the demo exactly, with no random seed, network, paid API, model download, GPU or dataset needed.

Distinct from 'Let the baseline learn too', BanditLab's first principle, which is about giving the greedy baseline the same outcomes the method uses — here nothing is compared at all, the question is what a request may cost before it starts — and from 'The best first move can be the one that looks worse now', BanditLab's second principle, which is about a lower current mean being the right opening when its outcome decides what comes next — here the numbers are budgets, not decisions, and the failure mode is a refusal with no partial plan, not a worse answer.

None of this changes the audit. The bullet above covers the three-pull example, the greedy and fixed comparisons, the clairvoyant bound, the forward policy audit and the exact state counts; the budget rule is the layer underneath — what a solve or audit request is allowed to ask for, counted before anything runs, with nothing partial returned when it refuses.

Case study: [banditlab.md](banditlab.md)

### InfluenceLab: the linear prediction agrees with the sum of the parts

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/influencelab)

The entry never states the first-order number for the pair. Deleting rows 5 and 6 together has a first-order loss delta of -0.149822, against an actual endpoint delta of -0.357507 — the linear step gets the sign right and the size wrong by a factor of about 2.4. Row 5 alone has a first-order delta of +0.203204, so the pair's negative sign comes entirely from row 6's pull in the linear view; the refit agrees the pair moves the loss down, but puts the move at -0.357507, not -0.149822.

The number is worth stating because of what it sits next to. The two separate finite deletions sum to -0.142898, within 0.007 of the linear joint prediction — so a reader holding only linear numbers would conclude the group behaves like its parts, and the arithmetic would back them up. The actual joint refit sits about 0.215 away from that same sum. The case study makes the point in one sentence and the table makes it in numbers: at the base point the group derivative is the sum of the individual derivatives, so the linearization is bound to agree with the sum-of-parts story; the finite deletion is where the additivity breaks.

The path integral is what catches it. Simpson16 along the deletion path gives -0.357502140, within 4.825e-6 of the endpoint — the local derivatives, integrated step by step as the weights fall from 1 to 0, recover the refit that a single linear step misses. The information was in the derivatives all along; one step from the base point throws most of it away.

Distinct from 'A group deletion is not the sum of its single deletions', InfluenceLab's first principle, which is about the linear prediction missing the refit for row 5 and the single deletions not adding to the group — here the linear prediction is the subject, not the error: it agrees with the sum of the parts, and the question is what that agreement is worth when the refit says otherwise — and from 'A deletion is defined by what you hold fixed', InfluenceLab's second principle, which is about which finite refit the words 'delete row 5' name — here every number compared is either a linear prediction or a refit under the same held-fixed quantities, and the question is whether one linear step can stand in for the path, not which refit the words mean.

None of this changes the audit. The bullet above covers the row-5 linear-versus-refit gap, the group non-additivity, the sign reversal on all seven rows and the full test counts; the pair's first-order delta is the layer underneath — the number the linear view offers for the group, how close it sits to the sum of the parts, and how far the refit sits from both.

### BeliefLab: the caps are part of the contract

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/belieflab)

The entry says "preflight caps" and moves on, but the numbers are the contract. Each layer preflights `actions * previous_retained_vectors**2` before it allocates any candidate vectors. `candidate_limit` is an integer in 1..4,096, and the aggregate generated-candidate cap is 8,192, excluding the initial zero vector. If a layer would exceed either cap, the call raises an error identifying that layer and returns no partial solution — a refused contingent solve returns no partial comparison and never starts open-loop enumeration.

The file and scope caps are just as explicit. JSON model files are capped at 256 KiB; CLI policy files are limited to 64 KiB, with duplicate keys rejected. The scope is two hidden states, two observations, 1..3 actions, discount in [0,1], horizon 0..4 and zero terminal value — at most 81 fixed sequences under that scope. A forward audit permits at most 15 decision histories and 16 terminal histories, not 15 actual visits in one execution: all 31 records are expected-occupancy witnesses, including zero-probability paths.

None of those caps is a performance promise. Horizons and candidate caps limit representation counts, not a requested convergence tolerance, Fraction bit complexity, time or RAM — complete sequence counts are different from contingent backup candidate counts, and neither bounds rational bit cost, time or RAM. The theory behind the backups is credited where it belongs: finite-horizon policy-tree alpha-vector backups follow MIT 6.0411/16.420, Fall 2023, L19: Solving POMDPs Offline. The two-state exact hull, the schema, the resource contract, the code, the fixture, the independent oracles and all four figures are original.

Distinct from 'Optimal at one belief is not optimal at every belief', BeliefLab's first principle, which is about where a fixed tree stops winning across beliefs — here no tree is judged at all, and the question is what a request is allowed to cost before anything runs — and from 'The value of feedback is the gap to the best open-loop plan', BeliefLab's second principle, which is about the gap between two optima — here the gap never enters, and the question is whether the call is refused before either optimum is computed — and from 'One residual is not a value certificate', BeliefLab's third principle, which is about a discounted residual bracketing an infinite value — here no value is bracketed, and the caps say nothing about how close a finite value is to the infinite one.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the sensing fixture, the open-loop gap, the forward occupancy audit, the fixed-tree regret certificates and the discounted brackets; the caps are the layer underneath — the numbers that decide whether a call runs at all, and what it refuses to hand back when it doesn't.

### RobustLab: what you can run, and where the math comes from

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/robustlab)

The entry states the margins and the training result, but not what you actually type. `python -m robustlab demo` runs the comparison; `python -m robustlab audit examples/model.json examples/data.json --norm l2 --epsilon 0.4` audits a supplied model at an L2 radius of 0.4; `python -m robustlab fit examples/data.json --norm linf --epsilon 0.3 --steps 1000` fits the L-infinity-trained arm for a thousand steps at radius 0.3. The suite runs with `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v`, and `PYTHONPATH=. python examples/render_audit.py --check` regenerates the figure. It is dependency-free Python 3.10+, with CI on 3.10–3.13, and needs no network, paid API, model download or GPU.

The two exact APIs have their own commands. `python -m robustlab exact-demo` and `python -m robustlab exact-audit examples/model.json examples/data.json --norm linf --epsilon 0.1` run the float-to-rational audit; `python -m robustlab box-demo` and `python -m robustlab box-audit examples/model.json examples/data.json examples/bounds.json --norm l1 --epsilon 0.3` run the box-intersection audit. The entry already says what each returns — here the point is only that they are separate commands with their own render checks, not flags on the same call.

The math is credited, not claimed. The implementation follows Kolter and Madry's tutorial and the logistic inner problem, Theorem 2 of arXiv:2208.09449, for standard norm duality and robust linear logistic training. The exact float-to-rational conversion follows Python's Fraction documentation, and the box derivation is written out in the repo's DESIGN.md beside Duke's standard fractional-knapsack formulation. All code, tests, synthetic rows and diagrams are original, MIT licensed.

The scope stays narrow in writing too. The bias is fixed, and coordinates and epsilon are used as supplied, so feature units matter. Strict robustness requires a worst signed margin above zero, and a boundary tie fails conservatively even when the +1 prediction rule gets that label right; a failed strict flag is not necessarily a label-flipping witness. Fixed-step descent can oscillate at norm kinks and carries no convergence claim.

Distinct from 'A clipped attack cannot prove robustness', RobustLab's first principle, which is about why carrying an unconstrained optimum into a box and clamping it cannot establish the minimum — here nothing is carried or clamped, and the question is which commands exist and what they take — and from 'A float pass at the boundary is not an exact pass', RobustLab's second principle, which is about one fixture where the float verdict and the exact verdict point opposite ways — here no verdict is compared, and the question is which command runs the exact check and which paper the inner problem comes from.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the margins, the two training arms, the rational certificates and the box witness; the commands and the citations are the layer underneath — how to rerun it, and whose theorem it stands on.

### ParticleLab: the references are named, not implied

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/particlelab)

The entry names a Kalman reference and an RTS reference and leaves it there, but the filter itself comes from somewhere specific. The bootstrap filter follows Gordon, Salmond and Smith's 1993 paper, Section 3. What it does not take from that paper matters as much as what it does: none of the bearings-only experiments, none of the roughening, none of the prior editing, none of the performance claims. The fixtures are its own, the resampling is systematic with an adaptive ESS trigger, and the comparisons are against exact scalar references rather than against the paper's results.

The retrospective half is credited the same way. The retained-history SIR smoother and the scalar RTS reference are independently implemented with Särkkä and Svensson (2023), sections 12.2/15.1/15.2, as the technical reference. No source text, code or figures are copied from either source, and the same holds for the supporting notes: SYMMETRY.md carries the derivation, assumptions and complete fixed-seed comparison for the square-observation fixture; SMOOTHING.md the index/weight derivation, figure, complete fixed results and limits; BACKWARD.md the algorithm, comparison figure, numerical guards and limits; PROPOSAL.md the derivation, all-seed figure, complete metrics and limits.

The scope stays narrow in writing too. The Kalman reference is an exact scalar linear-Gaussian filter with an explicit no-transition-at-t0 convention, and the RTS reference conditions on the complete observation sequence — both are checks on a scalar supplied-parameter model with finite particles and floating point, not a tracking benchmark. There is no convergence certificate, no high-dimensional proposal, no learned parameters, and the evidence estimates are random, with logs that are not unbiased log-evidence estimates.

Distinct from 'Effective sample size measures weights, not support', ParticleLab's first principle, which is about a tidy ESS hiding a collapsed support — here no score is reported at all, and the question is whose method the filter implements and what was left behind — and from 'A conditioned proposal is not a better filter', ParticleLab's second principle, which is about a proposal that uses the observation failing to improve the filter — here no proposal is compared, and the question is which sections of which book the smoother and the RTS reference were checked against.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the collapse fixture, the genealogy, the backward draws and the conditional proposal; the citations are the layer underneath — whose work the filter stands on, and what it deliberately does not borrow.

### PrivacyLab: the version, the caps and the citations

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/privacylab)

The entry describes what the adaptive and frontier extensions compute and leaves it there, but it never says which version added them. Version 0.2 adds the full finite history-conditioned mechanism trees; version 0.3 computes the complete worst-directed-neighbor delta profile for every finite factor. The labels matter because the suite and the wheel grow with them: 29 tests in the original, 46 retaining them with the adaptive extension, 63 with the frontier — and a five-module wheel matching both original CLI outputs, a six-module wheel matching all four, a seven-module wheel matching all six. The entry already states the counts; the versions say when each count arrived.

The entry also states the table-size caps and stops there, but the caps that decide whether a call is accepted at all are the input caps. The point audit takes at most 8 secrets, 64 outputs and 28 undirected edges, with 512-bit rational components and a 100000-byte CLI input cap. The adaptive trees run at most six rounds, eight outcomes per round, 64 transcripts and 128 conditional nodes, with 512-bit constructed probability and flow components. Inputs are exact integers, unsigned decimal or fraction strings, or API Fraction values — floats and bools are rejected, rows sum exactly to 1 without renormalization, and long canonical fractions are resubmitted as API Fraction values under a 160-character input-string cap. The frontier keeps the 512-bit input-probability and 160-character string rules while its exact internal coefficients and intersections are not rounded back into those caps. A call that exceeds a cap is refused, not approximated.

The citations get the same treatment. 'Modern accounting notes' in the entry is arXiv:2210.00597, and the fully adaptive composition context behind the adaptive extension is arXiv:2203.05481v3 — with the explicit note that no filters or odometers from that paper are implemented. The hockey-stick arithmetic itself follows Dwork and Roth's Algorithmic Foundations: the (epsilon, delta) definition, read as a worst-event excess over exp(epsilon), is Definition 2.4 in Section 2.3, and the fixed round factors multiplying to T follow the basic composition of Theorem 3.16 in Section 3.5. Google Research's piecewise-linear accounting overview is the third source the frontier section names. None of that is a new privacy theorem or a Connect-the-Dots implementation; the code is an original small implementation of established arithmetic, and the IDs say whose arithmetic it is.

Distinct from 'Marginals do not compose when randomness is shared', PrivacyLab's first principle, which is about a joint release being worse than its marginals because one shared coin couples two releases — here no joint is compared to its marginals, and the question is which version added what and what a call is allowed to carry — and from 'A reveal on an impossible history costs nothing', PrivacyLab's second principle, which is about a conditional failure costing only the mass that reaches it — here no failure is charged and no history is conditioned on, and the question is the input limits and the version labels, not what a reveal costs.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the shared-coin failure, the developed table, the adaptive trees and the frontier profile; the version labels, the caps and the citations are the layer underneath — when each piece arrived, what a call may carry, and whose work the arithmetic stands on.

### SegmentLab: the coordinates are the question

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/segmentlab)

The entry says SegmentLab answers exact requested-pair cut-inclusion queries, and then never shows what a question looks like. It looks like this: `python -m segmentlab joint examples/sequence.json --pairs '[[1,3],[2,4]]'`. Each pair is a coordinate into the joint cut law — are there cuts at both 1 and 3, and are there cuts at both 2 and 4 — and the answer is the exact joint mass of both cuts occurring, with other cuts allowed, returned as finite log probabilities, with an impossible event and an undefined conditional getting different flags. The entry already states why the coordinate matters: on six zeros with a count cap of 2, cuts 2 and 4 each carry marginal mass .011658220257 while their exact joint mass is 0, so the pair [2,4] is the coordinate where multiplying marginals invents a structurally forbidden event. Two marginal lookups cannot ask that question at all.

The predictive command takes the same kind of coordinate, with a different meaning. `python -m segmentlab predictive examples/sequence.json --queries '[[2,0.0],[3,1.0]]'` asks for the replicate density at value 0.0 for observation 2 and at value 1.0 for observation 3 — [index, value] pairs, not cut positions. The two commands share the bracket syntax and nothing else: one queries the partition, the other queries the replicate mixture, where new replicate observations integrate partition and segment-mean uncertainty, then add fresh noise. In the selected five-point outlier fixture a moment-matched Gaussian overstates density at 2 by 1.54 nats, a shape comparison using all fitted observations, not forecasting. Mixing the two coordinate systems up would ask a question neither command can answer.

The other commands take their coordinates the same way. `infer` returns the full evidence and marginals; `sample --draws 4 --seed 23` draws complete partitions backward from the posterior; `information` returns the full discrete partition entropy, the conditioned-prior KL and the dependence of the entire cut vector rather than an independent-cut shortcut; `decide --false-cut-cost 1 --missed-cut-cost 1` returns the constrained minimum expected false/missed boundary-loss decision. The fixtures the entry already states are all coordinates of one kind or another: MAP [12,13] at probability .960218 under sigma .15 against MAP [] at .648223 under sigma 2, where model confidence can isolate contamination and is not a noise-model quality ranking; the no-cut decision at boundary risk .680000591 against MAP [3] at .869071786, where MAP and additive-boundary loss answer different questions and neither number is observed accuracy; a four-point posterior entropy 1.029013408 above the prior .836308115 with KL .166962288; and K4 with exact mass .000115427 and zero sampled visits in 10,000 joint draws at seed 19, where zero visits do not imply zero posterior mass. Full marginals cost O(N²·K²) work, bounded to 120 observations and 12 requested segments. A coordinate is a pair of cuts, an index and a value, or a partition — and the answer is only exact when the question names which one it is.

Distinct from 'Marginal peaks are not a joint partition', SegmentLab's principle about the MAP partition and the per-gap marginals being two different objects that look interchangeable — here no MAP is reported and no per-gap winner is stitched into anything, and the question is how a joint query is written, not which of two summaries to trust — and from 'Two possible cuts can be jointly impossible', the principle about the joint answer being zero while both marginals are positive — here the [2,4] fixture is the reason the coordinate exists, not the result being reported, and the subsection is about the syntax that asks the question, not the zero that comes back.

None of this changes the inference. The entry covers the evidence, the marginals, the MAP backtrace and the decision loss; the coordinates are the layer underneath — how each question is actually posed to the CLI, and why a pair of numbers means something different depending on which command receives it.

### PolicyLens: the behavior value and the sources

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/policylens)

The entry states the target value 0.78 again and again and never says what the policy that logged the data earns. On the constructed two-context design the behavior value is 0.29: behavior (.8,.2)/(.2,.8), target (.1,.9)/(.9,.1), reward means (.2,.8)/(.9,.1). The 0.49 gap between the logging policy and the target is the improvement the estimators are being asked to measure, and without the 0.29 the reader cannot tell whether recovering 0.78 is a large correction or a rounding error. Twenty deterministic mean-reward rows match the logging frequencies exactly, and with a wrong q of .5 the estimators still recover the target — IPS, SNIPS and DR all give .78 while the direct method gives .5 — but the direct estimate sits between the two values, closer to the policy that logged the data than to the one being evaluated. That is what a wrong model does: it reports the policy you already have.

The sources get the same treatment as the numbers. The case study names two arXiv IDs — arXiv:1503.02834v1 and arXiv:1906.03323v2 — and then draws the line itself: standard IPS/SNIPS/DM/DR only, not an empirical-likelihood implementation. The IDs say whose estimators these are; the disclaimer says what the code does not do. The cross-fitting background is Kallus et al. (2022), introduction and algorithm discussion, at https://proceedings.mlr.press/v162/kallus22a/kallus22a.pdf — with the same boundary drawn: PolicyLens implements only ordinary contextual-bandit estimators and a tabular reward-model helper, not that paper's distributionally robust algorithms or its efficiency theorem. The crossfit numbers the entry already states sit under that boundary: five folds of 400 rows each, seed 17, fixed default prior, direct 0.7496455 and DR 0.7509088 against oracle DR 0.7532475 and a known population value of 0.78 — explicitly not closer to truth, not an estimator ranking.

Distinct from 'Label what the data cannot identify', PolicyLens's first principle, which is about a target action the data cannot speak about getting a label instead of a silent zero-weight success — here no action is flagged and no support gap is labeled, and the question is what the logging policy itself earns and whose papers the estimators come from — and from 'A deletion report is not a deletion experiment', PolicyLens's second principle, which is about a delete-one-row report holding the policy and the predictions fixed while it counts one row's contribution — here no row is deleted and nothing is recomputed, and the question is the baseline number and the citations, not what a report holds fixed.

None of this changes the estimate. The entry covers the estimators, the clipping trade-offs, the crossfit helper and the ledger; the behavior value and the sources are the layer underneath — what the logged policy was worth, and whose work the arithmetic stands on.

### LatentLab: the sources, named with their IDs

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/latentlab)

The entry credits two papers by name — Kingma & Welling for the variational autoencoding, Burda, Grosse & Salakhutdinov for the importance weighting — and then stops. Names without IDs are hard to check, so here they are: Kingma & Welling, Auto-Encoding Variational Bayes, arXiv:1312.6114, Sections 2-3 and Appendices B-C, for the standard variational autoencoding, reparameterization and analytic Gaussian KL; Burda, Grosse & Salakhutdinov, Importance Weighted Autoencoders, arXiv:1509.00519, Section 3 and Theorem 1, for the standard importance/IWAE formulas. The derivations the case study links — DESIGN.md in the repo — are the hand-derived backward pass through every weight, bias and logvariance path, not a re-derivation of either paper.

The case study draws the boundary right next to the credits, and the entry should carry it: this is an original scalar implementation, synthetic fixture, audit and test suite, not a new algorithm or a reproduction of their experiments. The importance extension gets the same treatment — it is inference auditing, not IWAE training, not a confidence interval, not a new estimator, not an ESS accuracy certificate and not exact neural evidence. The numbers sit under that boundary: the 150-parameter Gaussian VAE in scalar Python with no array library or autodiff, 43 tests in total after the extension adds 15 importance tests and three figure tests, and a five-module v0.2 wheel that reproduces both demos outside checkout with the original demo output and original SVG byte-identical.

Distinct from 'A maximum effective sample size can still miss the mass', LatentLab's first principle, which is about the two-atom failure control where ESS 64 still misses nearly all the joint mass — here no weights are drawn and no mass is missed, and the question is whose papers the formulas come from — and from 'Reconstruction is not generation', LatentLab's second principle, which is about the held-out table where beta 0 reconstructs slightly better and generates worse — here no reconstruction or generation number is compared, and the question is the citations and the boundary around them, not what the model can and cannot do.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the VAE, the held-out table, the importance witnesses and the failure control; the IDs and the boundary are the layer underneath — whose work the arithmetic stands on, and what the code does not claim to be.

### FisherLab: redundant coordinates change the approximation, not the answer

[Case study](fisherlab.md) - [Technical derivation](https://github.com/midya8/fisherlab/blob/main/DESIGN.md) - [Martens (2020), JMLR 21/17-678](https://jmlr.org/papers/volume21/17-678/17-678.pdf)

The main entry names the redundant chart but never writes down its rows, so here they are: (1,2,1,0),(0,1,0,1),(0,0,0,0) — four parameters, rank 2. The third action's row is all zeros, and the four columns span the same two directions the baseline's two already do, so the chart adds coordinates without adding reachable tangents. The exact natural direction doesn't move: the natural L1 gap is still 0, the same (-5/12,1/18,13/36) tangent as the baseline, shear and full-logit charts.

What moves is the diagonal approximation, and it moves the wrong way. Its L1 gap on the redundant chart is 37/20 — the worst of the four full charts, against 5/9 on the baseline, 14/15 on the shear and 31/60 on full logits. The exact direction is untouched by the extra coordinates; the diagonal approximation is not. Damping goes the other way — 31/216, the smallest of the four — so redundancy punishes one approximation and flatters the other, and neither response is a property of the exact direction. The three SVG tests verify all 60 table cells and positions, so every one of those gaps is checked cell by cell rather than asserted.

The attribution gets its citable handle here too. The case study credits Martens (2020) for the Fisher and pullback framing, the damping and the infinitesimal-versus-finite-step line — that is James Martens, New Insights and Perspectives on the Natural Gradient Method, JMLR volume 21, paper 17-678, Sections 5 and 9 on Fisher and pullback, Section 10 on damping, Section 12 on infinitesimal versus finite-step parameterization invariance — alongside Kakade (2001) Section 2. The paper is the background; the charts, the witnesses and the controls are the original small-model work.

Distinct from 'A local metric does not certify a finite step', FisherLab's first principle, which is about a finite step whose landing differs by chart — here no step is taken, the chart is overcomplete and the exact direction is unchanged, and the question is what the extra coordinates do to the approximations — and from 'An aggregate improvement can hide a per-context regression', FisherLab's second principle, which is about a shared parameter trading one context off against another — here there is one fixture and one chart, no aggregate and no context, and the question is redundancy, not weighting.

### TagLab: the witnesses between the versions

[Source](https://github.com/midya8/taglab)

The main entry says real Chrome exercises 327 optimizer states plus 984 model/sequence/focus states, but it never gives the layout counts that came with them: 320, 390 and 1280 layouts. V0.2 has the same kind of omission at the edges: the test list includes a -2000 log-probability underflow case — an event whose finite log probability underflows to zero in floating point — and a run of 256 positions with large finite scores. Both are checks on the boundary between a finite log probability and no probability at all, and neither made it into the entry.

V0.3 is where the gap is widest. The entry describes the backward-conditional sampler and its paired independent-node control, but none of the counts that back it: 428 exhaustive Fraction path-product witnesses, 2,304 Python/JavaScript full traces and 384 browser queries, for 5,031 UI states/queries overall — between the 4,647 browser states the entry reports at v0.2 and the 5,895 UI states/queries at v0.4. The combined suites pass 37 Python and 16 Node/real-Chrome tests at v0.3, against 31 Python and 12 Node/real-Chrome at v0.2 and 43 Python and 20 Node/Chrome at v0.4. The counts are not the whole witness, either: it keeps model path log probability, ideal kernel-product log probability and the finite-precision numeric kernel separate -- three quantities for the same draw, not one.

Distinct from 'The normalizer is part of the model', TagLab's first principle, which is about where the normalizer sits, per step or over the whole sequence — here the normalizer never moves, the fitted scores are fixed, and the question is only what each version's checks actually ran — and from 'A count is not an expectation', TagLab's second principle, which is about an expected count versus an exact total on one sequence — here the numbers are counts of witnesses, traces and browser states, not expectations, and nothing is drawn from them, so there is no total to meet and no draw that can fail it.

### ClusterLab: the holdout table, arm by arm

[Case study](clusterlab.md) · [Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/clusterlab/)

The entry gives the holdout ARI for the four arms — .662690, .665149, .669707, .665596 — and stops there. The case study's holdout table carries two more columns, and they do not rank the arms the same way. Raw k-means++ scores NMI .751821 with silhouette .186434; raw uniform starts score NMI .749688 with silhouette .186142; PCA16 scores NMI .740137 with silhouette .224268; whitened PCA16 scores NMI .739303 with silhouette .149408. PCA16 takes the best ARI (.669707) and the best silhouette (.224268) while sitting second-lowest on NMI; whitened PCA16 is lowest on both NMI and silhouette. No arm wins all three, and the NMI column is not adjusted for chance, so it is not a corrected agreement score either.

That spread is the point of keeping all three columns visible. The entry already says cross-geometry silhouette differences are descriptive, not a ranking of one unchanged objective, because PCA and whitening change the distance being measured — the same caution holds across the scores themselves. Picking the arm by ARI alone, or by silhouette alone, would quietly choose a different winner, and none of the three numbers is the objective any arm was fitted on: every arm still chooses by minimum fitting inertia among seeds 100–109.

Distinct from 'The objective you minimize is not the score you report', ClusterLab's second principle, which is about selection by fitting inertia versus evaluation on the holdout — here every number is an evaluation score, and the gap is between the evaluation scores themselves, not between selection and evaluation — and from 'An optimum is not a uniqueness proof', ClusterLab's first principle, which is about whether an optimal matching is the only one — here nothing is being matched, and the question is what three scores that agree on no winner say about four arms that already fit.

Case study: [clusterlab.md](clusterlab.md)

### ScoreLab: the audit starts before the first trajectory

[Case study](scorelab.md)

The entry gives the variance numbers and the matrix control, but it never says where the exactness comes from. The policy is a softmax with known weights and integer features, and ScoreLab audits it at theta = 0 — the anchor where the weights alone fix the probabilities and the log-probability score is a feature minus its weighted average, so both are rational and there is no floating-point softmax anywhere in the production audit. A separate backward sensitivity recursion computes the expected return and its exact derivative first, before any trajectory is enumerated, and the estimators' means are then checked against that independent answer rather than against themselves.

The audit also counts before it generates. A count-only dynamic program computes the exact number of complete positive-probability paths from the transition support, and the trajectory limit is checked against that count before a single path is built — a refusal produces no partial audit at all. The two-state, two-action, horizon-three fixture has exactly 64 paths: a limit of 63 refuses, a limit of 64 succeeds. The same care shows at the edges: horizon zero has one empty trajectory with zero return, zero gradient and zero covariance and no baseline nodes at all, and a time/state node that is never visited — or whose score is zero — has zero energy, so its optimal baseline is unidentifiable and is reported canonically as zero, not as proof that its conditional value is zero.

Distinct from 'The natural baseline is not the best baseline', ScoreLab's principle, which is about which baseline value removes the most variance — here no baseline is compared and no variance is ranked, and the question is what the audit establishes before any baseline is chosen: the anchor that makes the arithmetic exact, the count that decides whether the enumeration runs, and the report a node gets when it was never reached — and from 'An unvisited zero is still a score', RAGLine's principle, which is about an implicit zero in a sparse representation outranking a negative match — here the zero is an explicit report about a node with no energy, not a value the ranking arithmetic produced by accident — and from 'Pin the baseline, not just the result', StructGate's principle, which is about gating a control so fixture drift cannot erase a contrast — here no control is gated and nothing is compared; the count and the anchor are computed, not measured against a baseline.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the Bernoulli counterexample, the sequential model and the matrix control; the anchor, the count and the unidentifiable node are the layer underneath — why the numbers are exact, why a refused audit says nothing at all, and what a zero means when nothing was visited.

Case study: [scorelab.md](scorelab.md)

### ForestLab: the reference matrix is the check, not the display

[Case study](forestlab.md)

The entry says what the leaf-collision matrix means — a collision count, not a similarity score — but it skips how the numbers in it are checked. The new diagnostic checks 6,272 source-pair entries through independent traversal and set intersections, and the complete native/scalar-JS reference matrices carry 1,152 mixed-partition entries and 4,608 heatmap entries plus 8,928 terminal coordinates, so the matrix the browser shows is compared against one computed independently, entry by entry, instead of being trusted because it rendered. Real Chrome checks every pair and all 13,824 view-specific colors, along with the exact large integers, the indexed and feature-only states, invalid retention, model context, keyboard, clipboard and the actual Blob JSON download.

The wheel check closes the loop the same way. The installed wheel preserves all 19 fresh fits and the permutation audits, then checks two new exact exports — the geometry is reproduced outside the checkout, not only inside the test run that built it. And the fixed 24-row matrix stays what it is: descriptive, not selected for quality and not a whole-data embedding. No labels are read by the new calculation, but the fitted partitions already carry supervised label information, so the geometry is frozen model geometry either way.

Distinct from 'Proximity is a leaf-collision average, not a similarity score', ForestLab's third principle, which is about what the matrix is — a collision count that can fail to be a kernel — here the question is how the entries in it are verified: the traversal counts, the reference matrices and the wheel reproduction, not the interpretation — and from 'Out-of-bag is still in-sample', ForestLab's first principle, which is about what an out-of-bag score measures when the tree subset changes — here nothing is scored and no subset is chosen; every pair is checked against an independent reference — and from 'A permutation that refits is a different question', ForestLab's second principle, which is about a frozen model facing broken input pairings — here the model is frozen too, but the inputs never move; the check is on the matrix the frozen model produces.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the bootstrap counts, the joint permutation and what the Gram matrix means; the traversal counts, the reference matrices and the wheel check are the layer underneath — why the displayed matrix can be believed, entry by entry, and what the fixed 24-row view is and is not.

Case study: [forestlab.md](forestlab.md)

### GroundCheck: the audit keeps the whole input, not just the verdict

[Case study](groundcheck.md)

The entry says the inspector recomputes the report independently and that audit parity is bounded ASCII, but it never says what the audit actually keeps. The bounded ASCII audit retains every raw sentence unit, every content-token occurrence, every citation resolution, every source-score fraction and every equal-valued numeric occurrence, each with its exact input offset — and every source text is shown in full, including sources outside the current policy, so a source the policy excludes is still in the record instead of silently dropped. The inspector recomputes the full report in scalar JavaScript, switches between all provided sources and only citations in the same raw unit, lets you edit the answer, the sources and the threshold, inspect all documents and export the current JSON — and neither policy unions documents, so a sentence that draws on two sources is never scored as supported by one alone.

The browser side is kept inspectable the same way the numbers are kept exact. Explicit bounds, invalid-input handling, stale-result hiding and safe text rendering are what make the recomputation something you can watch rather than trust: a stale result never sits beside a fresh edit pretending to be current, and an invalid input gets handled instead of rendered into a plausible-looking report. The arithmetic underneath is checked with exact Fraction comparisons and exhaustive small token subsets, four Node/actual-Chrome tests check full-report parity, every displayed source score, edits and export, and 320/390/1280 containment, and Python 3.10–3.13 regenerate the same artifacts.

Distinct from 'A lossy view of a number is not the number', GroundCheck's second principle, which is about the number itself — a float or a rounded display hiding the exact value — here nothing is rounded or converted; the question is what the record retains and whether the browser work can be watched, not whether two numbers are the same number — and from 'A resolved citation is not a correct citation', GroundCheck's third principle, which is about what a resolved pointer proves — here the audit keeps the citation resolution in the record either way; the point is that the resolution, the score and the offset are all retained, not what any one of them certifies — and from 'A lexical check can catch a fabrication, not certify a truth', GroundCheck's first principle, which is about what a lexical check can certify about a claim — here the audit makes no claim at all; it keeps the input and the recomputation visible so the certification question stays with the reader.

None of this changes the audit. The entry covers the decimal equality, the full-precision gates and the 25 controls in both scopes; the retained record and the browser's inspectability are the layer underneath — why the report can be re-derived from what it shows, and what stays visible when the policy changes.

Case study: [groundcheck.md](groundcheck.md)

### PartsLab: the certificate exports the problem, not just the verdict

[Case study](partslab.md)

The entry says the fixed-basis certificate adds exact binary64 primal/dual arithmetic and portable JSON proofs, but it never says what a proof actually contains. The certificate exports the actual represented pixels, the basis and the candidate coefficients together with a rational dual vector and the primal, dual and gap values — the problem the proof is about travels with the proof, so the checker is verifying the embedded problem in front of it rather than a summary of it. The checker certifies that embedded problem, not its origin or the quality of the model it came from, and the 120 witness references cover only the twenty already-public examples for the six models, not all holdout rows — the exactness is bounded by which problems were exported, and the boundary is stated in the same place as the result.

The same visibility runs through the rest of the work. The projected-gradient and complementarity diagnostics stay visible while the fits run, so the stationarity state is something you can look at rather than take on trust, and the browser side is checked the same way it is built: real Chrome drives arbitrary edits, keyboard input, basis controls, copy denied and copy success, actual download and the expanded 320/390/1280 layouts, across all 2,048 pixels in the 32-part contribution grid. Nothing here is a screenshot of a result; the diagnostics and the controls stay in view so the exact claims can be re-derived from what the page shows.

Distinct from 'Decreasing loss is not convergence', PartsLab's first principle, which is about whether the optimizer arrived at all — here arrival is not in question, the six fits ran their full budgets and the frozen-basis solves meet their per-row KKT thresholds, and the point is what the certificate ships and what stays visible, not whether a falling loss means anything — and from 'A reconstruction does not identify the parts', PartsLab's second principle, which is about many decompositions giving the same reconstruction — here the reconstruction is fixed and the question is what the proof contains, not what any of the parts mean — and from 'An exact interval is not a path', PartsLab's third principle, which is about one candidate's exact range against the whole response path — here the bound is not a range over pixel values at all; the limit is which problems the witness references cover — and from 'A nomination is not a certificate', PartsLab's fourth principle, which is about the exact check refusing a nomination — here nothing is nominated or refused, the certificate is already built, and the question is what it exports and what the checker certifies — and from 'A valid proof does not authenticate its source', MarginLab's first principle, which is about a proved bound saying nothing about where the proved object came from — here the boundary is in the artifact itself: the certificate lists the embedded problem and the twenty examples it covers, so the limit is stated rather than left to be inferred.

None of this changes the certificate or the fits. The entry covers the exact arithmetic, the Fraction verifier and the 120 witness references; the exported problem and the visible diagnostics are the layer underneath — why the proof can be re-checked from what it ships, and what stays in view while the numbers move.

Case study: [partslab.md](partslab.md)

### ArgumentLab: the deployed inspector is checked live, not just agreed offline

[Case study](argumentlab.md)

The entry reports the offline agreement for the ideal/eager release — native bitsets, an independent complete-ternary-labelling/set checker and JavaScript sets agree on 645 full reports, PyArg 2.0.2 matches 1,290 ideal/eager families, and the suite is 53 Python and 18 Node tests — but it never says what happened on the deployed site. Live Chrome checks the ideal/eager inspector itself: 42 reports, 84 core views, 117 trace states, 4,266 candidate selections and 14 trusted completed downloads, independently replayed at 1280/390/320 widths. All 61 full unmasked screenshots match across live, local, feature and main, 21 deployed assets and the root alias equal main, and exact feature/PR/main CI and Pages passed — so the pages a visitor opens are the ones the tests ran against, not a stale copy of them.

The same layer exists for the SCC release, and the entry compresses it to one line: 42 SCC reports at 320/390/1280 with all 52 screenshots matching. The full deployed record behind that line is 171 subproblems, 1,056 candidate selections, 1,128 obligations and 14 real independently verified downloads, with all 15 deployed assets plus root matching main and feature/PR/main CI and Pages green. The two layers answer different questions. Agreement between engines proves the semantics are computed the same way twice; the live checks prove the deployed pages show those same answers in a real browser at phone and desktop widths. A mismatch between the deployed assets and main would be caught by the asset comparison, not by the test suite — which is why both numbers are in the record.

Distinct from 'Removing an attack can remove an acceptance', ArgumentLab's first principle, which is about edits to the attack relation changing acceptance — here nothing is edited, the graph is fixed, and the question is whether the deployed pages show the answers the engines computed, not whether an edit helps or hurts — and from 'A decomposition is not decided in advance', ArgumentLab's second principle, which is about components recomputed after each choice — here there is no recursion and no choice left to make, the fixed point is already computed, and the question is whether the deployed inspector displays it — and from 'A skeptical intersection is not a defensible position', ArgumentLab's third principle, which is about the parent intersection needing pruning before it defends its own members — here admissibility is already settled by the fixed point, and the question is whether the live pages show the pruned result rather than the intersection.

None of this changes the semantics. The entry covers the offline agreement, the PyArg match and the test counts; the live checks are the layer underneath — why the deployed inspector can be trusted to show the same answers the engines computed, and what would catch it if it didn't.

Case study: [argumentlab.md](argumentlab.md)

### HingeLab: the radius workflow is seeded and checked on its own numbers

[Case study](hingelab.md)

The entry gives the radius extension its report counts — 104 cross-language profile reports, 28 radius reports in Chrome — but it never says what the radius tests were seeded with. The retained suites are counted per workflow: 200 base and 160 reference seeded networks, and the radius tests add 40 seeded profile networks of their own. Those 40 are the radius suite's own evidence, not a share of the base suite's 200, and without them the three workflows read as one suite with one count.

The same gap is in the browser record. The entry says Chrome checks 28 radius reports; the case study adds that those 28 run across 320/390/1280 pixels, and that the full phone and desktop workflows were visually inspected. The radius reports are not trusted on their own: generator-disabled checks and forged report fields and types remain covered, so a report that was never generated, or was tampered with after generation, does not pass as evidence. The documentation review preserved every one of the 42 non-document source files — the review checked the docs, not the code, and nothing in the code was dropped to make the docs read cleanly.

The radius checker itself is bounded, and the bound is stated in the case study: O(P^4) intersection checks for P mask-derived boundary/root points, plus mask enumeration, evaluation and sorting, with rational bit cost not constant. That is the price of covering every radius atom by finite ordering rather than a sampled grid — exact, but quartic in the number of boundary points, and the bit cost grows with the rationals themselves.

Distinct from 'A set can have zero length and still exist', HingeLab's principle, which is about a set with zero length that still exists — the class-1 singleton at radius 2, length zero and still there — here nothing is about measure versus existence; the seeded counts, the pixel widths and the O(P^4) bound are about what the radius workflow was run against and what its checker costs, not about whether a zero-length set counts as existing.

None of this changes the radius answers. The entry covers the report counts and the Chrome counts; the seeded suites, the widths and the cost bound are the layer underneath — what the radius checks were actually run against, and what bounds them.

Case study: [hingelab.md](hingelab.md)

### FactorLab: the two parsers disagree before any arithmetic runs

[Case study](factorlab.md) · [Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/factorlab/)

The entry reports the native and BigInt implementations agreeing on 24 complete reports and 23,114 exact leaves, and it reads as if the two sides are interchangeable. They are, once a factor file is loaded — but the case study says the two front doors accept different files. Native JSON rejects duplicate keys, floating-point index tokens and anything beyond 32 KiB of UTF-8. Browser JSON.parse keeps the last duplicate key, accepts integer-valued numeric tokens such as 1.0, and bounds input at 32,768 characters. A file can be valid in one and rejected in the other before a single potential is multiplied.

The difference is about input, not arithmetic: potential arithmetic is exact in both, so the 23,114-leaf agreement holds over files both sides accept. And the two caps are not the same unit even though the numbers look equal — 32 KiB is bytes of UTF-8, 32,768 is characters — so a file near either limit can pass one and fail the other. The entry already covers invalid-input handling on the browser side, in the Chrome checks for invalid/stale clearing, but it never says where the split sits: a factor file the browser happily parses can still be refused by the native parser for a duplicate key or a 1.0 token.

Distinct from 'A sum is not a max', FactorLab's principle, which is about a hidden variable summed or maximized into a mass or a decision, with the two answers differing — here nothing is summed or maximized, and the question is what each parser lets through the door before any query runs, not which query the numbers answer.

None of this changes the inference answers. The entry covers the retained counterexample, the census and the cross-language agreement; the parser split is the layer underneath — what each side accepts before the arithmetic starts, and where a file can be valid in one and refused in the other.

Case study: [factorlab.md](factorlab.md)

### ArgumentLab: the six sources, named

[Method and six sources](https://github.com/midya8/argumentlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Case study](argumentlab.md)

The entry says "six inspected sources" and leaves it at that. They are worth naming, because the workbench is built on established semantics and the attribution is part of the work: Dung (1995) for the core defense and extension definitions; Baroni, Caminada and Giacomin (2011) and Toni (2016) for the extension and labelling distinctions; Charwat et al. (2015) for the range-based stage and semi-stable context; Rudolph (2024) for the classical conventions on querying an empty stable family; Dauphin and Schulz (2014) for the existing graphical and labelling educational work. The precise inspected sections and source URLs are in the method.

Naming them also draws the line the entry states but doesn't show: the theory and the prior tools are explicitly attributed, while the implementation and the synthetic controls are original. Nothing here is a new theorem or a novel solver — the case study says that plainly — and no external solver code, data or examples were copied, and there is no affiliation with the existing ArguLab or ARGTEACH tools.

Distinct from 'Removing an attack can remove an acceptance', ArgumentLab's first principle, which is about edits to the attack relation changing acceptance — here nothing is edited, the graph is fixed, and the question is where the definitions the workbench implements came from — and from 'A decomposition is not decided in advance', ArgumentLab's second principle, which is about components recomputed after each choice — here there is no recursion and no choice, the sources are fixed, and the question is attribution — and from 'A skeptical intersection is not a defensible position', ArgumentLab's third principle, which is about the parent intersection needing pruning before it defends its own members — here admissibility is already settled, and the question is which published work each definition traces to.

None of this changes the semantics. The entry covers the nine families, the witnesses, the labellings and the grounded chain; the sources are the layer underneath — whose definitions the workbench implements, and where a reader can check them.

Case study: [argumentlab.md](argumentlab.md)

### ConceptLab: the counts live in the method files, not the case study

[Method](https://github.com/midya8/conceptlab/blob/main/METHOD.md) · [Completion semantics](https://github.com/midya8/conceptlab/blob/main/PARTIAL.md) · [Exploration protocol](https://github.com/midya8/conceptlab/blob/main/EXPLORATION.md) · [Case study](conceptlab.md)

The case study describes the three modes and then stops short of the numbers: it says the method "retains individual query, lattice and witness counts" without printing any of them. The entry prints them, and they check out — but only if you open the method files the case study links. The complete-context corpus is in METHOD.md: 14 controls, 194 attribute subsets, 390 object subsets, 69 concepts, 30 canonical rules, 635 ordered lattice operations and 5,762 premise/conclusion query combinations. The completion stage is in PARTIAL.md: twelve controls over 86 completions, 438 implication queries, 4,634 query/completion witness rows, 63 goal subsets and seven minimal impossible goal sets. The exploration stage is in EXPLORATION.md, which names the release 0.3.0 and records the native tests enumerating all 6,654 expert-family/initial-observation combinations.

One comparison needs its framing kept, not just its value. The entry carries the earlier optional concepts 0.9.2 comparison, and the case study is explicit that it was not rerun for the documentation review and does not validate the exploration scheduler. EXPLORATION.md says the same thing in plainer terms: the external check compares support IDs and closures against that package's extent/intension operations, empty-axis requests are counted but excluded from it because that package requires nonempty axes, and it is a reference, not an oracle. It is a historical comparison, kept for the record — not a fresh check of the current scheduler.

Distinct from 'Unknown is not false', ConceptLab's first principle, which is about implications that hold in the two extreme fill-ins but fail in a world between them — here nothing is filled in and no implication is judged; the question is where the numbers the entry states can be checked, not what any of them means — and from 'A table is not an expert', ConceptLab's second principle, which is about an implication that fits every observed row and still fails in the supplied domain — here no row is observed or judged; the counts are about the retained corpus, not about whether any rule survives the expert table.

None of this changes the counts. The entry covers the corpus, the completions and the exploration audit; the method files are the layer underneath — where each number lives, and where a reader can verify it without rerunning the suite.

### Correlab: the original verification's counts live upstream, not in the case study

[Design and proofs](https://github.com/midya8/correlab/blob/main/DESIGN.md) · [Checks and limits](https://github.com/midya8/correlab/blob/main/README.md) · [Case study](correlab.md)

The case study prints the current suite — 50 tests — and the headline 2,835 game/law cases, then stops. It never says what those cases are made of. The entry gives the breakdown, and I checked it against the files the case study links: the original verification is 33 tests covering 2,835 combinations of all 81 ternary 2x2 row payoff tables with 35 quarter-grid joint laws, the column table varied separately, checked against exhaustive swap-map and one-pair enumeration. DESIGN.md states it in one sentence; the README's checks section says the same thing from the other side, leading with the 2,835 cases and naming the 81 tables and 35 laws behind them.

The rest of the original layer is in the same two files. The 28 rectangular and larger controls reach 256 maps per player, all 256 length-four 2x2 histories are checked at three prefixes, and three figure tests bind the obedience figure's 36 numeric cells with the full compressed report, hidden-term tampering rejected and byte-exact regeneration required. None of that is in the case study either — it reports the current 50-test suite and moves on, which is fair for a case study, but it means the entry's original-verification paragraph has no anchor in the file it points to.

Distinct from 'A recommendation changes the deviation', Correlab's first principle, which is about which deviations the audit allows — here the audit is fixed and the question is where the numbers behind it can be checked — and from 'Coarsening a signal costs exactly its slacks', Correlab's second principle, which is about what coarsening an observation costs — here nothing is coarsened; the counts are about the retained verification, not about what any signal reveals.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the original verification and the private-signal extension; the design file and the README's checks section sit underneath it, where each number can be checked without rerunning anything.
