### Kurdistan International Sociological Association website: the site costs about ten dollars a year to run

[Deploy runbook](https://github.com/midya8/kurdistansociology-website/blob/main/DEPLOY.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/kurdistansociology-website/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](kurdistansociology-website.md)

The case study says the site is a static export served from Cloudflare and that pushing to main redeploys it. The runbook puts a number on that: the domain runs about $10.44 a year from Cloudflare Registrar, and everything else — Pages hosting, DNS, and a single-user mailbox on the association's domain — sits on free tiers. The total is roughly ten dollars a year. That is the static-export decision stated as a bill rather than an architecture claim: there is no server line item because there is no server to keep up.

The push-to-deploy path has a gate in front of it. CI runs on every push to main and every pull request — `npm ci`, then lint, then the full build — on a Node version pinned by `.node-version`, with read-only permissions. The same push triggers the Pages build, with build command `npm run build` and output directory `out`, so a broken build fails in CI and never becomes a deployment. The custom domain is attached after the first deploy lands.

One detail the case study doesn't mention: the config does more than label the languages. Sorani's `htmlLang` is `ckb`, not `ku` — the locale code in the URL stays `ku`, but the page tells the browser the language is Central Kurdish, and its direction is `rtl` while Kurmanji's is `ltr`. English is the default locale, and the root page sends visitors to `/en`. Direction and language identity live in one typed config, so a page can't be served with the wrong language tag or the wrong reading direction.

Distinct from 'Choose the simplest architecture that meets the requirement', which is about picking the architecture — editorial content gets a static export, not a server — here the architecture is already chosen and the question is what running it actually costs and what stands between a push and the live site. The bill and the CI gate are the consequence of that choice, not the choice itself.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the trilingual structure, the static export and the file-based publishing; the runbook and the CI workflow sit underneath it, where the cost and the deploy path can be checked without taking the case study's word for it.

Case study: [kurdistansociology-website.md](kurdistansociology-website.md)

### midya.ca: the build checks the types before it ships anything

[Deploy config](https://github.com/midya8/midya.ca/blob/main/.do/app.yaml) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/midya.ca/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](midya-ca.md)

The case study says the site is "typed and checked before it ships" and leaves it there. The check is really two gates, and the second one contains the first. CI runs on every push to main and every pull request, on Node 22, and runs `npm ci`, then `npm run typecheck`, then `npm run build`. The build script itself starts with `tsc -b`, so a type error fails the build before Vite ever runs — the separate typecheck step in CI runs the same `tsc -b` check on its own, before the build. Nothing untyped can reach the deploy step, because the deploy step is the build.

The deploy side is just as plain. App Platform rebuilds on push — `npm ci && npm run build`, output `dist` — in the Toronto region, with midya.ca as the primary domain and www.midya.ca as an alias. The index and the catchall are both `index.html`, so a deep link or a refresh on a client-side route serves the app instead of a 404. There is no server step in either file: the whole path from push to live is install, check, build, serve static files.

Distinct from 'The scene is generated, not shipped', which is about the galaxy having no image assets to break — here the assets are fine and the question is what stands between a push and the live site — and from 'One content file', which is about copy living apart from the scene — here the copy is already separate and the question is whether a bad edit can ship, which the typecheck inside the build answers.

None of this changes what the site looks like. The entry covers the galaxy, the adaptive profiles and the reduced-motion path; the CI workflow and the deploy config are the layer underneath — what has to pass before a push becomes the live site, and what serves it once it does.

Case study: [midya-ca.md](midya-ca.md)

### sre-toolkit: the flags reshape the report, not the math

[Commands and exit status](https://github.com/midya8/sre-toolkit/blob/main/README.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/sre-toolkit/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](sre-toolkit.md)

The case study's command table says what each command returns. The README's table says the same thing with one extra column: exit status. `budget` exits 1 when the budget is exhausted, `report` exits 1 if any budget is exhausted, `burn` exits 1 when a critical rule fires, and `metrics --action-items` exits 1 when a resolved incident older than `--stale-days` — 14 days by default — still has open items. The case study states all of this in prose under "Run the examples"; the README puts it in the table, per command, so someone wiring a command into a script doesn't have to read the prose to know which exit code means what.

The rest of the README's CLI list is flags that change the shape of the output without touching the math. `triage --top-talkers` lists the noisiest deduped alerts first; `--by-severity` counts active alerts per severity; `--rules` swaps in your own keyword vocabulary. `report --summary` rolls the fleet up to status counts and the worst SLO. `metrics --by-service` breaks TTD/TTR down per affected service. None of these change severity classification, dedupe, or the burn rules — the 14.4x page and 6x ticket defaults stay put — they decide what the operator sees first.

The README also names the pieces. Its layout lists the eight working modules with one line each — `error_budget`, `alert_triage`, `silences`, `postmortem`, `burn_alerts`, `report`, `slo_doc`, and `__main__` — so the import surface is visible without opening the package. The Tests section says what the 254 tests cover, from boundary and validation cases in the budget math through silence matching and expiry, burn replay, and action-item follow-through. CI runs that suite plus a compile check on Python 3.10 through 3.13, on every push to main and every pull request.

Distinct from 'Alert rules and their limits', which is about what the rules can and can't promise — here nothing is promised or limited; the flags don't change the rules, only the report — and from 'Reproducible checks', which is about proving a checkout behaves the same — here the question is what the commands report, not whether they reproduce.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the commands, the rules and the checks; the README's table and the flag list sit underneath it — the exit-status contract and the reporting options, where a reader can check what a command returns before wiring it into a script.

Case study: [sre-toolkit.md](sre-toolkit.md)

### loglens: three ways in, one record out

[Usage and flags](https://github.com/midya8/loglens/blob/main/README.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/loglens/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](loglens.md)

The case study's commands all start from a file. The README's usage list shows two more ways in: `-` reads from stdin, so `python -m loglens - < app.log` pipes a live stream straight in, and `--format json` forces one parser where the default `--format auto` detects the format per line. The third way in is the library: `parse_lines` from `loglens.parse`, and `error_rate`, `latency_summary`, `top_paths`, `top_error_ips` and `percentile` from `loglens.stats` — the importable functions the entry mentions without naming. All three feed the same `LogRecord`, so the statistics never care which way the lines arrived.

The README also names the pieces. Its layout lists the eight modules with one line each — `parse.py` for both parsers, `stats.py` for rates, distributions, top-N and percentiles, `timeline.py` for buckets and timestamp parsing, `filter.py` for time windows, `compare.py` for before/after, `apdex.py` for scoring overall and per path, `report.py` for the Markdown incident report, and `__main__.py` for the CLI summary. The field sources are spelled out too: durations come from JSON `duration_ms`, nginx `request_time`, combined `$request_time` appended, or a trailing fractional number read as seconds; sizes come from JSON `size_bytes`, `body_bytes_sent`, `bytes` or `size`. Logs without durations skip the latency section; logs without sizes print a note instead of zeros.

The case study's question table names the flags; the README adds what they do with the numbers. `--by-hour` buckets the whole window by UTC hour of day, so a nightly batch job or a lunch peak stands out in a multi-day log. `--throughput` rolls the timeline into mean and peak per-bucket throughput. `--errors` lists the failing requests themselves, capped by `--top` with a "+N more" note. `--by-path` takes `--sort requests|p95` to rank by volume or latency instead of error count. And the README states the Apdex formula the case study only describes: (satisfied + tolerating/2) / samples, with the bands at T and 4T. None of these add a new measurement — they decide which slice of the same records you look at first.

Distinct from 'A gate with nothing to measure must fail, not pass', which is about what the gates do when there is nothing to measure — here every gate already has its measurement, and the question is which way the records arrived and which slice gets shown — and from 'Make tools that fit scripts as well as terminals', which is about the CLI and the library being the same tool — here that claim is made concrete: the named functions, the eight modules, the exact fields each parser reads.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the questions, the limits and the gates; the README's usage list and layout sit underneath it — the ways in and the pieces, where a reader can check how a log gets from a file, a pipe or an import to the same record.

Case study: [loglens.md](loglens.md)

### flowkit: the tail is the report, the log is the record

[Usage and flags](https://github.com/midya8/flowkit/blob/main/README.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/flowkit/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](flowkit.md)

The case study says the reports include the tails. The README adds the other side: a failed step always prints its tail under its report line, and `--show-output` prints successful steps' tails too, while the `--json` summary and the `--report` file both carry an `output` field per step. `--output-dir DIR` appends each attempt's full output to `DIR/<step>.log` — step names sanitized to safe file names — and the JSON report points at the file through `output_file`. The tail stays in memory; the whole run goes to disk.

The README also gives the numbers the case study states without them. `--backoff` defaults to 0.5s and doubles on each attempt, and `timeout_seconds` still bounds each attempt, a timeout consuming one. A `when` condition is `{"when": {"env": "DEPLOY", "equals": "yes"}}` or `{"when": {"env": "DRY", "set": false}}`, evaluated against the merged environment at run time. `--strict-env` turns a typo'd variable into a pre-run error that names the step and the variable, and that case exits 2 — the same code as an unreadable or invalid flow file. A malformed interpolation, like an unclosed `${`, is rejected at load time instead of expanding to something surprising.

The importable side has names too. `from flowkit.model import load_flow` and `from flowkit.runner import run_flow` run the same flow the CLI runs, and `result.status` reads `"succeeded"` or `"failed"`. The layout is four files: `model.py` for flow definitions, validation and topological order, `runner.py` for sequential execution, retries, timeouts and skip semantics, `report.py` for the text and JSON summaries, and `__main__.py` for the CLI — with `tests/` holding the unittest suite and `examples/` the sample flows. CI runs that suite plus a compile check on Python 3.10 through 3.13, on every push to main and every pull request.

Distinct from 'Make a failure explain itself', which is about a failed step's tail travelling with its verdict — here the point is what the run keeps for the steps that passed, and where the whole record lives — and from 'Make tools that fit scripts as well as terminals', which is about the CLI and the library being the same tool — here that claim is made concrete: the two import names, the four modules, the exact flags and their defaults.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the DAG, the retries, the hooks and the checks; the README's flags, conditions and layout sit underneath it — the defaults and the import surface, where a reader can check what a run keeps before wiring it into a script.

### uptimekit: the exit code is the alert

[Config and exit codes](https://github.com/midya8/uptimekit/blob/main/README.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/uptimekit/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](uptimekit.md)

The case study says invalid configuration is rejected before probing. The README gives the rules behind that sentence. An `http` target needs a `url`; `tcp` and `tls` need a `host` and a `port`. `expect_status` defaults to 200, `keyword` is optional, and the example sets `timeout` to 5. `max_latency_ms` defaults to 0, which means off. Unknown keys and missing fields are rejected with exit 2 — the same code flowkit uses for an unreadable flow file — so a typo in the config fails before a single probe runs, not after the first one times out.

`check` exits 1 when any target fails, so a cron line doubles as the alerting path; the README even shows the cron line piping the failure to mail. `--json` gives the same result machine-readable. `report` takes `--window 24h` or `7d`, `--trend` adds the per-day strip, and `--compare` diffs the window against the one right before it, so a regression reads as a regression instead of just a lower number. `watch` is the fallback for hosts without cron: `--interval 300` probes every five minutes, `--iterations N` stops after N rounds, and Ctrl-C stops cleanly with a summary.

The status page gets one more detail the case study doesn't mention: a 30-check tick strip per target, and targets come from `--config` when given, otherwise from the history itself. `examples/status.html` is a rendered example you can open. The layout is five files — `probes.py` for the target specs and the three probe kinds with config validation, `history.py` for the append-only JSONL store and retention, `availability.py` for the roll-ups, incident detection and current state, `statuspage.py` for the renderer, and `__main__.py` for the CLI — with `tests/` holding the unittest suite and `examples/` the sample config. CI is the smallest of the four SRE tools: Python 3.11 only, a compile check, then the suite, on every push to main and every pull request.

Distinct from 'Prefer a scheduled check over a service to keep alive', which is about nothing running as a daemon — here the point is what the one-shot check returns, and that the failure path needs no daemon either: exit 1 from `check`, exit 2 from a bad config, and a cron line as the whole alerting path — and from 'Validate before executing', which is about a latency assertion or a TLS threshold turning a quiet problem into a failure — here the validation happens before the monitoring does: the config itself is checked, field by field, before anything is probed.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the probes, the history, the incidents and the gates; the README's config rules, the compare and trend flags, and the layout sit underneath it — the part a reader checks before pointing it at a real service.

### RatingLab: the wheel is checked outside the checkout

[Reproduce](https://github.com/midya8/ratinglab/blob/main/README.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/ratinglab/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](ratinglab.md)

I wanted the reproduction to be something a reader can actually run, not a claim they take on trust. It starts with `python -m pip install -e .`, then `OPENBLAS_NUM_THREADS=1 python -m unittest discover -s tests -v` — the thread pin is in the command itself, and CI pins it the same way. From there the commands are the checks themselves: `python -m ratinglab report`, `audit`, `fit --output fresh.json`, `audit --report fresh.json`, `controls`, `witness --side item --index 84`, `export`, then `python examples/figure.py` and `node --test tests/*.cjs`. Python 3.10 or newer, NumPy 2.2.6, and everything runs offline once installed; the browser check is the only part that wants more, Chrome and Node 22 or newer, with `CHROME_BIN` set when the binary is not on the path.

The part I like most is the wheel check, because it runs outside the checkout. `python -m pip wheel . --no-deps -w /tmp/wheels`, install that wheel into `/tmp/installed`, and `bash examples/verify_wheel.sh /tmp/installed "$PWD"` imports all ten modules including the package root and exercises the eight main CLI commands plus the two single-row module commands against the installed copy — all eleven fresh fits, and the complete saved and fresh fit audits. The browser exporter does not fit, so that boundary is explicit too. `fit --budget 0` deliberately preserves an unfinished initial state, so the suite also covers the run where fitting never gets to run.

Prediction takes the same strictness. `predict` reads pairs like `{"pairs":[[0,0],[137,129]]}` — known stable user and item indices, not source identifiers or anything about the people behind them. Unknown indices, booleans, fractional indices, duplicate keys, nonfinite JSON and extra fields all fail, before anything is scored. Raw scores can fall outside [0,2]; clipping is reported separately and does not decide which model is selected. And for the avoidance of doubt, this is not restaurant advice.

Distinct from 'A missing rating is not a zero', which is about what the objective sums over while fitting, and from 'Edits add; deletions couple', which is about how batch responses compose inside a frozen block — here nothing is fitted and nothing is edited; the question is whether the same commands reproduce the same numbers from an installed wheel in a fresh directory.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the factorization, the controls, and the conditional and batch inspectors; the commands, the wheel check, and the strict input sit underneath it — the part a reader runs before trusting any of it.

Case study: [ratinglab.md](ratinglab.md)

### SpectralLab: two functions under the command line

[Reproduce](https://github.com/midya8/spectrallab/blob/main/README.md) · [CI](https://github.com/midya8/spectrallab/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) · [Case study](spectrallab.md)

The case study talks about the fits and the failures, but the code underneath is two small functions, and they are the part I would actually reuse. `graph` takes a finite feature matrix and builds the affinity — full RBF, union-kNN or mutual-kNN — with explicit zero self-loops, and kNN ties go to the lower original row index, so duplicates can be index-sensitive. `spectral` takes the affinity and does the rest: the symmetric normalized Laplacian, its two smallest eigenvectors including the trivial mode, unit-row normalization, and manual two-cluster k-means. NumPy supplies `eigh`/LAPACK, array arithmetic and random numbers — nothing else. Four points are enough to see the shape of it:

```python
from spectrallab import graph, spectral
weights = graph([[0, 0], [0, 1], [3, 0], [3, 1]], kind="union", neighbors=1)
fit = spectral(weights)
print(fit["status"], fit.get("fit", {}).get("labels"))
```

The contract on `spectral` is strict about what it accepts: a symmetric, nonnegative affinity with a zero diagonal. What it refuses is just as explicit — isolated vertices, more than two components, a retained-boundary eigengap at most 1e-10, or a zero embedding row — and it refuses rather than inventing edges or truncating a nullspace arbitrarily.

The command line is a thin wrapper over the same two functions. `python -m pip install .`, then `python -m spectrallab fit --output /tmp/spectral-report.json`, `audit` on that report, and `export` to a web directory; the sensitivity side has its own three, `python -m spectrallab.sensitivity build sensitivity.json`, `audit`, and `export examples/sensitivity.json > docs/sensitivity-data.js`. The suite runs offline — Python 3.10 or newer, NumPy 1.24 or newer, no SciPy, no sklearn, no model API — with `python -m unittest discover -s tests -v` and `node --test tests/*.cjs`, and `docs/index.html` opens directly or serves with `python -m http.server --directory docs`.

And the boundary that matters most: clustering here is full-data. Adding a point changes the graph and can change every assignment, so there is no inductive predict method — no fitting on one set and scoring a new point later. If you want labels for a new point, you rebuild the graph with it in.

Distinct from 'The graph decides the answer before the eigensolver runs', which is about which graph gets chosen before any solver runs — here nothing is chosen; the question is what the two functions accept, refuse, and return once the graph exists. And distinct from the relaxation paragraph inside that same principle, which is about the fitted cut versus the exact optimum — here nothing is fitted against an optimum; the question is the interface.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the fits, the failures, and the sensitivity work; the functions, the commands, and the refusals sit underneath it — the part a reader uses before trusting any of it.

Case study: [spectrallab.md](spectrallab.md)

### MarginLab: the repair is a separate calculation, not a fix to the model

[Case study](marginlab.md) · [Live 30-feature inspector](https://midya8.github.io/marginlab/) · [Live exact linear proof inspector](https://midya8.github.io/marginlab/certificate.html)

The part of MarginLab I keep coming back to is the repair — not because the model was wrong, but because the exact check asks for something the saved floats cannot give. The pair solver's coefficients are fine as binary64 numbers; the trouble only shows up when you ask for an exact rational equality, because the saved coefficients carry nonzero exact signed dual mass. So the certificate extension does not touch the model at all. It runs a separate calculation: convert the represented binary64 inputs to Fractions, clip to the box, and reduce coefficients in the excess class until the equality is exactly zero — and it writes down the original residual and the total movement, so you can see what the repair cost.

Then the bound comes from two objects that are both exactly feasible: a primal built from the same represented problem, and the repaired dual. Together they enclose each represented linear training optimum, and the gap readouts — 7.97e-8, 1.42e-6, 2.90e-5 and 3.23e-4 for C=.01, .1, 1, 10 — are decimals, not the bounds; the bounds are the full canonical objective ratios. What makes me trust that is who checks it: two readers written independently, one in Python with Fraction and one in the browser with BigInt, checking every constraint, objective and the repair accounting without ever importing the optimizer. Sixty more active-face exact optima have to land inside the produced intervals, and the browser checks all four full proofs against every original standardized input, label, C, coefficient and bias.

Distinct from 'A valid proof does not authenticate its source', MarginLab's first principle, which is about a proved bound saying nothing about where the proved object came from — here the question is not provenance but what the repair leaves alone: the model is unchanged, and the proof is about the represented problem the coefficients already describe — and from 'A perfect training score is not a finished optimization', MarginLab's second principle, which is about accuracy hitting 100% while the solver's gap is still 2/3 — here nothing is scored and nothing is still running; the equality either holds exactly after the repair or it does not — and from 'A sample is not the range', MarginLab's third principle, which is about points inside a box against the box's attained range — here no range is claimed; the bound is a pair of feasible objectives enclosing one optimum, not a sample of anything.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the solver, the replay, the controls and the boxes; the repair is the part underneath it — the separate calculation that makes an exact statement possible without rewriting the model that earned the numbers.

Case study: [marginlab.md](marginlab.md)

### ScanLab: a shorter chain is not less work

[Case study](scanlab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/scanlab)

The scan is shorter and more expensive at the same time, and that is the part worth slowing down for. The exact 16-token, two-state fixture uses 26 pair compositions, and the unit vector composition/apply span drops from 16 to 7 — but scalar multiplications rise from 32 to 136 and additions from 32 to 84. Those are actual DAG counts on supplied pairs, not runtime, not GPU/FLOPs, not fraction bit complexity, and both paths execute serial Python. The dense v0.3 extension does the same thing at the next level: unit-pair span 4 versus 6, treating whole matrix operations as units, while the six-token, two-state fixture uses seven matrix-matrix products and thirteen matrix-vector products — 108 multiplications and 80 additions against the sequential 24 and 24. A shorter dependency chain is not less arithmetic; it is the same arithmetic reorganized, and the counts say exactly how much more.

So how do you know the reorganized arithmetic is still right? Not by tolerance. The v0.2 extension records the full forward/tangent/reverse/loss-reverse/HVP-reverse scan DAGs, and the exact transposition identity is -817/120 on both sides; a wrong reverse-index control that uses the current rather than the next decay gives 139/360, with gap -259/36. The v0.3 dense extension does it again with full rational transition matrices: the exact transposition identity is -440563/15120 on both sides, omitting only the transpose in reverse propagation gives gap 7831/2520, and reversing only matrix-product order, with correct write composition, changes the final state from [9/2,187/36] to [-14/9,2/3]. These are exact rational equalities, not agreements within epsilon — the identity either holds or it does not.

The part that makes me trust the tests is what the mistakes hide behind. Both v0.3 mistakes become invisible under diagonal restrictions, which are tested separately — 27 of them in the v0.3 suite, alongside 216 associativity cases and 672 full-polynomial VJP parameter coordinates across 36 models. Under a diagonal restriction neither mistake shows up at all, so the suite has to go dense to catch it. The same instinct is in the v0.1 failure controls: the floating cancellation control writes [1e16,1,-1e16,1] with decay 1, where the sequential final state is 1, the scan is 0 and the exact rational result is 2; and the input-gate control gives final f(2x)-2f(x) about 0.20243342, so one fixed linear convolution cannot represent that model on all inputs. Neither failing control is turned into a tolerance-based success — they stay failing, on the record, as the reason the exact path exists.

Distinct from 'An input-dependent recurrence is not a fixed convolution', the ScanLab principle in approach.md, which is about the fixed diagonal LTI recurrence equaling direct causal convolution exactly while the separate sigmoid-gated recurrence stays fully inspectable — here nothing is equated with a convolution; the question is what the shorter chain costs in arithmetic and how an exact rational identity certifies the reorganized pass — and from the entry itself, which states the counts and the identities; the subsection is the reasoning underneath them: span is a property of the dependency graph, work is a property of the arithmetic, and an exact rational identity is what lets you trust the reorganized version.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the mechanism, the controls, the counts and the three versions; the span/work distinction is the part underneath it — the reason a scan that looks cheaper on the chain is more expensive in the arithmetic, and the reason the proof is an exact fraction rather than a tolerance.

Case study: [scanlab.md](scanlab.md)

### GameLab: the value is stable, the strategy is not

[Case study](gamelab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/gamelab)

The payoff-change audit gives you a bound on a number: with epsilon the maximum entry change, the value changes by at most epsilon and the old equilibrium pair has new gap at most two epsilon. Those are deterministic supplied-table bounds, not confidence intervals, and they are the reason you can trust the value to move slowly. But the strategy does not move slowly. A two-row/one-column payoff flip of 10^-12 changes the unique optimal row from [0,1] to [1,0] with the value unchanged. So "the game barely changed" is true about the number and false about the policy — the value is stable, the strategy is discontinuous, and a reader who only looks at the value will miss the jump entirely.

That is why the audit keeps the whole optimal face instead of one strategy. The exact-rational security-LP enumerator tries every active basis, at most 70 per player for a 4x4 table, and every singular, infeasible and feasible attempt retains rank, determinant, pivots, all slacks and violations. Feasible vertices merge duplicate bases, and all optimal vertices describe the full optimal face. The representative is a deterministic equal average of distinct optimal vertices, supplied with pure-action payoff, best-reply, probability, primal/dual and complementary-slackness witnesses — not a uniquely preferred or maximum-entropy policy. Coordinate extrema include attaining vertices, and their ranges are not independent choices. The face is the honest object; the representative is one point in it, chosen by a rule you can see.

The failure worth seeing is rock-paper-scissors. It has value zero and one-third mixed policies, and both fixed action-zero policies also yield payoff zero — but their unilateral gains sum to two, so a zero observed payoff is not an equilibrium certificate. The same lesson shows up in the 4x2 game with duplicate rows: the optimal row vertices [0,1/2,1/2,0] and [1/2,0,1/2,0] make every convex combination optimal, the fourth action is dominated, and the representative [1/4,1/4,1/2,0] preserves the face rather than pretending it is unique. A constant 2x3 table has every policy optimal, and a separate nonzero game independently checks value 3/2 with row policy [1/4,3/4]. When the value is one number and the optimal strategies form a continuum, writing down one strategy and calling it "the" solution would be the fiction — the audit writes down the face instead.

Distinct from 'When two sides face the same unknown, the worst case for each side is not a worst case for both', the GameLab principle in approach.md, which is about a fixed policy pair facing one unknown payoff table — the row's worst deviation needing entry zero while the column's needs entry two, so the shared worst gap is one while their incompatible separate maxima sum to two — here nothing is unknown and nothing is shared; the table is fixed and known, and the question is what stays still when the table moves a little: the value does, the strategy may not — and from the entry itself, which states the bounds, the enumerator and the controls; the subsection is the reasoning underneath them: value stability is a bound on a number, strategy stability is a different question, and the audit answers it by keeping every vertex.

So the entry stays as written. It covers the enumerator, the witnesses, the controls and the interval extension; the value/strategy distinction is the part underneath it — the reason the audit keeps the whole face when one number would have been easier to quote.

Case study: [gamelab.md](gamelab.md)

### BetLab: the average is the test, not the best component

[Case study](betlab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/betlab)

The fixed mixture runs five bets at once — [0,1/4,1/2,3/4,1] with equal positive weights by default — and the test is the average of their wealths, not the largest. Each component is its own wealth process with its own capital, including permanent ruin at zero, and the average of supermartingales is still a supermartingale, so Ville's inequality still bounds the probability of ever reaching 1/alpha by alpha. The maximum is not. A component that happens to be winning can have conditional expectation above one once you condition on it being the winner, and the case study says it plainly: their maximum is not substituted for their average.

That distinction is doing the work behind the 5/64. On the eight-round fair-coin null tree, the fixed mixture crosses threshold 4 with probability 5/64 and the past-only heuristic with 9/128, both with expected final and stopped wealth exactly 1 — those are probabilities for the average and for one fixed past-only rule, not for the best of either. If you ran both, watched, and reported whichever crossed first, you would be running two tests and picking the winner, and the case study does not cover that: selecting the winning test after running several is not covered, and the two families are separate tests, not an automatically corrected union.

The same rule governs the start allocation. One unit is split across fixed starts before the stream is seen — at most 16 starts in 1..256, positive weights summing exactly to one — and the complete statistic is a fixed convex mixture, not the largest start-specific result. The delayed-signal control shows why the distinction matters in both directions: bet one with starts 1, 9, 17 at equal weights first crosses threshold 4 at round 12 and ends at zero, while the one-start control is permanently ruined at round one. Ruin is real for a component and final for that component; the mixture survives it only because the other components still hold capital, and the average is what the crossing probability describes.

Distinct from 'A free restart is not optional stopping', the BetLab principle in approach.md, which is about whether a ruined test may be topped up with fresh capital — here nothing is ruined and nothing is topped up; every component runs on its original capital and the question is whether you report their average or their best — and from 'A running interval must remember what it excluded', which is about a confidence sequence retaining earlier exclusions as bounds — here no interval is built and nothing is retained; the question is what single number the test reports at each round, not what its interval remembers — and from 'A zero average is not a valid null', which is about the null holding at every round rather than on average — here the average is over wealth processes, not over differences, and the question is which average is a valid test, not whether an average difference is a valid null — and from the entry itself, which states the mixture, the heuristic, the restart failure and the start allocation; the subsection is the reasoning underneath them: the average is the test because the average is still a supermartingale, and the maximum is not.

So the entry stays as written. It states the mixture, the heuristic, the restart failure and the start allocation; the average-versus-maximum distinction is the part underneath them — the reason the crossing probabilities attach to the average and not to whichever component looks best afterwards.

Case study: [betlab.md](betlab.md)

### SparseLab: a feature can leave and come back with the opposite sign

[Case study](sparselab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/sparselab)

The developed centered four-row fixture has columns a and 2a+b, response 2a-b, Gram [[1,2],[2,5]] and cross [2,3]. Its five supplied branches cover [3,infinity),[4/3,3],[1,4/3],[1/3,1],[0,1/3]. Feature 1 enters positive at 3, feature 0 enters at 4/3, feature 1 leaves at 1 and re-enters negative at 1/3. Adjacent knots agree exactly in coefficients, predictions, objectives and dual vectors; the zero-penalty endpoint is [4,-1]. The positive-definite Gram makes this fixture's coefficient path unique, not a general uniqueness claim — the surprise is not that two fits disagree, but that one feature's sign changes as the penalty falls.

The extension verifies that behavior with exact affine KKT branches, not a dense grid of approximate fits, and the grid is the thing to distrust here: a grid could step over the re-entry or place it between two grid points and call it noise. The entire interval claim comes from affine inequalities, not endpoint sampling alone. The caller supplies one weak sign pattern. Rational Gram solves give beta(lambda), and the complete selected-sign/inactive-correlation inequalities give its maximal closed nonnegative-lambda interval. A feature can leave the nonzero support as regularization falls, then return with the opposite sign — and here the return is at exactly 1/3, not somewhere between two grid points.

The weak-sign discipline is what keeps the branches honest. Selected signs are weak: boundary zeros are allowed and are not a recovered support. An infeasible pattern returns a noncertificate with its conflicting bounds; a zero-lambda singleton is a valid point without a strict-sign interior. Selecting both duplicate columns instead returns singular_active_gram, not a fabricated ridge solution or proof of infeasibility. Selecting either duplicate separately yields different coefficients and identical predictions — the same ambiguity the first principle names, seen from the branch side rather than the solver side.

Distinct from 'A unique prediction does not identify a coefficient', SparseLab's first principle in approach.md, which is about duplicate columns giving different selected features with the same prediction — here the fixture's Gram is positive-definite, so the coefficient path is unique, and the question is what one feature does along that path, not which of two fits to believe — and from 'A sufficient certificate is not a complete detector', SparseLab's second principle, which is about a one-sided certificate staying silent on features it cannot clear — here there is no certificate at all, only the complete branch enumeration, and the question is what the path does, not what a screen can prove — and from the entry itself, which states the five branches and their knots; the subsection is the reasoning underneath them: the branches are exact because they come from the KKT inequalities, and the re-entry is real because the knots are exact.

So the entry stays as written. It states the five branches, the knots, the endpoint and the weak-sign rules; the drop and re-entry are the part underneath them — the reason a feature leaving the model as the penalty falls is not the end of its story.

Case study: [sparselab.md](sparselab.md)

### ErgodLab: an even block has nothing left to vary

[Case study](ergodlab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/ergodlab)

Two symmetric chains, both with state rewards 0 and 1, have the same one-step stationary reward variance: 1/4. That is where the agreement ends. A chain that stays in the same state with probability 7/8 has asymptotic reward-sum variance per step 7/4 — seven times the one-step figure. A chain that alternates deterministically has zero. The alternating chain has period two, so its exact stationary block-sum variance is zero for even lengths and 1/4 for odd lengths, and the figure shows all lengths 1..16 with both exact limits on the same axis. These are prescribed analytical fixtures, not training results or benchmark trials.

The even block has nothing left to vary. Alternation forces exactly half the steps into each state, so the reward sum is exactly half the block length no matter where the block starts — same sum, zero variance, nothing to estimate. The odd block has one degree of freedom left: one state gets an extra step, and which one is decided by the starting state alone. Under the stationary start that is a coin flip, so the sum is (L-1)/2 or (L+1)/2 with equal probability, and the variance is exactly 1/4 — the whole number comes from the starting state, not from anything that happens inside the block. The staying chain is the mirror image: each step usually repeats the last reward, so the block sums spread out instead of cancelling, and the long-run variance lands at 7/4 against the same one-step 1/4.

The audit does not estimate any of this from samples. A centered Poisson solve returns `h` satisfying `(I-P)h=r-rho` and `mu h=0`, and for every positive edge the report returns the centered martingale increment `D=R-rho+h(next)-h(state)` and its nonnegative variance contribution, whose stationary second moment is the asymptotic variance of centered reward sums per step. Exact conditional first/second-moment dynamic programs return every finite-block variance through the requested length, without enumerating exponentially many paths. The checks cover the alternating chain through length 32 — past the figure's 1..16 — plus period-three chains and zero-asymptotic-variance telescoping edge rewards, and periodicity is computed and reported, not silently ruled out, because the even/odd split is a period-two effect and ruling it out would erase the example.

Distinct from 'A one-step quantity is not a long-run quantity', ErgodLab's principle in approach.md, which states the two chains and the even/odd split as the gap itself — here the question is why the split takes exactly those values: the even block is forced, and the odd block's only freedom is the extra step — and from 'A mean is not the distribution', which is about a summary dropping the spread — here nothing is summarized: the block variance is computed exactly at every length, through 32 in the checks, so the even/odd split is a computed fact, not a summary artifact — and from the entry itself, which states the two chains, the split and the figure; the subsection is the counting underneath them: an even block has no variance because its counts are forced, and an odd block has exactly 1/4 because its only freedom is the starting state.

So the entry stays as written. It states the two chains, the 7/4 against zero, the even/odd split and the figure; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason an even block has nothing left to vary and an odd block's variance is exactly the starting state.

Case study: [ergodlab.md](ergodlab.md)

### DPPLab: repulsion narrows the size, it doesn't just forbid a pair

[Case study](dpplab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/dpplab)

The entry states the mean subset size, 25/18, and its variance, 113/324, as bare figures. Both come straight out of the three inclusion probabilities, with no fitting anywhere. The mean is just the marginals added up: 4/9 + 4/9 + 1/2 = 25/18, the trace of the inclusion kernel. The variance is where the repulsion actually shows up. Each item on its own contributes its Bernoulli variance — 20/81 for item 0, 20/81 for item 1, 1/4 for item 2 — and those three alone sum to 241/324. The reported variance is 113/324, less than half that. The difference is the covariance, and there is only one nonzero term in it.

Items 0 and 1 never appear together, so their covariance is 0 − 16/81 = −16/81, and it enters the variance twice, subtracting 128/324. Items 0 and 2 do appear together: the principal minor [[4,0],[0,1]] has determinant 4, so their joint inclusion is 4/18 = 2/9, exactly (4/9)(1/2), and the covariance is exactly zero — the same for items 1 and 2. That is item 2's independence stated as a covariance rather than a marginal: it is independent not because its inclusion probability is 1/2, but because its joint probabilities factor exactly. The mean cannot see any of this — independent items with the same marginals would have the same mean, 25/18 — but their size variance would be 241/324 instead of 113/324. Repulsion narrows the size distribution; the zero joint probability is the headline, and the shrunken variance is the same fact measured on the whole subset.

The audit does not estimate any of this from samples. Native Fraction elimination and Gauss-Jordan inversion compute every subset weight, the normalizer and the inclusion kernel exactly, and the independent BigInt JavaScript recomputes the same laws with permutation determinants and adjugate inverses; the two agree on 24 complete reports and 4,050 exact leaves. The checks behind those numbers include 120 determinant references, 50 inverse identities, 140 full Gram-kernel oracles and 100 conditioning oracles in the 24 Python tests, with actual Chrome verifying 252 subset rows, 786 matrix cells and 504 bar fraction/geometry witnesses. A variance that came out of sampling could hide a fudged covariance; here every term is an exact fraction, so the −16/81 is a computed fact, not a fitted one.

Distinct from 'Two items can have the same inclusion probability and never appear together', DPPLab's principle in approach.md, which states the marginals-versus-joint gap itself — the joint inclusion is zero while the marginals match the independent story — here the question is where that gap lands in the subset size: the mean does not move at all, and the variance drops from 241/324 to 113/324, all of it through one covariance — and from the entry itself, which states the mean and variance as results; the subsection is the arithmetic underneath them: the variance is 113/324 because the only nonzero covariance is −16/81, and item 2's independence is visible as two covariances that are exactly zero.

So the entry stays as written. It states the control, the mean and variance, the conditioning rules and the checks; the decomposition is the part underneath them — the reason the subset size varies less than the marginals alone would suggest, and the exact place the repulsion shows up.

### CutLab: two free labels are not two free choices

[Case study](cutlab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/cutlab)

The entry states two fixtures side by side: in a tied agreement, two variables are free but only two masks are optimal; in the zero six-variable graph, all 64 assignments are optimal. Two free binary variables make four combinations, so "free" is not a count of choices: the family is the forward-closed sets of the residual SCC graph, and a combination only counts if it closes — two of the four don't, which is why the tied agreement has two optimal masks instead of four. In the zero graph every cost is zero, so every assignment ties at energy 0 and the family is everything: 64 combinations, 64 optimal masks. Same word, opposite ends — in one fixture the labels are free and coupled, in the other free and unconstrained — and the family, not the label count, is the answer.

The certificate is what makes that family checkable cut by cut. Each cut exposes capacity minus flow as outgoing residual slack, so a feasible flow and an equal-capacity cut certify the minimum, and the 160 every-cut slack audits check that on every cut rather than trusting the one the solver returns. The 250 independent dense-energy cases check the answer against original-energy enumeration, and every shortest augmenting path, bottleneck and complete edge-flow state stays visible — including the six-augmentation control, where a reverse variable arc has to cancel earlier flow, so the flow is built by changing its mind rather than by monotone progress.

The checks behind those numbers are 30 Python tests, including all 21 original fixed-cost tests, and six Node/actual-Chrome tests; the 24 Python/BigInt reports agree on 24,624 exact scalar leaves, and Chrome checks the rendered cut, flow, reduction, residual and local-search records, SVG labels and colors, export, invalid/stale clearing and 320/390/1280 containment. A family claim that came out of sampling could hide a missing mask; here every cut's slack is an exact rational, so the two masks in the tied agreement are a computed fact, not a fitted one.

Distinct from 'A local minimum is not a global optimum', CutLab's first principle in approach.md, which is about a search stopping early on one fixed landscape — here no search is involved, the family is enumerated exactly, and the question is what "free" means inside it — and from 'A label that leaves can come back', CutLab's second principle, which is about the optimum family being nonmonotone in a moving parameter — here the parameter is fixed and the graph is fixed, and the question is the family structure on that one graph, not how it moves — and from the 'CutLab response-path extension' subsection above, which covers the moving-parameter side, open-cell masks 7, 0 and 1 with breakpoints 1/4 and 2 — here the fixed-cost side, two fixtures and the counting underneath them — and from the entry itself, which states the two fixtures as results; the subsection is the counting underneath them: two free variables make four combinations, the closed-set condition leaves two, and the zero graph leaves all 64.

So the entry stays as written. It states the reduction, the witnesses, the family and the checks; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason two free labels give two masks instead of four, and the exact place the family structure shows up.

### UnifyLab: minimal is not smallest

[Case study](unifylab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/unifylab)

The entry states the distinction in one clause: the subset stage distinguishes an inclusion-minimal conflict from a smallest conflict, and the default control has independent duplicate/cycle conflicts with repairs of different sizes. The two ideas sound alike and they are not the same thing. A conflict is inclusion-minimal when no proper subset of it is still a conflict — take any equation out and the rest unifies. A smallest conflict is the one with the fewest equations. Every smallest conflict is minimal, but a minimal conflict need not be smallest: the default control has two independent conflicts, one from a duplicate and one from a cycle, and their repairs are different sizes, so both are minimal and they are not the same size. "Minimal" is a property of the set; "smallest" is a comparison across sets, and the control shows the two coming apart on the same system.

The repair is what makes the claim checkable rather than asserted. A confirmed deletion repair lists the indices to REMOVE, and every single restoration fails: put any one removed equation back and the conflict returns, which is exactly the inclusion-minimal test applied to the repair rather than the conflict. A repair that survives every single restoration is minimal; a repair that survives them all and is also the shortest is smallest, and the two are only the same when no shorter repair exists. The enumeration is what settles that: eight synthetic controls cover 371 subsets, including all 256 masks at the eight-equation bound — 2^8, every combination of the eight equations, none skipped — so the claim that no shorter repair exists at the bound is a census, not a sample, and a resource limit stays unresolved with no inferred status rather than being read as a pass.

The checks behind those numbers are the two engines agreeing with each other and an oracle agreeing with neither: independent Python and JavaScript engines match 108 full audits and 1,032 subset summaries, bringing the suite to nineteen native tests on Python 3.10-3.13 and six Node/Chrome tests, with a third lazy-binding oracle checking all proper subsets and independent minimal hitting-set enumeration, and installed wheels reproducing seventeen base reports and eight subset audits through both entry points. The subset stage keeps duplicate equation identities — two copies of the same equation are two different indices, so a conflict that needs the duplicate is a different conflict from one that doesn't — and any selected subsystem opens its detailed rewrite trace, so a repair can be followed back to the equations it removes.

Distinct from 'A binding is not a solution', UnifyLab's principle in approach.md, which is about a solving-time assignment that has not been checked against the original system — here nothing is being solved, the system is already known to fail, and the question is which equations to blame, not whether a candidate solves it — and from 'A minimal repair is not a cheapest repair', Diagnoselab's principle, which is about a removal set being minimal versus cheapest — here nothing is costed at all, and the distinction is minimal versus smallest, size against size, not size against price — and from the entry itself, which states the distinction as a result; the subsection is the counting underneath it: 2^8 masks enumerated, 371 subsets covered, and two different-sized minimal conflicts on the same control.

So the entry stays as written. It states the distinction, the repairs and the checks; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason minimal and smallest come apart, and the exact place the enumeration shows it.

### UnifyLab: the earlier bindings have to move too

[Case study](unifylab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/unifylab)

The entry states the point in one clause: a normalized simultaneous substitution is applied to every original equation. The clause is easy to read past, and the case study is where it gets its weight. Composition matters because a variable bound inside another term can later get a value of its own — and when that happens, every earlier binding that mentions the variable has to change too. A solver that records the new value and leaves the old bindings alone has not applied a substitution; it has filed two assignments that disagree with each other. The native engine composes eagerly, rewriting earlier bindings as new values arrive, so the substitution it ends with is one assignment applied everywhere at once. The separate test oracle does the opposite on purpose: lazy bindings with symmetric dereferencing, so the two engines can disagree about the mechanism and still have to agree about the result.

Reversing an alias is not automatically a cycle, and the case study keeps that distinction visible rather than folding it into the occurs check. Each step retains the pending equations, the earlier bindings and the change made — deleting, orienting, decomposing or eliminating — so a rewrite can be followed back to the equation it came from. Constructor clashes and occurs-check failures keep their witnesses, and the successful result is the substitution applied to every original equation, not to a rewritten copy of the system. That last part is the check the entry is really making: the answer has to solve the equations as given, not the equations as simplified along the way.

The counting underneath it is the two engines agreeing and a checker agreeing with neither. Independent Python and JavaScript engines reproduce 167 complete reports and 270 rewrites; seventeen original controls retain 38 rewrites — eleven successes, four non-unifiable tasks and two actual resource-limit cases — and the lazy oracle is compared on 600 seeded systems and all 64 ordered pairs from a small term pool. A separate native certificate checker reconstructs the local rewrites, the resource limits and the terminal claims without calling the solver, so the certificate is a replay, not a second run of the same code. The bounds are stated with the results: six variables, eight equations, 96 term nodes, depth six and arity two, with a separate 4,096-node budget on each composed state and on the final applied-original result, and at most 256 rewrites retained. A run that hits a limit keeps only the completed prefix and claims neither success nor non-unifiability — a stopped run is not a failed system, and the certificate never says otherwise.

Distinct from 'A binding is not a solution', UnifyLab's principle in approach.md, which is about a solving-time assignment that has not been checked against the original system — here the assignment has been checked, and the question is whether the bindings were composed into one substitution before the checking started: an uncomposed binding can pass every local rewrite and still disagree with itself — and from the first UnifyLab subsection, 'minimal is not smallest', which is about a system already known to fail and which equations to blame — here the system may succeed, and the question is not which equations fail but whether the answer applies to all of them at once — and from the entry itself, which states the substitution as a result; the subsection is the mechanism underneath it: the eager composition, the lazy oracle that disagrees about the mechanism, and the certificate that replays the rewrites instead of re-running the solver.

So the entry stays as written. It states the rewrites, the composed substitution and the checks; the composition is the part underneath them — the reason a binding that was never composed is not a solution yet, and the exact places the two engines and the checker show it.

### STRIPSLab: the relaxed graph cannot see a deletion

[Case study](stripslab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/stripslab)

The entry states the gap in one clause: the real successor deletes then adds, the relaxed successor only adds — and the case study's controls are the places that gap shows up as a number. The cleanest is the restoration control: the real optimum is three steps, the relaxed optimum is two. The relaxed graph never pays to restore anything, because a delete is invisible to it: an action that adds a fact and deletes another looks, in the relaxed graph, like it only adds. So a plan that needs a fact restored after it was deleted costs two relaxed steps and three real ones, and the difference is exactly the deletion the relaxation throws away. The same blindness is what makes the consumable-resource control fail outright rather than merely overcount: a resource that permits either goal but not both is spent once, and the relaxed graph, which never deletes the resource, finds a plan for both — so the relaxed graph finds a plan where the real graph has none. One control is off by one step; the other invents a plan that does not exist. Both errors come from the same omission.

The shared-effect control shows the other direction of the same arithmetic. One real action achieves two goals at once, so the real cost of both goals together is one step — but h-add sums a cost per goal, and the shared achiever's cost is counted once for each goal it serves, so h-add counts it twice. The entry states that as a clause; the counting underneath is that h-add is a sum over goals, not a count over actions, and any action that serves two goals is charged twice by construction. That is why the search inspector's controlled task returns cost 3 under h-add while the exact cost is 2: the overestimate is not noise, it is the double-count, and under the disclosed tie and goal-pop policy the search commits to the 3-step plan. Zero, h-max and exact h-plus all return 2 on the same task — h-max takes the largest per-goal cost instead of the sum, so the shared achiever is charged once, and h-plus is computed from the full relaxed state graph rather than from either propagation estimate, so it does not inherit the double-count at all.

The checks behind those numbers are the two engines and the oracles agreeing on the whole state space rather than on a sampled path: the export retains every one of the 2^m states — at most 64, for six facts — including unreachable ones, with every blocked and applicable action, missing precondition, actual added and removed fact, real and relaxed successor, exact remaining goal distance, all shortest suffix choices and one representative replay, and initial optimal-plan edges retain every shortest tie. That is what makes the six shortest sequences for independent action orders a census rather than a sample: every order is retained, so the count is the whole family. The availability stage is the same idea applied to actions rather than states: eighteen controls retain 466 scenarios, 21,731 state rows and 408 optimal edges, with Python BFS and independent JavaScript distance sweeps agreeing on 118 complete reports covering 2,515 scenarios and independent native set-transition and all-proper-subset oracles covering another 80 seeded tasks. The original base release had eight Python tests and three Node/Chrome tests; the current suite has seventeen Python tests and nine Node/Chrome tests across all three modes, with an isolated installed wheel reproducing 14 base, 15 search and 18 availability reports outside the checkout, checking five installed module origins and running both CLI entry points in all three modes, and the 47 isolated installed reports preserve all earlier modes, with Chrome checking every retained record and scenario at 320/390/1280.

Distinct from 'Used does not mean necessary', STRIPSLab's principle in approach.md, which is about necessity across plans — an action appearing in one representative plan says nothing about whether every plan needs it, and the check disables every static action subset — here nothing is disabled and no plan is being judged necessary; the question is what the relaxed graph charges for a plan it can see, and the error is a miscount inside one graph, not a claim about all plans — and from the entry itself, which states the controls as results; the subsection is the arithmetic underneath them: a deletion the relaxed graph cannot see costs one step in the restoration control and a whole plan in the consumable control, and a shared achiever counted once per goal is what turns an exact 2 into an h-add 3.

So the entry stays as written. It states the state space, the failures, the search control and the availability counterfactuals; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason the relaxed graph's 2 is not the real graph's 3, and the exact place the deletion disappears.

### DFALab: passing the suite is not knowing the language

[Case study](dfalab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/dfalab)

The entry states the gap in one sentence: a closed and consistent observation table can still propose the wrong language, and what settles the question is the distinguishing experiment. The learning stage is where that gap is counted rather than asserted. The native suite enumerates every three-state binary teacher — 17,496 of them, with every start and accepting set — plus all 648 three-state unary teachers and all 128 two-state binary teachers, 18,272 teachers in total, and the learner still has to ask its way out: globally cached membership queries, closure and consistency repairs, every prefix of each exact counterexample, and an equivalence query that lays out the complete reachable target/hypothesis product with all outputs and all edges, whose first mismatch is a shortest shortlex counterexample. No mismatch proves all-word language equivalence for the supplied finite teacher — but that proof is about the teacher in hand, and the final DFA's minimality proof is narrower than it looks: a shortest access word and a shortest distinguishing suffix for every state pair proves the DFA has the fewest reachable states, not that the learner asked the fewest questions. Agreement on every experiment run so far is not agreement on the language.

The W-method stage puts a number on the bound the suite depends on. The inspector separates four claims — finite suite agreement, the supplied candidate's reachable-state bound, a bounded equivalence certificate, and a separate exact graph audit — and the control that matters is the faulty extra-state candidate: it passes while its bound is understated, and increasing the bound exposes the fault. The verdict turns on the bound, not on how many tests ran. Six added native tests run 52,488 exhaustive reference/candidate cases, 131 full native/browser-engine reports are cross-checked, and actual Chrome audits 3,444 test selections and 141 probes across eleven controls and three widths. At that stage the suite stood at fourteen native tests and seven Node/Chrome checks, with installed two-family reproduction — and the point of the enumeration is that the bound, not the test count, is what the certificate rests on.

The fault-atlas stage asks the same question about the suite itself: which tests can go? Every complete labeled implementation in a tiny unary/binary state domain is enumerated, equivalent survivors are separated from real faults, and every subset of the constructed W-suite is checked under test-count then letter-count cost, retaining all minima, escaping faults and chosen-test deletion witnesses. Irredundancy is not confused with minimum cardinality — a suite can have no single removable test and still not be smallest. Tests cover all 60 tiny minimal references with a separate equivalence oracle, eleven complete cross-engine atlas reports match, and actual Chrome audits 6,996 candidate selections and 804 subset selections across eight controls and three widths. The current suite — eighteen native checks on four Python versions, ten Node/Chrome checks, installed three-family reproduction — preserves the learning and conformance stages underneath, and the original four Node/Chrome learning checks still compare 189 complete reports and replay 93 table selections, 45 conjectures and 411 new-query rows at 320/390/1280, with nine installed controls reproduced outside the checkout.

Distinct from 'A closed and consistent observation table can still propose the wrong language', DFALab's principle in approach.md, which states the gap and names the remedy — here nothing new is claimed about the remedy; the question is what the counting shows about it: the exhaustive small-teacher census, the bound that flips a verdict, and the subset minima that separate irredundant from smallest — and from the entry itself, which states the stages as results; the subsection is the arithmetic underneath them: 18,272 teachers enumerated, one state of bound between pass and fail, and a minimum that is smaller than merely irredundant.

So the entry stays as written. It states the learner, the equivalence product, the minimality proof and the two later stages; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason passing every test run so far does not settle the language, and the exact places the numbers draw the line: the census, the bound, and the subset minimum.

### SynthLab: four examples can be right and still leave three rivals

[Case study](synthlab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/synthlab) · [Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/synthlab/)

The entry states the gap in one sentence — fitting the examples is not identifying the function — and the two-bit XOR control is where that gap gets counted. There are 132 distinct rival functions and 65,536 input subsets; CEGIS stops correctly with four examples, and three rivals still fit them. The learner is not wrong: the cheapest expression fitting those four examples is correct on every input it has seen, and the verifier has nothing left to object to. It is just not identified. The teaching atlas is the other side of the same count: it checks every input subset and finds 182 four-row teaching sets that do identify the target. Same number of examples, different identifying power — because a teaching set is chosen to eliminate every rival, not merely to be satisfied by the target. Five teaching controls add seven native tests and three Node/Chrome checks, bringing the project to 15 native tests and seven Node/Chrome checks, and actual Chrome audits 15 teaching reports, 1,203 subset selections and 597 rival truth selections.

The adaptive stage asks the next question the same way, one answer at a time. A full seeded two-bit grammar leaves ten functions after two known observations, and two further adaptive queries suffice to identify whichever one it is — while every fixed passive separating set needs three rows. The policy is not guessing better splits: it solves the smallest worst-case number of further queries, audits every nonempty version space, every query's label partition, all tied minimax rows and every possible target path, and across all balanced tie resolutions the policies need two or three further queries. Immediate split balance is not the same objective, and the comparison uses neither an easier hand-selected class nor an unlucky chosen baseline tie. Seven additional native tests bring the project to 22 on Python 3.10-3.13 and five additional Node/Chrome checks bring it to 12; the adaptive tests compare 541 complete native/JavaScript reports, and actual Chrome audits 15 adaptive reports, 1,583 version-space selections, 111 query partitions, 306 answer branches, 84 target traces and 105 passive mask selections.

Underneath both stages is the same finite frontier, and that is what keeps the claims small enough to check. Pure unsigned 1..3-bit expressions built from 0/1/x/y and selected add/and/neg/not/xor operators through AST node cost 5, with canonical representatives by minimum node cost then ASCII prefix-expression order; full-domain semantic signatures deduplicate programs without using the target, and a separate verifier enumerates every raw ordered grammar tree to check bounded completeness and canonical minimum cost. The base suite — eight native tests including 512 Boolean target/grammar pairs and 2,764 raw trees at each of three widths, four Node/Chrome tests auditing 30 reports, 90 iterations, 903 candidate signatures and 3,420 expression probes — separates example agreement, exact finite correctness and minimum AST-node cost inside that explicit grammar, with ten synthetic controls. The teaching-set idea is credited to Goldman and Kearns, the adaptive policy to classical decision-tree dynamic programming and generalized binary search; no novel algorithm, unbounded synthesis, cvc5 integration, expected-cost or PAC claim is made.

Distinct from 'Fitting the examples is not identifying the function', SynthLab's principle in approach.md, which states the gap and names the remedy — here nothing new is claimed about the remedy; the question is what the counting shows about it: four examples that leave three rivals, 182 four-row sets that leave none, and two adaptive queries where any fixed set needs three — and from the entry itself, which states the stages as results; the subsection is the arithmetic underneath them: the rivals, the teaching sets, and the queries.

So the entry stays as written. It states the learner, the verifier, the teaching atlas and the adaptive policies; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason four correct examples are not an identification, and the exact places the numbers draw the line: the rivals, the teaching sets, and the next question.
