### RewriteLab: fitting is not reaching

[Case study](rewritelab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/rewritelab) · [Live inspector](https://midya8.github.io/rewritelab/)

The entry states the gap in one sentence — a cheap equivalent expression can fit the grammar yet stay unreachable — and the base release is where that gap first gets counted. Finite ordered binary trees over `0,1,x,y,+,*` bounded to 3, 5 or 7 nodes give 36, 548 or 10,788 grammar terms, and `x*(1+1)` has two reachable five-node-budget terms costing 5 while `x+x` costs 3. Seven nodes unlock a three-rewrite distributive bridge to the cheaper term, so the cheaper term is not missing from the grammar — it is missing from the reachable set at five nodes. The other control makes the same point from the far side: `(1+1)*(1+1)` with multiplication cost 8 has one reachable term costing 14 at seven nodes against an addition-only semantic optimum of 7, out of a complete semantic census of six terms. The evidence is not sampled: separate rule-template and grammar enumeration checks every proof edge, outgoing closure and shortest-distance inequality, and exact evaluation on a 5-by-5 grid proves polynomial identity for this degree-bounded grammar. The base release is 25 exact source files with 11 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and three Node/actual-Chrome tests, 72 complete cross-language reports with 111,316 proof rows and 111,342 semantic rows, and live Chrome checking 26 reports, all 3,612 proof and 3,630 semantic selections at 320/390/1280, with all five deployed assets matching main.

The budget-barrier atlas turns the gap into two numbers per pair: the first budget that fits both source and target, and the first budget that admits a rewrite proof. For `x*(1+1) -> x+x`, first fitting is 5, first reachable is 7, extra budget 2 and shortest distance 3 — and thirteen of its 213 cap-7 semantic candidates need a larger bridge still. Five of six weighted constant-product candidates remain unreached through seven nodes, and null means not reached through the cap, not impossible at larger budgets. The small controls keep the labels honest: leaf `x` first has allowed budget 3, but its zero-step path has actual peak 1, so a shortest proof's peak need not equal its allowed budget, and source-too-large and target-too-large are never mislabeled as blocked. The stage is 35 source-exact files with 20 Python tests on 3.10-3.13 and six Node/actual-Chrome tests, 36 complete Python/JS atlases comparing 13,016 candidate rows and 13,745 nested proof rows, and Chrome checking 25 atlases and every 3,844 candidate selection, with nine deployed assets matching main and 57 screenshots matching without masks.

The cost atlas holds the graph fixed and varies the prices: all 512 positive integral (+, multiplication, leaf) weight triples in 1..8. For constant four, the product vector (2, 1, 4) ties the addition-only vector (3, 0, 4) exactly when addition and multiplication costs agree — 224 positive-gap triples, 288 zero-gap, 64 ties, each semantic group winning at 288 triples because ties credit both, so the win counts are not a probability partition. The five-node bridge source is the control that keeps this honest: it is strictly dominated by blocked `x+x` and has a gap at all 512 triples, and no crossover is invented for a dominated pair. The stage has 27 Python tests across 3.10-3.13 and nine Node/actual-Chrome tests, 17 complete Python/JS weight reports comparing 8,704 outcomes and 93 groups, and Chrome checking 25 weight reports, all 4,096 outcome selections and 34,570 expression selections, with 13 live assets matching main and 83 screenshots matching without masks.

A finite grid cannot settle whether an expression ever wins elsewhere, so the continuous-region extension computes complete optimum regions over every strictly positive cost ratio — the parameter triangle from normalizing the three costs to sum one — with exact halfplanes, reduced rational vertices and separate semantic and reachable regions. Seventeen Python/JavaScript region reports agree on 93 groups and 215 vertices, and the independent checks cover all 36 three-node sources, 500 random vector sets and 64 outside-grid positive points. Closed geometry is not a positive-cost verdict: a boundary-only winner is not obtainable at strictly positive costs, while an interior tie point or segment can be a real winner despite zero area, and area is not a probability. The stage has thirty-five Python tests on each of 3.10-3.13, alongside eight Node engine and four actual-Chrome tests, with Chrome checking 25 region reports, all 4,096 point selections and 34,570 expression selections, 16 deployed assets matching main and 111 screenshots matching exactly without masks.

Distinct from 'A budget can fit both ends of a rewrite and still admit no proof between them', RewriteLab's principle in approach.md, which states the gap and names the remedy — here nothing new is claimed about the remedy; the question is what the counting shows about it: a first fitting of 5 against a first reachable of 7, 224 positive-gap triples against 64 ties, and a parameter triangle where a boundary winner is not a winner at all — and from the entry itself, which states the stages as results; the subsection is the arithmetic underneath them: the budgets, the triples, and the regions.

So the entry stays as written. It states the grammar, the bridge, the barrier atlas, the cost atlas and the continuous regions; the counting is the part underneath them — the reason a cheaper term can fit and still not be reachable, and the exact places the numbers draw the line: the budgets, the triples, and the regions.

Case study: [rewritelab.md](rewritelab.md)

### SparseLab: a supplied branch is a check, not a path

[Case study](sparselab.md) · [Repository](https://github.com/midya8/sparselab)

The branch verifier takes one weak sign pattern the caller supplies and returns that pattern's maximal closed nonnegative-lambda interval — nothing more. The case study says it in "Choose a check": verify the interval for one supplied affine sign branch, and one supplied branch does not discover a complete path. A failed screening test does not establish relevance, either. The report keeps every half-line and tie, all affine coefficients and correlations, and exact endpoint and interior dual vectors with primal equal to dual, and the whole interval claim comes from affine inequalities, not endpoint sampling alone. That is the difference between checking a branch and finding a path: the check is exact about the pattern it was handed, and silent about every pattern it was not.

The checks around the branch are what make the single-branch answer worth anything, and the rewrite names them. Partial residuals are recomputed on every sweep, so no incremental-residual drift accumulates. Descending regularization paths retain all fit histories and the actual warm starts. The exact oracle counts skipped singular patterns and returns valid nonsingular-support optima, not every point in a continuum. And the case study states its own limit plainly: the branch API shares a linear-solve helper with the per-lambda oracle, so equation reconstruction and analytic fixtures provide separate checks — it is not an independent linear-algebra comparison. The verifier is honest about the one branch it checked and about the machinery it checked it with; what it cannot do is tell you the branches nobody supplied.

Distinct from 'A unique prediction does not identify a coefficient', SparseLab's first principle in approach.md, which is about one prediction being compatible with many coefficients — here the fixture's Gram is positive-definite, so the coefficient path is unique, and the question is not which fit to believe but what one supplied branch can verify — and from 'A sufficient certificate is not a complete detector', SparseLab's second principle, which is about a one-sided certificate staying silent on features it cannot clear — here there is no certificate at all, only a check of the one pattern supplied, and the silence covers the rest of the path, not just the features the screen could not clear — and from the first SparseLab subsection, 'a feature can leave and come back with the opposite sign', which is about what the feature does along the path — here the question is what the verifier can say about any of it, not what the path does.

So the entry stays as written. It states the five branches, the knots, the endpoint and the weak-sign rules; the verifier's limits are the part underneath them — the reason one checked branch is a check of one branch, and the rest of the path is still unexamined.

Case study: [sparselab.md](sparselab.md)

### RAGLine: passing the check is not proving the answer

[Case study](ragline.md) · [Live engine inspector](https://midya8.github.io/ragline/)

The rewrite says it plainly, in the case study's own words: copying a sentence makes the overlap easy to pass. The answer is a sentence copied from the cited chunk, and the check scores how many answer content tokens occur in that chunk — so a copied answer starts at full overlap, and correct citations pass at 100% overlap on the fixture. The negative control is real: corrupting the citation to an unrelated chunk makes the check flag the answer. But the fixture numbers underneath it are a development evaluation over 8 questions with known topics, not a held-out benchmark — recall@3 1.00, MRR 0.94, grounded-answer rate 1.00, pinned by CI gates `--min-recall 1.0 --min-mrr 0.9 --min-grounded 1.0`. Those numbers say the gate works as built. They do not say the answers are right.

The embedding carries the same kind of limit, also stated plainly in the rewrite: it is lexical, not semantic. Shared hash bins can produce similarity without shared words, and paraphrase retrieval quality is not evaluated. The check flags the tested wrong-citation controls, but it does not prove entailment, factuality or correct interpretation. Answers are extractive — a copied sentence, never generated text — because the no-dependency rule excludes a language model, and both backends run offline in pure Python, without a language model or external API. A green gate means the check ran and the citation held. It is not a second opinion on whether the sentence is true.

Distinct from 'Grounding is a check you run, not a property you claim', RAGLine's first principle in approach.md, which is about making grounding a runnable gate — here the gate is unchanged, and the question is what passing it does and does not establish: the numbers come from a development fixture, not a held-out benchmark, and the check is lexical, so a pass is not entailment — and from 'An unvisited zero is still a score', RAGLine's second principle, which is about what the sparse arithmetic returns — here nothing is being ranked, and the question is what the grounding check can say about the answer it already has — and from the first RAGLine subsection, 'a zero can win', which is about an implicit zero outranking a negative match in the postings backend — here the backend is not in question, and the point is the check that runs after retrieval, not the retrieval itself.

So the entry stays as written. It states the cited extractive answers, the lexical-overlap grounding checks and the CI gates; the rewrite's limits are the part underneath them — the reason a green gate means the check ran, not that the answer is proved.
